# Unit u15 briefs: Part XVI sector deep dives, Chapters 69 to 76

Brief writer: u15 (senior curriculum architect). Date: October 3, 2026; revised October 3, 2026 (blueprint review round 1; revision log at the end of this file). Governing files: `standards.md`, `bible/architecture.md` (titles and Part XVI Owns notes), `bible/decisions.md` (D-008 LaTeX and label scheme; D-011 indicative ranges; D-013 arithmetic from inputs; D-014 to D-017 and D-114 running-case calibrations), `bible/ownership-resolutions.md` (rulings, which take precedence over this brief), `bible/style-sheet.md` with its Addendum of October 3, 2026, `bible/case-bible.md` with `bible/case-bible-annex-p.md` and `bible/case-bible-annex-tr.md` (the annexes take precedence), the figure ledgers `model/figure-ledger-case-p.md` and `model/figure-ledger-case-r.md` (authoritative IDs and values), `bible/anchor-registry.md`, `bible/glossary-canon.md`, and `facts/`.

## Rules that apply to every chapter in this unit

1. **Sector chapters apply; they do not re-teach.** Every concept below that is owned by an earlier chapter is cross-referenced with `\cref` to the section label in the anchor registry; where this brief gives only a chapter, the writer looks up the section in the registry and reports any topic the registry does not show. Each Part XVI chapter owns only sector-specific mechanics, structures and terms that no earlier chapter teaches (ruling R-101; Owns column of `architecture.md`). Terms whose glossary home is in another chapter are used without bold and cross-referenced to that home; each chapter's glossary table below lists only terms the canon homes in that chapter.
2. **Fixed sector skeleton, specific headings.** Each chapter follows the order the architecture sets for Part XVI: industry economics; how the asset works (cross-reference Chapters 11 or 12 and add only what a financier needs that those chapters do not give); revenue models; risks; the contract set; financing terms (dated); modeling specifics (cross-reference Chapter 45); landmark deals and failures; then the required elements (walkthrough, running-case installment, practitioner's notebook, judgment drill, close, exercises, solutions, sources). Each section heading names the sector's own content; no two sections in the unit share a title apart from the fixed element headings (Practitioner's notebook, Judgment drill, Exercises, Solutions to exercises). Headings take the serial comma, carry no colon except after "Case P", "Case R" or "Walkthrough", and use sentence case (style sheet 3.1 and 4.1). The locked tables of contents below are the corrected headings; where they differ from the anchor registry, the brief wins and the central editor updates the registry (see the revision log).
3. **Financing terms and D-011.** A financing term is printed in one of three forms only. (a) A verified figure from a fact sheet, with the deal or survey, market and date, attributed in text, for example "(Norton Rose Fulbright 2026)". (b) An indicative range under D-011, printed with the word "indicative", the market and the period, and the drivers that move it; each such range is listed in the chapter's status note so the domain-expert reviewer can sign it off. (c) A running-case input or ledger figure, labeled `\casep` or `\caser`, never presented as market evidence. Every brief below says which form each number takes. The "Do not state" lists in every fact sheet cited bind. In particular, no writer prints "Saudi solar tenors of 25 to 27 years", "European CfD wind at 1.15x to 1.20x" or "PPP gearing of 85% to 90%" in any form, indicative or not. Two items that `t-market-norms` listed as unverified are now verified by `t-market-norms-2` and may be printed only in this attributed form: REIPPPP bid windows 1 to 4, where most awarded projects indicated a 70% to 80% debt split (Eberhard and Naude 2016), and Baltic Power's 25-year contract for difference (Northland Power, Q2 2026 report).
4. **Illustrative numbers and settings.** Every worked example below is Illustrative unless labeled otherwise, and every example that models a project, financing or contract names a country or market, a year and fictional parties; the writer states in the example that the inputs are the example's and not market or official data for that country. The unit spreads its examples across the Americas, Europe, the Middle East and Africa, and Asia-Pacific. No example reuses a style-sheet sample figure (such as 412.6) as an input. All results were computed in Python by the brief writer (revised round 1) and are stated to the precision the writer should print. The writer recomputes every figure before printing; the numbers auditor checks against these values. Running-case numbers are never computed here; they appear as "from Case Bible ledger: <figure ID>" and are printed only as the ledger gives them, except simple arithmetic from inputs or ledger values that D-013 allows, which the brief marks "D-013".
5. **Running-case installments in Part XVI** are mostly short (several are marked "(small)" in Case Bible Part 6, as amended by Annex P 2.2 and Annex TR R.9). They show the running case meeting the sector, never re-run a beat owned by an earlier chapter, and keep story dates exactly as the Case Bible gives them (ruling R-105).
6. **Exercises.** Each chapter carries 12 to 14 exercises across the three tiers, each with a full solution; Tier 2 and Tier 3 solutions end with the most common wrong answer and why it is wrong (style sheet 4.7). Every Tier 2 and Tier 3 calculation or model task below states its inputs and its expected answer in this brief; the writer copies the inputs into the exercise text.
7. **Status labels and LaTeX.** Every example, exhibit and clause carries one status macro (`\illustrative`, `\casep`, `\caser`, `\realcase{Name, years}`). Diagrams use only the `pfbook.sty` TikZ styles; at most 15 nodes. Labels follow D-008 (`ch:N`, `sec:N.M`, `ssec:N.M.K`, `ex:N.K`, `exh:N.K`, `cl:N.K`, `exr:N.K`, `eq:N.K`, `fw:slug`). Excel formulas in this brief contain no `%`; any formula that needs one goes in an `excel` block, never in `\xl{}`.
8. **Target lengths** are floors for the core teaching of each sector, not caps; the fixed apparatus (walkthrough, installment, notebook, drill, exercises and solutions) is in addition to the sector teaching and is never compressed to fit.

# Chapter 69. Thermal power

## 69.0 Rulings applied

- R-001: Chapter 69 cites sec:11.8 for levelized cost and never re-derives LCOE; the screening curve (eq:69.1) is a cost-at-capacity-factor tool, not an LCOE derivation.
- R-024 and R-116: spark, clean spark and dark spreads are homed in Chapter 11 (ssec:11.10.5, eq:11.7). Chapter 69 applies them; eq:69.2 is captioned "Merchant gross margin from the spark spread over dispatched hours, citing eq:11.7"; "spark spread", "clean spark spread" and "dark spread" leave the Chapter 69 glossary (sequencing defect 2).
- R-049: the tariff formulas are Chapter 18's eq:18.1 and eq:18.2 with the symbols $\mathrm{HR}^{\mathrm{c}}$ and $k_{\mathrm{HHV/LHV}}$ (style sheet A.5); Chapter 69 uses them without restating them.
- R-054: power tolling is owned by Chapter 18 (sec:18.7); Chapter 69 compares tolling with other revenue models by system role.
- R-067: Chapter 69 is the glossary home of IWPP (ssec:69.3.2).
- R-074 (CR-u15-5): Chapter 29 (sec:29.3) owns the Arrangement as revised and the pre-2023 project-finance terms; Chapter 69 says only that Case P's 2018 cover followed the terms then in force, citing sec:29.3, and may cite `t-oecd-pf-2018` for the existence of the pre-2023 project-finance annex.
- R-121: heat-rate headroom is homed in Chapter 18 (ssec:18.3.2); Chapter 69 applies it and drops it from its glossary. EOH and major inspections are Chapter 11 and Chapter 24 terms (R-027).
- R-101, R-105, R-113: Part XVI ownership; Case Bible Part 6 row as amended by Annex P 2.2 (figures add P-F57); architecture title.

## 69.1 Header

- Chapter: 69
- Title: Thermal power
- File: `chapters/69-thermal-power.tex`
- Label: `ch:69`
- Target length: 9,000 words (sector chapter; range 8,000 to 10,000)
- Dominant expert lens: the senior project finance banker who has sat on credit committees for contracted emerging-market IPPs and merchant US gas plants, paired with the independent engineer for fuel, heat rate and dispatch. The government lens (single-buyer planner) is the second voice, because the thermal IPP is where host governments have been hurt most often.

## 69.2 Purpose and capability statement

Purpose. Thermal plants are the asset class project finance learned on, and the one that has failed most instructively: Dabhol, Paiton and Mundra all failed with the plant running perfectly. This chapter teaches the reader to look at a gas, coal or oil-fired plant the way a credit committee does in 2026: first by asking what role the plant plays in its system (baseload, mid-merit, peaker, firming capacity for renewables), because the role decides how it earns money; then by following the fuel from wellhead or mine to burner tip and asking who bears price, volume and deliverability at each link; then by matching revenue model, contract set and debt terms to that role. It closes with the transition question every thermal deal now faces: how a lender gets repaid by a plant whose dispatch, fuel and permits may shrink before the loan matures.

What you will be able to do (printed under `\section*{What you will be able to do}`):
- Screen a thermal power project by its system role and fuel chain, and name the two or three risks that decide bankability (Capability 2, Capability 13).
- Test a thermal plant's contract set for gaps between dispatch, fuel take-or-pay and tariff pass-through (Capability 3).
- Size the debt capacity of a contracted versus a merchant gas plant and explain the difference to a credit committee (Capability 5, Capability 14).
- Judge how energy-transition policy, fuel-supply risk and ECA rules limit tenor and structure for a new thermal plant (Capability 15).

## 69.3 Concepts owned and concepts assumed

### Owned (taught in full here)

1. Thermal power industry economics for financiers: the cost structure of OCGT, CCGT, coal and oil-fired engines (capital-heavy versus fuel-heavy); the screening curve as the tool that links capacity factor to least-cost technology; where each technology sits in the merit order and why that sets its revenue model.
2. The system roles of a thermal plant (baseload, mid-merit, peaker, firming capacity behind renewables) and how each role maps to a revenue model and a debt structure.
3. Thermal revenue models compared as a set: single-buyer capacity-plus-energy PPA; tolling; independent water and power project (IWPP); merchant energy-only with scarcity pricing; capacity-market or resource-adequacy revenue; hybrid contracted-merchant. (The contract mechanics are owned by Chapters 18 and 20; this chapter owns the comparison by system role and the credit consequences.)
4. Merchant thermal margins: spark, clean spark and dark spreads (defined in ssec:11.10.5, eq:11.7) applied over dispatched hours by price block, and the concentration of merchant gross margin in scarcity hours.
5. Heat-rate headroom outcomes under a fuel pass-through (the term is defined in ssec:18.3.2): who keeps the gain or loss when actual heat rate differs from contracted heat rate over the maintenance cycle.
6. Dispatch and fuel take-or-pay mismatch as the signature thermal contract gap, and the pass-through rules that close it.
7. Starts-based maintenance cost: why a cycling plant costs more per MWh to maintain than a baseload plant (applied from Chapter 24's EOH concept).
8. Fuel-chain lock-in: the boiler or turbine designed for one fuel and one supply route, and why lenders treat it as a single point of failure (Mundra).
9. Energy-transition risk for thermal plants as a financing constraint: declining dispatch, carbon cost, permit and ECA restrictions (the OECD Arrangement's coal prohibition and the 12-year cap for non-CCSU power plants), the lender universe set by public exclusion policies (World Bank Group upstream exit announced December 12, 2017 and applied after 2019; EIB Energy Lending Policy of November 14, 2019 with its 250 gCO2/kWh standard; OECD agreement of October 22, 2021 to end support for new unabated coal plants; from `t-thermal-exclusions`), and stranding before maturity.
10. Financing terms for thermal power by market segment: verified contracted-thermal anchors in emerging and Gulf markets (`t-market-norms-2`), verified US merchant-gas pricing (`t-market-norms`), and indicative ranges under D-011 only where no anchor exists.
11. Thermal-specific modeling: dispatch modeling, part-load heat rate, degradation and recovery around overhauls, starts and EOH-based LTSA costs, fuel cost and pass-through checks (cross-referencing Chapter 45 for the general sector modeling method and Chapter 41 for the revenue and cost build).
12. Framework 69.1, the fuel-chain trace (original).
13. Landmark thermal deals and failures, from the thermal angle: Hub Power, Dabhol, Paiton I, Tata and Adani Mundra, the Gulf IWPP programs, Azura-Edo, and the thermal side of Winter Storm Uri.

## 69.4 Locked table of contents

Opening (no heading). See 69.5.

`\section*{What you will be able to do}` (four items, 69.2).

**69.1 Where thermal plants make money** (`sec:69.1`)
- 69.1.1 Capital-heavy and fuel-heavy plants (`ssec:69.1.1`). The cost stack of each technology (capex per kW, fixed O&M, heat rate, fuel), and why OCGTs and engines are cheap to build and expensive to run while coal is the reverse. Exhibit 69.1 (technology inputs table). Verified US new-build context from `t-power-tech-norms` (Lazard LCOE+ 2026): CCGT capex of about USD 1,450 to 2,100/kW in the base range, with quotes of USD 2,400 to 2,600/kW in the high case, which the writer reads as a sign of turbine supply scarcity; `t-construction-norms` adds GE Vernova's 116 GW gas-equipment backlog plus slot reservations at mid-2026 against annual output of about 20 GW, and an Israeli CCGT that ordered equipment in February 2026 and expects completion during 2030.
- 69.1.2 The screening curve (`ssec:69.1.2`). Annualized fixed cost per kW-year plus variable cost per MWh times hours run; crossover capacity factors; Example 69.1; Exhibit 69.2 (pgfplots screening curve chart with four lines). Equation 69.1 (cost per MWh at capacity factor; a screening tool, with LCOE cited from sec:11.8 rather than re-derived).
- 69.1.3 System roles and what they pay (`ssec:69.1.3`). Baseload, mid-merit, peaker, firming capacity; how solar and wind growth pushes gas plants down the screening curve toward lower capacity factors and why capacity-based revenue becomes more important as energy revenue shrinks. Verified dispatch evidence from `t-power-tech-norms`: US combined-cycle plants ran at a 58.4% capacity factor in 2025 (EIA, subject to revision) against Lazard's 30% to 90% new-build range. Gulf IWPP repositioning (Taweelah C as firming capacity, from `gulf-iwpp`).

**69.2 Heat rate, starts, and fuel flexibility as credit variables** (`sec:69.2`)
- 69.2.1 Heat rate, degradation, and recovery (`ssec:69.2.1`). Cross-reference ssec:11.2.1 and ssec:11.2.3 for the physics; teaches only the financial consequence: recoverable degradation between overhauls, non-recoverable degradation over life, and the saw-tooth heat-rate profile that drives fuel margin. Uses Case P inputs for illustration of shape only (Case Bible 1.2: 0.12% a year non-recoverable plus 0.8% average recoverable). Verified context from `t-power-tech-norms`: the US gas fleet averaged about 7,754 Btu/kWh in 2024 against Lazard's new-build CCGT assumption of about 6,475 to 6,550 Btu/kWh (Lazard LCOE+ 2026); the writer states that neither source gives the heating-value basis, so the figures are not converted to the book's kJ/kWh LHV convention.
- 69.2.2 Starts, hours, and maintenance intervals (`ssec:69.2.2`). EOH from Chapter 24 applied to a cycling plant; Example 69.2. Verified LTSA fee shape from `t-om-ltsa-norms`: a 25-year GE contractual service agreement for an Ohio CCGT (Guernsey, 2019) paid USD 25,000 a month plus USD 500 per gas-turbine fired hour, both escalating 2.5% a year. Verified fleet-defect case from `oem-fleet-defects`: GE's third-quarter 2018 charge of USD 0.2 billion for an oxidation issue in Stage 1 blades of its HA and 9FB turbines and its fourth-quarter USD 0.4 billion LTSA assumption charge show that a long-term service agreement is the OEM's fixed-price bet on fleet failure rates (no affected plants or owners named; Do not state).
- 69.2.3 Fuel flexibility and fuel-chain lock-in (`ssec:69.2.3`). Dual-fuel turbines, coal specification ranges, the boiler designed for one coal (Mundra, ADB paragraph cited in `mundra`), LNG-to-power chains.

**69.3 Revenue models for thermal plants** (`sec:69.3`)
- 69.3.1 The single-buyer capacity-plus-energy PPA (`ssec:69.3.1`). Cross-reference Chapter 18 for mechanics. Credit reading: capacity payment as the debt-service line; energy payment as fuel pass-through; the buyer's ability to pay as the residual risk (pointer to Chapter 59).
- 69.3.2 Tolling and the IWPP (`ssec:69.3.2`). The offtaker supplies fuel; the project sells conversion. The Gulf IWPP model: single state buyer, state fuel, 60/40 government-private equity in Abu Dhabi (from `gulf-iwpp`), long PWPAs.
- 69.3.3 Merchant energy and scarcity pricing (`ssec:69.3.3`). Spark, clean spark and dark spreads applied from ssec:11.10.5 (not re-defined); Equation 69.2 (merchant gross margin over dispatched hours, citing eq:11.7); Example 69.3 (merchant CCGT gross margin by price block, showing 60.7% of margin from 120 scarcity hours). Verified market rule from `t-electricity-market-design`: after Winter Storm Uri the PUCT cut ERCOT's high system-wide offer cap from USD 9,000/MWh to USD 5,000/MWh, effective January 1, 2022, which caps the scarcity block in Example 69.3's market.
- 69.3.4 Capacity markets and hybrid revenue (`ssec:69.3.4`). Capacity or resource-adequacy payments as the floor under merchant energy; contracted-merchant hybrids; cross-reference Chapter 20 and ssec:11.10.4. Verified price paths from `t-capacity-ancillary-markets` and `t-electricity-market-design`: PJM's RTO capacity price rose from USD 28.92/MW-day (2024/25) to USD 269.92/MW-day (2025/26) and then cleared at the negotiated cap for 2026/27 (USD 329.17), 2027/28 (USD 333.44) and 2028/29 (USD 325.00); the GB T-4 auction cleared at GBP 60.00/kW-year for 2028/29 and at GBP 27.10/kW-year for 2029/30 (March 2026). Lesson: capacity revenue is policy-set and volatile, so lenders size on the contracted term of a capacity agreement, not on the latest clearing price (GB agreement lengths are not verified; Do not state).
- Exhibit 69.3: thermal revenue models compared (rows: model; who takes fuel price; who takes dispatch; who takes availability; typical debt-service line; credit question).

**69.4 Fuel, dispatch, and transition risk in thermal deals** (`sec:69.4`)
- 69.4.1 Fuel supply, price, and deliverability (`ssec:69.4.1`). Take-or-pay in the GSA against dispatch in the PPA; Example 69.4. Supplier performance when the market moves against it (Mundra). Winter Storm Uri: gas units lost fuel and froze (58% of affected units gas-fired; freezing and fuel 75% of events, `winter-storm-uri`), so "firm" thermal capacity was not firm.
- 69.4.2 Heat rate and performance (`ssec:69.4.2`). Example 69.5 (heat-rate headroom).
- 69.4.3 Dispatch and affordability (`ssec:69.4.3`). A plant contracted for baseload but dispatched to zero (Paiton 1999); a plant too large and inflexible for its system (Dabhol). The buyer's affordability as the true capacity risk.
- 69.4.4 Transition, permits, and stranding (`ssec:69.4.4`). Carbon cost in the spark spread; the OECD Arrangement's ban on official export credits for new coal plants without CCUS and the 12-year cap for non-CCSU power plants (from `t-oecd-arrangement`); the lender universe as a financing constraint, taught from `t-thermal-exclusions`: the World Bank Group's December 12, 2017 announcement that it would no longer finance upstream oil and gas after 2019 (midstream, downstream and gas-fired power stayed eligible), the EIB's November 14, 2019 Energy Lending Policy ending new unabated fossil-fuel energy lending from the end of 2021 and replacing its 550 gCO2/kWh Emissions Performance Standard with 250 gCO2/kWh, and the OECD participants' October 22, 2021 agreement to end support for new unabated coal plants. Panel B of Exhibit 69.5 (in sec:69.6) maps which lender types (DFIs, OECD ECAs, international banks, domestic banks, non-OECD ECAs) can lend to a coal plant, an unabated CCGT, a hydrogen-ready CCGT and a CHP plant tested against the EIB 250 g standard; the text explains what a smaller, more domestic lender pool does to margin, tenor, currency and refinancing risk. No commercial bank's policy, no NZBA status and no ADB, AIIB, AfDB or IFC policy detail is stated (Do not state). Declining capacity factors over the debt life; the mitigation set (shorter tenor, capacity-based revenue, cash sweeps, hydrogen-ready or CCS-ready design with a one-sentence forward reference to Chapter 83).
- 69.4.5 Water, emissions, and site (`ssec:69.4.5`). Once-through seawater cooling (Case P inputs), water rights inland, air permits; cross-reference Chapter 50.

**69.5 How PPA, fuel, and maintenance contracts interlock in a gas IPP** (`sec:69.5`)
- 69.5.1 The contract map of a gas IPP (`ssec:69.5.1`). Exhibit 69.4 (TikZ contract map of a gas IPP: project company, offtaker, host government, gas seller, transporter, EPC contractor, LTSA provider, O&M operator, lenders, ECA; at most 12 nodes). Cross-references to Chapters 17, 18, 22, 24, 25, 27, 28.
- 69.5.2 Where thermal contracts leave gaps (`ssec:69.5.2`). The four classic gaps: dispatch versus take-or-pay; contracted versus guaranteed heat rate; fuel force majeure versus PPA force majeure (deemed availability when the fuel supplier fails); change in law in the fuel supplier's country (Mundra). Applies Chapter 28's gap scan, not re-teaching it.
- 69.5.3 Carbon-cost pass-through in a tolling agreement (`ssec:69.5.3`). Clause 69.1 variants a (sponsor-friendly), b (lender-friendly) and c (offtaker-friendly), set in a tolling agreement for a 2027 CCGT in the GB market (Illustrative, fictional parties), with annotations and the landing paragraph (indicative, D-011, labeled with market and period).

**69.6 Financing terms for thermal plants** (`sec:69.6`)
- 69.6.1 Contracted IPPs in emerging and Gulf markets (`ssec:69.6.1`). Verified anchors from `t-market-norms-2`, each attributed and dated, read as single-deal evidence and not as market averages: Syrdarya CCGT (Uzbekistan; ACWA Power; 25-year PPA with gas under a tolling mechanism; IFC-disclosed cost of approximately USD 950 million with up to USD 760 million of long-term debt, about 80%; signed March 16, 2023; capacity stated by IFC as 1,600 MW and by ACWA as 1,500 MW, so the writer gives both); Shuweihat S3 (UAE, 1,600 MW CCGT, approximately USD 1.4 billion financed 80% debt and 20% equity, per KEPCO's FY2025 Form 20-F); the four SPPC CCGT IPPs in Saudi Arabia (25-year PPAs signed November 2023, financial close by July 11, 2024, combined value approximately USD 7.338 billion); Qatar's Facility E (JBIC up to approximately USD 990 million within approximately USD 2,971 million of co-financing, January 15, 2026, 25-year offtake with Kahramaa; JBIC's figures are not the whole debt package); Jordan IPP 7 (IFC A loan of up to USD 200 million proposed September 17, 2026 on approximately USD 900 million cost, 25-year PPA with NEPCO); ACT in Mexico (an 18-year tranche at SOFR plus credit adjustment plus 3.75%, 75% hedged, distributions at a DSCR of at least 1.20x, reported for December 31, 2023). Gulf margins, DSCRs, DSRA sizes and "typical" tenors are not verified and are not printed; any range for them is indicative under D-011 with drivers (buyer credit, sovereign support, DFI and ECA participation, PPA term). Case P's own terms are printed as a running-case reference (`\casep`), not as market data.
- 69.6.2 Merchant and capacity-backed thermal in liberalized markets (`ssec:69.6.2`). Verified: US merchant gas priced at SOFR plus 350 to 500 bps in early 2024, with private credit needing at least 400 bps (Norton Rose Fulbright 2024 panel, `t-market-norms`); contrast with contracted renewables at SOFR plus 125 to 200 bps.
- 69.6.3 ECA rules and tenor (`ssec:69.6.3`). Non-CCSU power plants capped at 12 years under the Arrangement as revised; coal prohibition; why gas plants now get shorter ECA terms than renewables (22 years under CCSU for listed sectors). One sentence on Case P: its 2018 ECA cover followed the terms then in force (cross-reference sec:29.3; `t-oecd-pf-2018` records that until the 2023 modernization project finance had its own annex in the Arrangement); no Case P tenor is re-derived here.
- Exhibit 69.5: financing terms and lender universe for thermal plants. Panel A, terms by segment (columns: segment; deal or survey and date; DSCR basis; tenor; gearing; margin; source type), each row marked verified (with the fact-sheet attribution) or indicative. Panel B, lender types available by technology (from `t-thermal-exclusions`; verified policies only, every other cell marked "policy not verified").

**69.7 Modeling a thermal plant** (`sec:69.7`)
- 69.7.1 Dispatch and part load (`ssec:69.7.1`). Dispatch factor scenarios (Case P: base 76.5%, banking 72.0%, downside 58.0%), part-load penalty, starts; cross-reference Chapter 45.
- 69.7.2 Fuel and pass-through checks (`ssec:69.7.2`). The model check that a fuel price shock leaves CFADS unchanged when the pass-through works (Case P sensitivity "gas price +30%, pass-through check", P-F16).
- 69.7.3 Maintenance cycle and reserves (`ssec:69.7.3`). Availability saw-tooth over the 8-year cycle (Case P inputs), MMRA (Chapter 37), LTSA fixed and variable fees (Chapter 24).
- 69.7.4 Merchant margin modeling (`ssec:69.7.4`). Hourly or block price duration curves, scarcity-hour sensitivity, carbon price.

**69.8 Thermal IPPs that failed with the plant running** (`sec:69.8`). Real cases from the thermal angle only; each one paragraph to three paragraphs, cross-referencing the home chapter for the rest.
- 69.8.1 Dabhol and the plant too big for its buyer (`ssec:69.8.1`). `dabhol`: 2,184 MW combined phases, LNG-fired, USD tariff, MSEB unable to pay by late 2000, Phase I shut June 2001; thermal lesson on sizing and inflexible base-load gas in a coal system (Ch 59 and 60 own the payment and political story).
- 69.8.2 Paiton I and zero dispatch (`ssec:69.8.2`). `paiton`: 1,230 MW coal, PPA 1994, PLN dispatched to zero in 1999, PPA extended from 2029 to 2040 in the 2002 amendment; thermal lesson: capacity payment is only as good as the buyer; tenor extension as the standard tool to cut annual tariff while preserving lenders' NPV.
- 69.8.3 Mundra and the fuel chain that broke (`ssec:69.8.3`). `mundra`: CGPL 4,000 MW imported coal; 55% of fuel unescalated; Indonesian benchmark pricing 2010 to 2011; Energy Watchdog v CERC (April 11, 2017) refused relief; ADB records no default because Tata Power funded the shortfall. Lesson: the fuel chain is a single point of failure, and an upstream equity stake hedges the group, not the project.
- 69.8.4 Hub Power, Azura-Edo, and the Gulf programs (`ssec:69.8.4`). Short contrasts: Hub Power's World Bank PRG and long-term credit fund extending tenor (`hub-power`); Azura-Edo's four-layer security stack for an OCGT (`azura-edo`; detail in Chapters 34 and 59); the Gulf single-buyer programs as the template for low-tariff, high-leverage thermal procurement (`gulf-iwpp`; Oman gas intensity falling from 374 to 200 Sm³/MWh).

**69.9 Walkthrough: reading a thermal plant's dispatch and fuel model** (`sec:69.9`). See 69.7.

**69.10 Case P: why Kessara chose a CCGT** (`sec:69.10`). See 69.10 below.

**69.11 Practitioner's notebook** (`sec:69.11`). Subsections per style sheet: Checklist; Red flags; Rules of thumb and their limits; Common mistakes; Questions experts ask.

**69.12 Judgment drill** (`sec:69.12`). The situation; Reasoning it through; What would change the answer.

**69.13 A thermal plant is repaid by its buyer and its fuel chain** (`sec:69.13`). Close; open problem for Chapter 70.

**69.14 Exercises** (`sec:69.14`). **69.15 Solutions to exercises** (`sec:69.15`). `\section*{Sources}`.

## 69.5 Opening

A Case P scene is not used here; the opening is the Paiton I moment from `paiton`. In 1999 both 615 MW units of Paiton I reached commercial operation in East Java into a market whose currency and demand had collapsed, and PLN, the state utility that had signed for their capacity, dispatched them to zero and then went to court in Jakarta to annul the contract. Two paragraphs set the paradox: a technically excellent plant, contracted on capacity, earning nothing because the buyer could not pay and did not need the power. The third paragraph turns it into the chapter's question: what decides whether a thermal plant repays its debt, if not the plant? (No tariff figure, no "first IPP" claim without the ECA release's qualifier, no corruption finding; see `paiton` Do not state.)

## 69.6 Worked examples

**Example 69.1. Screening four technologies for Bangladesh's single-buyer system in 2015** (`ex:69.1`, Illustrative; Section 69.1.2). Setting: in 2015 a fictional consultancy, Meridian Power Advisory, screens new-build options for a coastal single-buyer system in Bangladesh for a fictional development lender; the writer states that the inputs are the example's, not the country's official planning data. Technologies compared at 2015 price levels, 10.0% real discount rate. Inputs:

| Technology | Capex (USD/kW) | Fixed O&M (USD/kW-year) | Heat rate (kJ/kWh LHV) | Fuel | Variable O&M (USD/MWh) | Life (years) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| OCGT | 720 | 14.6 | 10,450 | Gas USD 6.40/MMBtu GCV | 4.1 | 25 |
| CCGT | 1,085 | 23.8 | 6,350 | Gas USD 6.40/MMBtu GCV | 3.6 | 25 |
| Coal (imported) | 2,140 | 52.3 | 9,300 | Coal USD 86.5/t at 25.1 GJ/t NCV | 4.7 | 30 |
| HFO engines | 980 | 31.2 | 8,450 | HFO USD 412/t at 40.2 GJ/t NCV | 9.8 | 20 |

Gas fuel cost per MWh uses the Case P convention: HR × 1.108 × price / 1,055.056. Solid and liquid fuel: HR / (NCV × 1,000) × price per metric ton. Annualized fixed cost = capex × CRF(10%, life) + fixed O&M; CRF(10%, 25) = 0.11017; CRF(10%, 30) = 0.10608; CRF(10%, 20) = 0.11746.

Results: annualized fixed cost (USD/kW-year) OCGT 93.92, CCGT 143.33, coal 279.31, HFO 146.31. Fuel cost (USD/MWh) OCGT 70.24, CCGT 42.68, coal 32.05, HFO 86.60; total variable cost OCGT 74.34, CCGT 46.28, coal 36.75, HFO 96.40. Cost per MWh at capacity factors 10%, 30%, 50%, 80%: OCGT 181.55, 110.07, 95.78, 87.74; CCGT 209.90, 100.82, 79.00, 66.73; coal 355.60, 143.03, 100.52, 76.61; HFO 263.42, 152.08, 129.81, 117.28. OCGT is cheapest below 1,761 hours a year (capacity factor 20.1%); the CCGT is cheapest above that; coal never beats the CCGT at these prices (crossover would need 14,269 hours); HFO engines are dominated. Sensitivity: at gas USD 9.80/MMBtu, CCGT variable cost is 68.95 USD/MWh and coal beats the CCGT above 4,223 hours (capacity factor 48.2%). Lesson: the technology choice is a bet on the fuel price and the capacity factor; the screening curve shows where the bet flips. Equation 69.1: $c(\mathrm{CF}) = \frac{F \times 1{,}000}{8{,}760 \times \mathrm{CF}} + v$, where $F$ is annualized fixed cost per kW-year and $v$ variable cost per MWh; Excel: `=F_annual*1000/(8760*CF)+v`.

**Example 69.2. What starts cost a cycling CCGT in England** (`ex:69.2`, Illustrative; Section 69.2.2). Setting: Kingsferry Power Ltd (fictional) runs one F-class gas turbine in a CCGT on the Humber estuary, England, under an LTSA with a fictional OEM, comparing its 2018 baseload year with its 2025 cycling year; the OEM prices the LTSA in USD (Illustrative). The turbine is under an LTSA with a variable fee of USD 518 per EOH and 10 EOH per start (Chapter 24 convention). Baseload year: 7,900 operating hours, 25 starts, EOH 8,150, LTSA variable cost USD 4.222 million, output 3,175,800 MWh at an average 402.0 MW when running, USD 1.33/MWh; starts are 3.1% of EOH. Cycling year: 4,100 hours, 260 starts, EOH 6,700, cost USD 3.471 million, output 1,521,100 MWh at 371.0 MW average, USD 2.28/MWh; starts are 38.8% of EOH. Lesson: as renewables push a CCGT into cycling duty, maintenance cost per MWh rises by about 71% while total revenue falls; models that hold LTSA cost per MWh constant overstate CFADS.

**Example 69.3. Merchant gross margin of a Texas CCGT in an energy-only market** (`ex:69.3`, Illustrative; Section 69.3.3). Setting: Brazos Bend Power LLC (fictional), a 620 MW CCGT selling into ERCOT's energy-only market in 2023, considers merchant term debt from a fictional bank club; the price blocks are the example's, not ERCOT data, and the scarcity block sits below the USD 5,000/MWh offer cap. Plant inputs: heat rate 6,450 kJ/kWh LHV; gas USD 3.45/MMBtu GCV; variable O&M USD 3.20/MWh; availability 92.5%; fixed O&M USD 26.40/kW-year; capex USD 1,085/kW. Price duration simplified to four blocks: 120 hours at USD 845.00/MWh; 1,460 hours at 52.80; 3,880 hours at 33.10; 3,300 hours at 19.60 (8,760 hours). SRMC = 6,450 × 1.108 × 3.45 / 1,055.056 + 3.20 = USD 26.57/MWh. The plant runs in the top three blocks: generation 3,131.3 GWh (capacity factor 57.7%); gross margin by block USD 56.32 million, 21.96 million, 14.53 million, nil; total USD 92.82 million (USD 149.71/kW-year); fixed O&M USD 16.37 million; margin after fixed costs USD 76.45 million. Scarcity hours (1.4% of the year) produce 60.7% of gross margin. If the 120 scarcity hours cleared at USD 52.80 instead (a price cap or a capacity mechanism that suppresses scarcity), gross margin falls to USD 38.30 million (USD 61.78/kW-year) and margin after fixed costs to USD 21.93 million. Debt capacity: on a 12-year annuity at 7.25% sized at 2.00x (an illustrative merchant ratio; the chapter states that US banks quoted merchant gas pricing but no merchant gas DSCR in the fact sheets), debt is USD 299.6 million with scarcity (44.5% of the USD 672.7 million capex) and USD 86.0 million without (12.8%). Equation 69.2 (captioned "Merchant gross margin from the spark spread over dispatched hours, citing eq:11.7"): $\mathrm{GM} = \sum_b h_b \times C \times A \times \max(0,\, p_b - \mathrm{SRMC})$, where the per-MWh spread $p_b - \mathrm{SRMC}$ is the spark spread of eq:11.7 less variable O&M, $h_b$ the hours in block $b$, $C$ capacity and $A$ availability; the clean version subtracts carbon cost $e \times p^{\mathrm{CO_2}}$ inside SRMC, with $e$ = 0.3618 tCO₂/MWh for this plant (6.45 GJ/MWh × 56.1 kg/GJ, rounded to four decimals; the exercise answers use 0.3618). The writer cites eq:11.7 and does not re-define the spreads. The carbon case is left to Exercise 69.6.

**Example 69.4. Take-or-pay exposure at an Ivorian CCGT when dispatch falls** (`ex:69.4`, Illustrative; Section 69.4.1). Setting: Lagune Énergie SA (fictional), a 400 MW CCGT near Abidjan, Côte d'Ivoire, in 2023, buys domestic gas from a fictional supplier, Golfe Gaz SA, under a GSA whose terms are the example's. The plant, heat rate 6,480 kJ/kWh LHV, burns 65,330 MMBtu a day at full load (400 × 24 × 1,000 × 6,480 × 1.108 / 1,055,056). GSA: DCQ 58,300 MMBtu a day (89.2% of full-load need), ACQ = DCQ × 365 = 21,279,500 MMBtu, take-or-pay 85% of ACQ = 18,087,575 MMBtu, gas USD 6.95/MMBtu. At 72% energy dispatch (no part-load penalty assumed) the plant burns 17,168,638 MMBtu: shortfall 918,937 MMBtu, take-or-pay payment USD 6.39 million. At 48% dispatch with a 4.6% part-load penalty it burns 11,972,264 MMBtu: shortfall 6,115,311 MMBtu, payment USD 42.50 million. The writer then shows the PPA question: if the offtaker caused the low dispatch, does the PPA pass the take-or-pay payment through (Case P does: "dispatch-caused GSA take-or-pay amounts" are a pass-through, Case Bible 1.4)? If not, the USD 42.50 million falls on CFADS. Make-up gas (recoverable later) is mentioned as partial relief with its own risk.

**Example 69.5. Heat-rate headroom at a Pakistani CCGT** (`ex:69.5`, Illustrative; Section 69.4.2). Setting: Indus Delta Power Ltd (fictional), a 450 MW CCGT in Sindh, Pakistan, in 2024, sells to the national single buyer under a capacity-plus-energy PPA (terms are the example's). The plant sells 2,890 GWh a year; the PPA pays fuel at a contracted heat rate of 6,520 kJ/kWh LHV; gas costs USD 7.85/MMBtu GCV; HHV/LHV 1.108. Fuel paid by the offtaker: 2,890,000 MWh × 1,000 kWh/MWh × 6,520 × 1.108 / 1,055,056 = 19.7884 million MMBtu. In a year when actual heat rate is 6,410 kJ/kWh (new and clean), the plant burns 19.4545 million MMBtu (fuel bill USD 152.72 million) and keeps a gain of USD 2.62 million. In the year before a major inspection, at 6,575 kJ/kWh, it burns 19.9553 million MMBtu (USD 156.65 million) and loses USD 1.31 million. Lesson: heat-rate headroom is a profit line for equity and a volatility line for CFADS; lenders size on the downside heat rate and the offtaker's auditor tests the contracted value.

## 69.7 Walkthrough

**Walkthrough: reading a thermal plant's dispatch and fuel model** (`sec:69.9`). The artifact is an Illustrative two-page extract from the lenders' model of Example 69.5's plant, Indus Delta Power's 450 MW CCGT in Pakistan under a single-buyer PPA, as reviewed by a fictional lenders' technical adviser in 2024 (Exhibit 69.6, a table extract of the Operations sheet rows for three semiannual periods: availability, dispatch factor, net generation, part-load penalty, contracted heat rate, actual heat rate, gas burned, gas paid by offtaker, take-or-pay quantity, shortfall, LTSA EOH and fee). Steps:
1. Find the dispatch assumption and its source (system planner's merit-order study or the offtaker's dispatch history); check it against the screening curve role from 69.1.
2. Trace net generation from availability and dispatch; confirm the availability saw-tooth matches the maintenance cycle.
3. Check that fuel paid by the offtaker uses the contracted heat rate at the dispatched load, and fuel burned uses the IE's actual heat-rate curve; compute the headroom line (Example 69.5 logic).
4. Compare annual gas burn with the GSA take-or-pay quantity in the base, banking and downside dispatch cases; find the first year with a shortfall; confirm whether the PPA passes it through.
5. Check that LTSA cost moves with EOH, including starts, not with MWh.
6. Run the fuel price sensitivity and confirm CFADS is unchanged (pass-through check); if it moves, find the leak (a fuel cost not passed through, a lag in indexation, a heat-rate cap).
7. Write the two-sentence finding a credit paper would carry.
Exhibit 69.6 is Illustrative; it does not use Case P numbers.

## 69.8 Frameworks

**Framework 69.1: Fuel-chain trace** (`fw:fuel-chain-trace`; original). Purpose: find the link in a thermal plant's fuel chain where price, volume, quality or deliverability risk is not matched by a contract. Steps: (1) Draw the chain from source to burner tip (field or mine; processing; pipeline, rail, ship or LNG terminal; plant storage). (2) For each link, name the contract and counterparty. (3) For each link, record who bears price change, volume shortfall, quality deviation and physical interruption. (4) Compare each with the PPA: does the PPA pass it through, deem availability, or leave it with the project company? (5) Test each counterparty's incentive to perform when the market moves against it (Mundra) and the law that governs its obligation. (6) Mark every unmatched cell; size it with the cash-flow impact of a 12-month interruption or a 30% price move; decide on mitigation (stock, dual-fuel, alternative supply, sponsor support, reserve). Demonstrated on Example 69.4 and in the judgment drill. One sentence on when to use it: any thermal deal at screening and again at the GSA term sheet.

Established tool used (credited as standard power-system planning practice, not a named author): the screening curve (Section 69.1.2). Chapter 15's bankability ladder and Chapter 28's gap scan are applied, not re-taught.

## 69.9 Real cases

| Slug | Use in this chapter | Transferable lesson |
|---|---|---|
| `paiton` | Opening; Section 69.8.2 | A capacity payment pays only while the buyer can pay; zero dispatch of a contracted base-load plant is the warning; extending the PPA tenor (2029 to 2040) cut the annual tariff while preserving the lenders' NPV. |
| `dabhol` | Section 69.8.1 | Size and flexibility must fit the buyer's system and budget; an inflexible base-load gas plant with take-or-pay LNG in a coal-dominated system destroys value even when it runs. |
| `mundra` | Sections 69.2.3, 69.4.1, 69.8.3 | A fuel contract below market is a hedge only while enforceable under the supplier's law; plants designed for one imported coal are locked into one chain; courts treat fuel price rises as commercial hardship, not force majeure. |
| `gulf-iwpp` | Sections 69.1.3, 69.3.2, 69.8.4 | Standardized single-buyer procurement with state fuel makes thermal plants financeable at low tariffs; the same programs now reposition gas plants as firming capacity (Taweelah C, 2.6 GW, PPA to 2050, carbon-capture ready). |
| `hub-power` | Section 69.8.4 | Multilateral guarantees and a subordinated long-tenor fund extended tenor and lowered tariff; the later dispute cut equity return, not debt service. |
| `azura-edo` | Section 69.8.4 (one paragraph) | An OCGT in a weak-buyer market needs a layered security stack; convertibility remained the hardest residual risk (detail in Chapters 34 and 59). |
| `winter-storm-uri` | Section 69.4.1 | Thermal "firm" capacity failed on fuel and freezing; for merchant gas plants, scarcity pricing is the margin and the exposure at once. |
| `t-market-norms` | Section 69.6.2 | Merchant gas priced at SOFR plus 350 to 500 bps in early 2024; private credit at least 400 bps. |
| `t-oecd-arrangement` | Section 69.6.3 | Coal prohibition; 12-year cap for non-CCSU power plants. |
| `t-oecd-pf-2018` | Section 69.6.3 (one sentence) | Before 2023 project finance had its own Arrangement annex; Case P's 2018 cover followed the terms then in force (detail in sec:29.3). |
| `t-thermal-exclusions` | Section 69.4.4; Exhibit 69.5 Panel B | The lender universe, not only the cash flow, decides financeability: WBG upstream exit (announced 2017, applied after 2019), EIB 250 gCO2/kWh (2019), OECD coal agreement (2021). |
| `t-market-norms-2` | Section 69.6.1; Exhibit 69.5 Panel A | Contracted thermal in emerging and Gulf markets runs on 25-year PPAs with about 80% debt in the disclosed deals (Syrdarya, Shuweihat S3), anchored by DFIs and Asian ECAs; margins and DSCRs are rarely disclosed. |
| `t-power-tech-norms` | Sections 69.1.1, 69.1.3, 69.2.1 | Fleet averages and new-build assumptions differ; plant vintage sets merit-order position and running hours. |
| `t-construction-norms` | Section 69.1.1 | Gas turbine backlogs measured in years push equipment commitments before financial close. |
| `t-om-ltsa-norms` | Section 69.2.2 | LTSA fees have a fixed monthly part and a per-fired-hour part, so cost follows running regime. |
| `oem-fleet-defects` | Section 69.2.2 | A fleet defect (GE HA blades, 2018) lands first on the OEM's LTSA economics; owners recover only through their contract terms. |
| `t-electricity-market-design`, `t-capacity-ancillary-markets` | Sections 69.3.3, 69.3.4 | Offer caps bound scarcity revenue; capacity prices swing with market rules (PJM, GB T-4). |

All fact sheets this chapter needs are delivered; there are no open requests for Chapter 69.

## 69.10 Running-case installment

**Case P: why Kessara chose a CCGT** (`sec:69.10`). Case Bible Part 6, Chapter 69 row, as amended by Annex P 2.2: story date 2015 to 2016; characters Abdoulaye Ndao-Sylla (then Secretary-General of MEH) and Tomasz Wierzbicki; figures: inputs (Annex P 4.12), P-F57 and P-F16.

Scene outline (`casescene`, place and date: "Ministry of Energy and Hydrocarbons, Dabakro, September 2015"; the first sentence of the installment says this is before the February 2016 RFQ). A market-sounding session the Ministry holds with prospective bidders under the Emergency Power Plan. Abdoulaye wants megawatts before the 2019 elections and asks why not more HFO engines, which can be installed fastest; he translates megawatts into towns (verbal habit, used once). Tomasz answers in tariff terms ("What does that cost us per kilowatt-month?" used at most once) and argues that the Sombé West gas due in 2019 makes a CCGT the cheaper baseload plant; Abdoulaye pushes for the shortest possible construction period, the position the Case Bible says bidders later priced (he is wrong; the narration after the box shows why with MEH's own screening). Nobody wins the argument in the room.

Narration outside the box: Kessara's 2015 system from Case Bible 1.1 (2,310 MW installed; 1,780 MW available; peak demand 2,140 MW growing 7.4% a year; 640 MW hydro on the Moraba cascade, 820 MW aging HFO and diesel, 690 MW SEKA OCGTs, 160 MW other). Then Exhibit 69.7 (`\casep`), "MEH's 2015 technology screening": the Annex P 4.12 planning inputs (OCGT USD 650/kW, CCGT USD 1,050/kW, imported coal USD 2,100/kW, HFO engines USD 1,100/kW; gas USD 5.50/MMBtu GCV; coal USD 75/t CIF; HFO USD 300/t; 10.0% discount rate, 2015 USD) and, from Case Bible ledger: P-F57, the annualized fixed cost, fuel cost and cost per MWh at 30%, 50%, 70% and 90% capacity factor for each technology. The reading applies Example 69.1's method: the CCGT is cheapest from 30% capacity factor upward (P-F57: USD 92.6/MWh against the OCGT's USD 96.1/MWh at 30%), so the existing OCGTs keep the peaking role and the HFO fleet is the costly residue; the writer may state the OCGT-CCGT crossover as D-013 arithmetic on P-F57 values: (137.7 − 85.6) × 1,000 / ((59.5 + 4.0) − (36.7 + 3.5)) = 2,236 hours, a capacity factor of 25.5%. Then a flash-forward table (Exhibit 69.7, second panel) showing Bélanou as a thermal reference point: from Case Bible ledger: P-F16 (equity IRR 13.3%, project IRR post-tax 11.0%, equity NPV at 16.0% of USD −43.1 million, and the sensitivity rows "dispatch 50%" 1.34x / 1.53x / 13.1%, "gas price +30%" 1.35x / 1.54x / 13.3%, "availability −3 points" 1.32x / 1.53x / 13.2% and "heat rate +2%" 1.30x / 1.49x / 12.5%, each as minimum DSCR / average DSCR / equity IRR). The narration reads the two thermal-specific rows: the gas price row leaves both ratios unchanged because the fuel charge passes through, and the dispatch row barely moves debt service cover because the capacity charge carries it. No other number is computed.

Ledger figures displayed: P-F57 (all four technology rows) and P-F16 (the rows listed). Inputs cited: Case Bible 1.1 power sector table; Annex P 4.12; Case Bible 1.2 plant table (dispatch factors 76.5%, 72.0%, 58.0%); 1.4 PPA fuel charge description.

## 69.11 Practitioner's notebook (outline)

Checklist (itemize, each with its reason): system role and dispatch study from an independent source; screening-curve position under low and high fuel prices; fuel-chain trace completed (Framework 69.1); GSA take-or-pay against downside dispatch, year by year; pass-through of dispatch-caused take-or-pay confirmed in the PPA; contracted versus guaranteed heat rate and degradation allowance; part-load curve in the PPA; deemed availability for fuel-supplier failure; LTSA fee basis (EOH, starts) and its cap; water, emissions and permit renewals over the loan life; ECA eligibility under the Arrangement as revised; transition stress (dispatch decline and carbon cost) in the downside case.

Red flags: dispatch assumption taken from the sponsor's own model; a PPA sized for baseload in a system whose demand forecast depends on one industrial customer; unindexed fuel components (Mundra); a fuel supplier whose home law can override its contract; a GSA longer than the reserves certification supports; a tariff denominated in a currency the buyer does not earn (cross-reference Chapter 59); LTSA priced on hours while the plant will cycle; scarcity hours carrying most of a merchant plant's margin.

Rules of thumb and their limits: "capacity payment covers debt service" holds only while the buyer can pay; "fuel is a pass-through" holds only to the contracted heat rate and only for the fuel the PPA names; "CCGT beats coal" depends on gas price (Example 69.1 crossover at USD 9.80/MMBtu); "merchant gas debt is half of contracted" is a market observation from 2024 US pricing, not a rule.

Common mistakes: modeling LTSA cost per MWh; ignoring the part-load penalty at low dispatch; treating make-up gas as free; assuming deemed availability covers a supplier failure that is the supplier's force majeure; sizing merchant debt on average prices instead of price-duration blocks.

Questions experts ask: Who dispatches this plant, and what happens to our capacity payment if they stop? Where does the gas come from in year 15? What does the offtaker pay for if the gas supplier fails? What heat rate does the PPA pay for at 50% load? How many starts a year does the LTSA price assume? Which lenders in the group have thermal exclusion policies that limit their hold or tenor?

## 69.12 Judgment drill

The situation (second person, numbers, deadline). You are the credit officer for an arranging bank on a 520 MW CCGT IPP in a West African single-buyer market (Illustrative; fictional sponsor and buyer), in March 2026. The PPA runs 20 years; the sponsor asks for 18-year amortizing debt at 75% gearing. The domestic gas field's certified reserves cover 14 years of the GSA's DCQ at the plant's base dispatch; the GSA allows the seller to reduce DCQ pro rata if the field underperforms. An ECA covers the turbine package; under the Arrangement as revised, a non-CCSU power plant gets at most 12 years of ECA support. Credit committee meets Thursday.

Reasoning it through (prose outline): the fuel-chain trace shows the binding risk is fuel volume after year 14, not the plant or the buyer; reserves tail and debt tenor must be aligned (debt fully repaid with a buffer before the reserves run out, or a backstop fuel route contracted); the ECA tranche is capped at 12 years, so an 18-year profile needs DFI or commercial tenor behind it; deemed availability for fuel shortfall shifts the risk to the offtaker and its guarantor, which is acceptable only if the sovereign support covers it; the answer an expert gives: offer a debt profile maturing no later than year 13 to 14 of operations unless (a) the PPA deems availability for gas shortfall backed by the government guarantee, or (b) an LNG import or alternative supply route is contracted; size at the shorter tenor and show the sponsor the tariff consequence.

What would change the answer: a reserves recertification raising 2P to cover 20 years (tenor can lengthen); a sovereign guarantee covering deemed capacity payments for gas shortfall (risk moves to the state); dual-fuel capability with on-site storage and a liquid-fuel supply contract (partial mitigation, at a heat-rate and cost penalty); a buyer whose credit is weak (shorter tenor regardless).

## 69.13 Exercise set (13 exercises)

Tier 1, concept checks (4):
- 69.1 Explain why an OCGT can be the least-cost choice for a plant that runs 1,200 hours a year even though its fuel cost per MWh is the highest of the gas options.
- 69.2 Name the four system roles of a thermal plant and match each to the revenue model that usually pays it.
- 69.3 Explain why a capacity payment does not remove dispatch risk from lenders (use Paiton I).
- 69.4 State which party should bear fuel-price risk under a tolling agreement and why lenders still ask about fuel.

Tier 2, calculation and drafting (5):
- 69.5 Recompute Example 69.1's OCGT/CCGT crossover at a gas price of USD 8.10/MMBtu GCV (answer: CCGT variable cost USD 57.62/MWh; OCGT USD 92.99/MWh; crossover 1,397 hours, capacity factor 15.9%).
- 69.6 Add a carbon price of USD 60/t to Example 69.3 (emission factor 0.3618 tCO₂/MWh) and recompute SRMC, dispatch and gross margin (answer: clean SRMC USD 48.28/MWh; the plant runs only in the 845.00 and 52.80 blocks; generation 906.1 GWh; gross margin USD 54.83 million plus USD 3.79 million, total USD 58.62 million against USD 92.82 million without carbon).
- 69.7 For Example 69.4, find the dispatch level at which the take-or-pay payment is zero without part-load penalty (answer: 18,087,575 / (65,330 × 365) = 75.9%).
- 69.8 Draft a deemed-availability clause in a PPA for gas-supplier failure, with sponsor-friendly and offtaker-friendly variants, and annotate.
- 69.9 Compute the heat-rate headroom gain or loss in Example 69.5 at an actual heat rate of 6,488 kJ/kWh (answer: gain USD 0.76 million: 2,890,000 × 1,000 × 32 × 1.108 / 1,055,056 = 97,120 MMBtu at USD 7.85).

Tier 3, case problems and model tasks (4):
- 69.10 Using Case Bible ledger P-F16 and Case Bible 1.2 inputs, explain in a half-page credit note why Bélanou's equity IRR is more sensitive to availability than to gas price; no new computation of running-case figures.
- 69.11 Build a four-block merchant margin model (Example 69.3 inputs) in Excel with a data table on gas price (USD 2.95, 3.45 and 3.95/MMBtu) and scarcity-block price (USD 545, 695 and 845/MWh); state the scarcity price at which margin after fixed costs covers an annuity of USD 38.0 million at 2.00x (answer: margin after fixed costs must reach USD 76.0 million; the three lower blocks contribute USD 36.50 million and fixed O&M is USD 16.37 million, so the scarcity block must earn USD 55.87 million on 68,820 MWh, a price of USD 838.43/MWh, only USD 6.57/MWh below the base case: the debt case rests almost entirely on scarcity pricing).
- 69.12 Inputs: a 1997 Southeast Asian coal IPP (Illustrative, fictional Selat Power) has 16 years left on its PPA, a level capacity tariff, and lenders who value the remaining tariff stream at 11.25%. After a currency crisis the buyer asks for a 12% cut. Using level annuities at equal present value, (a) compute the cut that an 8-year extension delivers and (b) the extension needed for exactly 12% (Paiton logic); state the lenders' conditions (answer: (a) 1 − AF(11.25%, 16) / AF(11.25%, 24) = 11.30%; (b) about 8.9 years, so a 9-year extension; conditions: debt maturity stays inside the original term or is re-sculpted with lender consent, the extension is in a signed PPA amendment with the guarantee extended to match, and the buyer's arrears are settled first).
- 69.13 Apply Framework 69.1 to an imported-coal plant whose supply contract is governed by the exporting country's law; identify the unmatched cells and propose mitigations.

## 69.15 Glossary entries owned

| Term | Abbreviation | Definition | Home section |
|---|---|---|---|
| screening curve | – | A plot of each generating technology's total cost per kW-year (or per MWh) against hours run, used to find the least-cost technology at each capacity factor. | 69.1.2 |
| baseload, mid-merit and peaking plant | – | Plants classified by the share of hours they run: baseload most hours, mid-merit for daily and seasonal swings, peaking only in the highest-demand or scarcity hours. | 69.1.3 |
| firming capacity | – | Dispatchable capacity contracted or held to cover periods when variable renewable output is low. | 69.1.3 |
| independent water and power project | IWPP | A privately financed plant that sells both electricity and desalinated water to a state single buyer under a long-term purchase agreement. | 69.3.2 |
| fuel-chain lock-in | – | Dependence of a plant on one fuel type, quality and supply route because its boilers or turbines were designed for them. | 69.2.3 |

Removed (homed elsewhere; used without bold and cross-referenced): spark spread, clean spark spread and dark spread (ssec:11.10.5; R-024; sequencing defect 2); heat-rate headroom (ssec:18.3.2; R-121).

## 69.16 Neighbors

Chapter 68 (Capital rules for banks and insurers) explains how Basel slotting, the output floor and Solvency II infrastructure classes set the capital cost of holding project loans and bonds, and so which institutions lend at which tenor; it closes Part XV and gives this chapter the reason bank appetite differs by sector and contract quality. Chapter 70 (Onshore wind and solar) moves from fuel risk to resource and price risk: plants with no fuel bill whose revenue is set by weather, capture price, curtailment and policy support, financed on P50 and P90 cases in markets where their own output lowers the price they earn.

# Chapter 70. Onshore wind and solar

## 70.0 Rulings applied

- R-024: capture price, capture ratio and cannibalization are homed in sec:11.12, curtailment and negative prices in sec:11.11; basis and shape risk in ssec:20.1.1. Chapter 70 applies them.
- R-067: turbine supply agreement (TSA) and balance of plant (BOP) are homed in Chapter 23 (ssec:23.2.3); Chapter 70 (ssec:70.5.1) applies them and drops them from its glossary (CR-u15-3).
- R-073: Chapter 67 (sec:67.10) owns the 2025 deadlines, beginning-of-construction rules and prohibited-foreign-entity tests; Chapter 32 (sec:32.9) owns tax equity and credit transfer; Chapter 70 owns only the sector consequences and cites both.
- R-121 and R-114: full-service agreement is homed in Chapter 24 (ssec:24.3.1) and is never abbreviated (FSA is not used); Chapter 70 drops it from its glossary.
- R-001: LCOE cited from sec:11.8, not re-derived.
- R-101, R-105, R-113: Part XVI ownership; Case Bible Part 6 row; architecture title.

## 70.1 Header

- Chapter: 70
- Title: Onshore wind and solar
- File: `chapters/70-onshore-wind-and-solar.tex`
- Label: `ch:70`
- Target length: 11,000 to 13,000 words (raised in round 1; the target is a floor for the core teaching of each sector, not a cap)
- Dominant expert lens: the developer and sponsor-side investor who has built and refinanced wind and solar portfolios, paired with the market advisor (capture prices, curtailment, basis). The lender lens enters through sizing by revenue quality; the government lens through procurement programs (REIPPPP) and policy support (US tax credits, Spain).

## 70.2 Purpose and capability statement

Purpose. Onshore wind and utility-scale solar are now the largest single source of project finance deal flow, and the most standardized; the risk has moved from the technology, which works, to the price the energy earns where and when it is produced. This chapter teaches the reader to follow a megawatt-hour from the resource to the bank account (gross yield, losses, curtailment, the node, the hour, the capture price, the hedge or contract), to read the contract set that wraps that chain, to know the financing terms the market applied in 2023 to 2026 by revenue quality, and to recognize the failures (Chile's decoupled nodes, Lake Turkana's missing line, Uri's frozen turbines, Ivanpah's under-performing towers) as failures of a specific link in that chain.

What you will be able to do:
- Trace a wind or solar project's revenue from resource to cash and identify the link that decides bankability (Capability 2, Capability 13).
- Read a wind or solar contract set (turbine or module supply, balance of plant, full-service agreement, interconnection, offtake) and find its gaps (Capability 3).
- Explain and quantify how revenue quality moves debt capacity, from fully contracted to merchant, using dated market terms (Capability 5).
- Judge a low-bid renewable project in a congested or policy-dependent market and say what a lender should require (Capability 15).

## 70.3 Concepts owned and concepts assumed

### Owned

1. Industry economics of onshore wind and utility-scale solar PV for financiers: capex and opex structure, falling technology cost and its consequence (revenue risk replacing cost risk), economies of scale and portfolio financing.
2. The resource-to-revenue chain as one sequence (gross yield, losses, availability, curtailment, node, capture, contract), and Framework 70.1 that operationalizes it (original).
3. Solar-specific design economics a financier must read: inverter loading ratio (DC/AC ratio) and clipping; trackers versus fixed tilt stated as an energy and cost trade-off (no technology claims beyond Chapter 11).
4. Wind-specific design economics: turbine choice, certification and platform maturity (the 2023 Siemens Gamesa onshore quality charges from `oem-fleet-defects`), availability guarantees, icing and cold-weather packages (Uri lesson), noise and wildlife curtailment as revenue risk.
5. Revenue models for wind and solar compared by credit quality: state auction PPAs and CfDs (cross-reference Chapter 19), utility PPAs, corporate PPAs physical and virtual, hedges, merchant (cross-reference Chapter 20); program-level design (REIPPPP) as a bankability device.
6. Congestion and basis as the dominant risk in high-penetration markets: nodal decoupling between injection and withdrawal nodes (Chile), curtailment, interconnection queues and evacuation (Lake Turkana).
7. The contract set for onshore wind and solar as applied to the sector: turbine supply agreement and balance-of-plant contract versus full EPC (terms homed in ssec:23.2.3); module supply agreement and solar EPC; the full-service agreement for turbines (homed in ssec:24.3.1); interconnection agreement; land leases (cross-reference Chapters 22 to 25); and Clause 70.1 on turbine availability guarantees.
8. Policy-support dependence as a financing variable: the sector consequences of US federal tax credits after OBBBA (rules owned by sec:67.10; dated and flagged), Spain's retroactive cuts (pointer to Chapters 19, 54 and 60), and how lenders treat policy-dependent cash flow.
9. Financing terms for onshore wind and solar 2022 to 2026: the US DSCR and spread ladder by revenue quality, tax-credit bridge loans, portfolio and back-levered debt behind tax equity (mechanics in Chapter 32); verified non-US anchors (India, REIPPPP bid windows 1 to 4, DFI-led African and Central Asian wind and solar, Spanish regulated-return refinancings) from `t-market-norms` and `t-market-norms-2`; indicative ranges elsewhere under D-011.
10. Modeling specifics beyond Chapter 45: capture-price build by asset and node; clipping; hybrid (co-located storage) revenue; hedge-settlement liquidity.
11. CSP as the contrast technology: Ivanpah and Noor Ouarzazate I, why CSP with storage was chosen and why PV plus batteries displaced it.
12. Hybrid plants (co-located solar or wind with batteries) as a revenue-protection device, with the storage economics left to Chapter 73.

## 70.4 Locked table of contents

Opening (no heading). See 70.5.

`\section*{What you will be able to do}`.

**70.1 The economics of plants with no fuel bill** (`sec:70.1`)
- 70.1.1 Cost falls, revenue risk rises (`ssec:70.1.1`). Capex dominates; opex is small and fixed; with technology cost falling, value depends on the price captured. Exhibit 70.1 (cost structure of a wind farm and a solar plant compared, Illustrative shares). Verified context from `t-power-tech-norms`: in Lazard's LCOE+ 2026, renewables remain the cheapest new-build energy on an unsubsidized basis, but every technology's LCOE rose year on year; LCOE is cited from sec:11.8, not re-derived.
- 70.1.2 Scale and portfolios (`ssec:70.1.2`). Why sponsors finance portfolios (diversified resource, shared costs) and how lenders treat correlation (cross-reference Chapter 9).

**70.2 Turbine, module, and hybrid design choices that move revenue** (`sec:70.2`)
- 70.2.1 Wind turbines, availability, and cold weather (`ssec:70.2.1`). Turbine type certification, manufacturer track record and platform maturity: Siemens Energy withdrew its guidance on June 22, 2023 after failure rates of certain onshore turbine components rose, booked EUR 1.6 billion for quality issues on certain onshore platforms within EUR 2.2 billion of Siemens Gamesa charges, and suspended sales of the 4.X and 5.X onshore platforms (`oem-fleet-defects`; no failed component named and no owner compensation stated); availability guarantees (time-based versus energy-based); icing and cold-weather packages after Uri (FERC-NERC: 27% of affected units were wind; freezing and fuel caused 75% of events, `winter-storm-uri`).
- 70.2.2 Solar modules, inverters, trackers, and clipping (`ssec:70.2.2`). Inverter loading ratio; Example 70.1 (DC/AC ratio decision).
- 70.2.3 Co-located storage as revenue protection (`ssec:70.2.3`). Co-located storage to absorb curtailed energy and move it to higher-priced hours; the corporate market's shift toward hybrid, baseload-like and firm-power contracts (`t-corporate-ppa-market`); one-sentence forward reference to Chapter 73.

**70.3 Auction, corporate, and merchant revenue for wind and solar** (`sec:70.3`)
- 70.3.1 Auctions, CfDs, and utility PPAs (`ssec:70.3.1`). Cross-reference Chapter 19. REIPPPP as program design that made many projects bankable at once (standardized, non-negotiable PPA, IA and direct agreements; bid bonds ZAR 100,000 per MW doubled at preferred bidder; ZAR 193 billion raised in BW1 to BW4, about 65% project-finance debt; `reipppp`).
- 70.3.2 Corporate offtake and hedges (`ssec:70.3.2`). Cross-reference Chapter 20; credit of the corporate buyer (pointer to Chapter 82 for data-center buyers). Verified market data from `t-corporate-ppa-market` (BNEF and Pexapark counts, not mixed): corporate clean-power PPA volumes grew every year to about 62 GW in 2024 (implied) and fell to 55.9 GW in 2025; Meta, Amazon, Google and Microsoft took 49% of 2025 activity; European solar PPA prices fell 8% year on year to Q1 2026 while North American wind and solar offer prices rose (LevelTen P25 index, about 24% and 13%); LevelTen's index covers tenors of 10 to 19 years, shorter than asset life, which is why lenders haircut the merchant tail.
- 70.3.3 Merchant revenue and capture (`ssec:70.3.3`). Example 70.2 (capture decline for a Spanish solar plant). Verified context from `t-electricity-market-design`: Germany recorded negative wholesale prices in 457 of 8,784 hours in 2024, against 301 in 2023.
- Exhibit 70.2: revenue models ranked by what they leave with the project company (price, shape, basis, volume, curtailment).

**70.4 Resource, congestion, grid, and policy risk in wind and solar** (`sec:70.4`)
- 70.4.1 Resource and performance (`ssec:70.4.1`). Cross-reference Chapters 9 and 48; Ivanpah's guaranteed energy production shortfall as the extreme of performance risk (`ivanpah`).
- 70.4.2 Congestion, curtailment, and basis (`ssec:70.4.2`). Chile's decoupling; Example 70.3 (nodal basis on a fixed-price block contract). Curtailment of about 5.6 to 5.9 TWh in 2024, roughly a fifth of solar and wind output, and 6,084 GWh in 2025 (`chile-solar`).
- 70.4.3 Grid connection and evacuation (`ssec:70.4.3`). Lake Turkana: plant ready July 2017, line energized September 2018, full COD March 2019; penalty of about EUR 127 million reported (`lake-turkana`; do not assert the mechanism). Interconnection queue risk stated generically.
- 70.4.4 Policy dependence (`ssec:70.4.4`). Sector consequences of US federal credits after OBBBA as of October 3, 2026 (rules owned by sec:67.10): wind and solar facilities must be placed in service by December 31, 2027 unless construction began by July 4, 2026; prohibited-foreign-entity rules (`t-us-tax-credits`); Example 70.4. Spain's retroactive cuts summarized in one paragraph with cross-references to Chapters 19, 54 and 60.
- 70.4.5 Weather extremes and hedge volume (`ssec:70.4.5`). Uri from the asset side: Clearway's disclosed USD 50 million revenue reduction and USD 25 million cash impact (`winter-storm-uri`); hedge mechanics stay in Chapter 20.

**70.5 Turbine, module, interconnection, and land contracts** (`sec:70.5`)
- 70.5.1 Wind turbine supply, balance of plant, and the full-service agreement (`ssec:70.5.1`). Why wind is usually split-contracted (turbine supply agreement and balance of plant applied from ssec:23.2.3; full-service agreement from ssec:24.3.1, never abbreviated); the OEM's warranty and availability guarantee as the performance wrap.
- 70.5.2 Solar modules, EPC, and warranties (`ssec:70.5.2`). Module supply agreement, product and performance warranties, supplier credit for module makers (cross-reference Chapter 23).
- 70.5.3 Interconnection, land, and permits (`ssec:70.5.3`). Cross-reference Chapter 25; the evacuation-completion test lenders should ask for (Lake Turkana).
- 70.5.4 The turbine availability guarantee (`ssec:70.5.4`). Clause 70.1 variants a (sponsor-friendly), b (lender-friendly) and c (OEM-friendly), set in a full-service agreement for a 2025 Texas wind farm with fictional parties, with annotations and an indicative landing paragraph (D-011).
- Exhibit 70.3: contract map of a split-contracted wind farm (TikZ; project company, turbine supplier, BOP contractor, full-service provider, offtaker, transmission owner, landowners, tax equity investor, lenders).

**70.6 Financing terms for onshore wind and solar** (`sec:70.6`)
- 70.6.1 The US ladder by revenue quality (`ssec:70.6.1`). Verified (Norton Rose Fulbright panels, `t-market-norms`): early 2026 P50 DSCRs of 1.25x to 1.30x for contracted solar, 1.35x to 1.40x for contracted wind; merchant 1.75x (solar) and 1.80x (wind) on P50; merchant P99 one-year 1.40x to 1.50x for wind (early 2025); construction spreads about SOFR plus 150 bps for clean deals in early 2026 with some at 125 to 137.5 bps; term loans SOFR plus 162.5 to 187.5 bps, with 10% to 20% merchant revenue adding about 25 bps and 30% to 40% merchant adding a further 25 bps; a practical merchant ceiling of 25% to 30% suggested in 2026. Example 70.5.
- 70.6.2 Tax-credit bridges and back-leverage (`ssec:70.6.2`). Verified: committed tax-equity bridges at a 98% advance rate priced about SOFR plus 150 to 162.5 bps (2024, 2025); uncovered credit-sale bridges at 75% of 90% of face (net 67.5%) priced SOFR plus 225 to 300 bps (2024) and about 225 bps (2026). Back-levered debt is structurally subordinated to tax equity (`t-us-tax-credits`; mechanics in Chapter 32).
- 70.6.3 DFI-led and auction markets outside the US (`ssec:70.6.3`). Verified contrasts only: India's 20-year rupee loans from Power Finance Corporation with two-quarter DSRAs and a 1.2x DSCR stabilization test (`t-market-norms`); REIPPPP's program-level funding mix (`reipppp`) and, for bid windows 1 to 4, project-finance debt of ZAR 125.6 billion (65% of about ZAR 193 billion) with most awarded projects indicating 70% to 80% debt (Eberhard and Naude 2016, via `t-market-norms-2`; printed only in this attributed form); DFI-led wind and solar under 25-year PPAs from `t-market-norms-2`: Amunet Wind, Egypt (approximately USD 709 million, about 80% debt, signed November 27, 2022), Aysha I Wind, Ethiopia (senior debt up to USD 378 million on up to USD 507 million, about 75%, disclosed June 8, 2026), Central Eléctrica da Namaacha, Mozambique (USD 230 million wind farm funded 75:25 with a 20-year overall tenor, approved August 1, 2025) and UZ Solar 3, Uzbekistan (about 62% debt with an IFC swap on the full notional, signed May 6, 2024); Spanish regulated-return solar thermal refinancings by Atlantica in 2022 and 2023 at 6-month EURIBOR plus 1.50% stepping to 1.70% to 1.80%, maturing 2035 to 2037, with distributions at a DSCR of at least 1.15x (Solacor 1 and 2); a rated Canadian wind bond maturing about 12 months before its feed-in contracts expire (Comber Wind, BBB, rating-case minimum DSCR 1.38x; `t-market-norms`); distribution-test DSCRs on Atlantica's operating assets ranging from 1.10x to 1.30x (`t-market-norms`). Any European, Latin American or Middle East range is printed only as indicative under D-011 with drivers, and never as one of the "Do not state" examples.
- 70.6.4 When the ladder breaks (`ssec:70.6.4`). Atlantica's Chile PV 1 and 2 in default at end-2023 because of low prices, unable to keep DSRA minimum (`t-market-norms`): the case for merchant DSCRs.
- Exhibit 70.4: financing terms for onshore wind and solar, 2023 to 2026 (rows by market and revenue quality; verified or indicative marked).

**70.7 Modeling wind and solar** (`sec:70.7`)
- 70.7.1 From P50 to cash (`ssec:70.7.1`). Cross-reference Chapter 45 for yield, degradation, availability and curtailment in the model; this chapter adds the capture-price layer by node and hour-block, and the hub-to-node basis.
- 70.7.2 Clipping, hybrids, and augmentation hooks (`ssec:70.7.2`). How the model carries ILR, clipping and a co-located battery's absorbed energy.
- 70.7.3 Hedge settlement and liquidity (`ssec:70.7.3`). A settlement line for any fixed-shape or fixed-volume hedge and a liquidity facility or reserve sized to a stress (cross-reference Chapters 20 and 37).
- 70.7.4 Tax-credit and tax-equity cash (`ssec:70.7.4`). Where tax equity cash leaves before sponsor-level debt service (cross-reference Chapter 32).

**70.8 REIPPPP, northern Chile, Lake Turkana, and concentrated solar** (`sec:70.8`)
- 70.8.1 REIPPPP and the bankable standard document (`ssec:70.8.1`). Program design, BW1 to BW4 numbers, PV tariff path from BW1 ZAR 2.76/kWh to about ZAR 0.42 to 0.49/kWh in BW7 (nominal), the BW6 reversal when grid scarcity set the price (`reipppp`).
- 70.8.2 Northern Chile and the price of the wrong node (`ssec:70.8.2`). María Elena Solar: KfW IPEX senior debt USD 91 million on a USD 104 million project, insolvent September 2022, liquidation April 2023; Cabo Leones II wind insolvent October 2022; Kimal–Lo Aguirre HVDC due 2029 (`chile-solar`).
- 70.8.3 Lake Turkana and the line that was not there (`ssec:70.8.3`). 310 MW, 365 Vestas V52 turbines, financial close March 2014 with AfDB as MLA, debt of approximately EUR 475 million (`lake-turkana`).
- 70.8.4 Concentrated solar at Ivanpah and Noor Ouarzazate I (`ssec:70.8.4`). Ivanpah: 392 MW gross, DOE-guaranteed FFB loan of up to USD 1.6 billion, term debt USD 1,219 million after the USD 485 million Section 1603 grant repaid the bridge, PG&E forbearance 2015 and amendments 2017, CPUC rejection of the 2025 termination agreements in December 2025 (`ivanpah`). Noor I: 160 MW with about three hours of storage; MASEN as single borrower and single buyer, State covering the tariff gap (about USD 60 million a year at appraisal); output averaging 424 GWh in 2016 to 2021 against 370 GWh target (`noor-ouarzazate`). Lesson: dispatchable solar value against technology and cost risk; detail of Noor's concessional stack in Chapter 34.

**70.9 Walkthrough: building the resource-to-revenue bridge for a solar plant** (`sec:70.9`). See 70.7.
