## 70.5 Opening

Real deal moment from `chile-solar`: María Elena Solar in the Atacama won a regulated supply contract in the 2015 to 2016 auctions to deliver a fixed volume in set daytime hours, on KfW IPEX senior debt of USD 91 million against a USD 104 million cost. Three paragraphs: the plant produced its power; the node where it injected paid nothing for much of the day; the contract obliged it to supply customers in the center, so it bought power at high marginal costs to cover deliveries, which rose further when fossil prices spiked in 2022; it became insolvent in September 2022. Close the opening with the question: if the sun shone and the contract held, which link broke? (No hourly counts, node names or peso arrears; see `chile-solar` Do not state.)

## 70.6 Worked examples

**Example 70.1. Choosing the inverter loading ratio in Rajasthan** (`ex:70.1`, Illustrative; Section 70.2.2). Setting: Thar Ujala Solar Pvt Ltd (fictional), a 180 MWac plant in Rajasthan, India, designing in 2024 for a 25-year auction PPA at INR 2.62/kWh (USD 31.38/MWh at INR 83.50 per USD; both Illustrative); costs are stated in USD for the sponsor's comparison. The plant compares an ILR of 1.20 (216 MWdc) with 1.35 (243 MWdc). Specific yield before clipping 1,840 kWh/kWdc a year; clipping loss 0.8% at 1.20 and 3.1% at 1.35; incremental DC cost USD 410/kWdc. Energy: 394.26 GWh at 1.20; 433.26 GWh at 1.35; increment 39.0 GWh a year. Incremental capex 27 MWdc × USD 410/kW = USD 11.07 million; capital recovery factor at 7.4% over 30 years 0.08385; annualized USD 0.928 million; cost of incremental energy USD 23.80/MWh. Lesson: the extra DC buys shoulder-hour energy at USD 23.80/MWh against a PPA price of USD 31.38/MWh, so a higher ILR raises CFADS; the IE checks clipping estimates and inverter warranties.

**Example 70.2. Capture decline on a merchant solar plant in Spain** (`ex:70.2`, Illustrative; Section 70.3.3). Setting: Solar Meseta SL (fictional), a 180 MWac merchant solar plant in Castilla-La Mancha, Spain, reaching COD in 2025 and refinancing with a fictional bank club; the hub prices and capture ratios are the example's, not Iberian market data. The plant produces 404.8 GWh a year at P50 (capacity factor 25.7%), loses 3.5% to curtailment, and sells at the Spanish day-ahead hub. Hub average price EUR 46.30/MWh, held flat in real terms. Capture ratio 0.88 in year 1, falling 1.8 points a year to a floor of 0.64. Year 1: capture price EUR 40.74/MWh, revenue EUR 15.92 million. Year 5: ratio 0.808, EUR 37.41/MWh, EUR 14.61 million. Year 10: 0.718, EUR 33.24/MWh, EUR 12.99 million. Year 14: 0.646, EUR 29.91/MWh, EUR 11.68 million. The floor binds from year 15 (0.64; EUR 29.63/MWh; EUR 11.58 million). Cumulative revenue years 1 to 15 is EUR 204.77 million against EUR 238.74 million if capture stayed at 0.88. Lesson: a falling capture ratio is a revenue decline with the hub price unchanged; lenders size on it, not on the hub (capture ratio defined in sec:11.12). Equation 70.1: captured revenue $= E \times (1 - c) \times p^{\mathrm{hub}} \times k_t$, with $E$ energy, $c$ curtailment and $k_t$ the capture ratio.

**Example 70.3. A fixed-price contract at the wrong node in northern Chile** (`ex:70.3`, Illustrative, modeled on the mechanism in `chile-solar`; Section 70.4.2). Setting: Atacama Sur Solar SpA (fictional), 2022, a 120 MW plant in the Antofagasta region with a regulated supply contract to fictional distribution companies in central Chile; the prices are the example's. The plant has a contract to deliver 260 GWh a year in set daytime hours at USD 61.40/MWh, settled at the load node in the center. It generates 296.8 GWh, of which 19% is curtailed; the injection node averages USD 4.20/MWh in solar hours; the withdrawal node averages USD 88.70/MWh in the contract hours. Contract revenue USD 15.96 million; cost of buying the contract volume at the withdrawal node USD 23.06 million; spot sales of uncurtailed output USD 1.01 million; net revenue negative USD 6.09 million. In the design case (both nodes at USD 48.10/MWh, no curtailment) the same plant earned USD 17.73 million. The break-even withdrawal price, with everything else unchanged, is USD 65.28/MWh. Lesson: shape, basis and volume are three separate exposures; a fixed price removes none of them.

**Example 70.4. What the investment tax credit is worth to an Arizona solar project in 2026** (`ex:70.4`, Illustrative, US law as of October 3, 2026; Section 70.4.4). Setting: Saguaro Flats Solar LLC (fictional), Arizona, which began construction in May 2026, selling its credit to a fictional corporate buyer. The 200 MWac project costs USD 1,146/kWac (USD 229.2 million); 94% of cost is eligible basis; credit rate 30% (no adders assumed); credit USD 64.63 million; sold under the transferability rules at USD 0.925 per USD 1 of credit, proceeds USD 59.79 million. Spread over 30 years at 7.4% the proceeds equal USD 5.013 million a year, or USD 11.42/MWh on 438.9 GWh a year. The writer states that wind and solar credits now require placing in service by December 31, 2027 unless construction began by July 4, 2026 (`t-us-tax-credits`), that the prohibited-foreign-entity material-assistance rules (sec:67.10) apply to construction beginning after December 31, 2025, and that the reader must check the current statute and IRS notices (26 U.S.C. §§48E, 6418; Notices 2025-42 and 2026-15); the rules themselves are cross-referenced to sec:67.10. The price per dollar of credit (0.925) is the Case R input also used for R7 and R8 (Case Bible 3.6); the writer says it is an illustrative price, consistent with the 2024 market price points quoted in `t-us-tax-credits` (ITC about 92.5 cents).

**Example 70.5. One Arizona solar cash flow, two debt amounts** (`ex:70.5`, Illustrative; Section 70.6.1). Setting: Gila Bend Solar LLC (fictional), Arizona, approaching term conversion in early 2026 with a fictional bank club. The project's CFADS is USD 21.74 million a year for 18 years; all-in debt cost 6.35%; annuity factor 10.5487. Sized at 1.30x as fully contracted: annual debt service USD 16.723 million, debt USD 176.4 million. Sized at 1.75x as merchant: debt service USD 12.423 million, debt USD 131.0 million. Difference USD 45.4 million, about a quarter of the contracted debt. At 1.40x (a contracted wind ratio): USD 163.8 million. The writer ties the ratios to the dated US ladder (verified) and says plainly that a real deal sizes each revenue bucket at its own ratio (cross-reference Chapter 36 and Case R's bucket sizing in Chapter 31).

## 70.7 Walkthrough

**Walkthrough: building the resource-to-revenue bridge for a solar plant** (`sec:70.9`). The artifact is Exhibit 70.5, an Illustrative one-page bridge table for Riverina Sun Pty Ltd (fictional), a 150 MWac plant in New South Wales, Australia, prepared for a 2025 refinancing, built step by step with Framework 70.1:
1. Start from the IE's P50 net yield at the meter (GWh), stating the loss assumptions already inside it (soiling, mismatch, inverter, transformer, availability).
2. Subtract curtailment by cause (economic, grid, regulatory), each with its source and whether any contract compensates it.
3. Split the remaining energy by hour-block (morning shoulder, midday, evening shoulder) from the yield model's 8,760 profile.
4. Price each block at the node, not the hub: hub price times hub-to-node basis.
5. Apply contract terms: which blocks a PPA or hedge covers, at what price, settled where.
6. Compute captured revenue by block and the plant's capture ratio; compare with the market advisor's.
7. Run the low case (capture decline 1.5 times faster) and a curtailment stress (+3 points).
8. Mark every line by who bears it (project, offtaker, hedge counterparty) and which revenue bucket it belongs to for sizing.
9. Write the credit-paper sentence: the link with the largest downside per MWh.

## 70.8 Frameworks

**Framework 70.1: Resource-to-revenue bridge** (`fw:resource-revenue-bridge`; original). Purpose: turn a wind or solar yield estimate into revenue with every loss and price adjustment visible and owned. Steps: (1) gross resource; (2) technical losses and availability; (3) curtailment by cause; (4) delivery profile by hour or block; (5) node price and basis; (6) contract and hedge overlay; (7) captured revenue; (8) risk owner and sizing bucket for each line. When to use: screening, IE and market report review, and lender sizing of any variable renewable asset. Demonstrated in the walkthrough and Examples 70.2 and 70.3.

Applied, not re-taught: Chapter 15 bankability ladder; Chapter 28 gap scan.

## 70.10 Running-case installment

**Case R: capture, curtailment, and credits at Mesa Corta** (`sec:70.10`). Case Bible Part 6, Chapter 70 row: story date 2022 to 2025; characters Carmen Villarreal-Ochoa and Rafael Quintanilla; figures R-F06.

First sentence states the time span: three years after Lattimer closed the A1 acquisition in March 2022.

Scene outline (`casescene`, "Lattimer Infrastructure Partners, Houston, February 2025"). Carmen brings the 2024 settlement data for Peeler Draw (R4) and Calloway Mesa (R5) ("Let me pull the settlement data," used once). West solar captured less than the A1 base case had assumed; West wind curtailment has stepped up toward the 6.0% the Case Bible sets from 2025. Rafael defends the A1 assumption, then asks about shape ("What's the shape?" used at most once); he is wrong about West solar capture (Case Bible 4.3: his A1 assumption is the high case in hindsight), and the narration shows it with the ledger figures, not with dialogue. Carmen pushes for a battery at the R5 site; Rafael says the fund already learned that lesson on R6 and R7 (pointer to Chapter 73). The scene ends unresolved.

Narration outside the box: Exhibit 70.6 (`\caser`), capture price and revenue build for R1, R3, R4 and R5, 2022 to 2030, base and low, from Case Bible ledger: R-F06 (no new computation); the reading uses Framework 70.1 to name which link moved (capture ratio and curtailment, not yield). Then one paragraph on tax credits: R3's PTC partnership flip and R5's ITC partnership flip with Castellan Bank's tax-equity desk (Case Bible 3.3 inputs: R3 99% of tax and 40% of cash to the investor until an expected flip on December 31, 2029, then 5%; R5 99% and 20% until June 30, 2027, then 5%), and why OBBBA's 2027 placed-in-service deadline matters for any future repowering of R1 (one-sentence forward reference to Chapter 88). All prices labeled Illustrative with the Case Bible exhibit source line: "Case Bible illustrative price paths; not ERCOT settlement data and not a forecast."

Ledger figures displayed: R-F06. Inputs cited: Case Bible 3.3 (assets, curtailment, tax equity), 3.5 (capture ratios, hub ratios, basis).

## 70.11 Practitioner's notebook (outline)

Checklist: IE yield report with loss stack and P-values; market advisor capture-price forecast by node and hour-block, base and low; curtailment history and forecast by cause; interconnection agreement, network upgrade obligations and evacuation completion; turbine or module supplier credit and warranty backing; availability guarantee basis (time or energy) and LD rate; cold-weather package (wind) and hail or wind-stow design (solar trackers) as stated by the IE; tax-credit qualification evidence (beginning-of-construction and prohibited-foreign-entity diligence) for US projects; hedge settlement node and volume definition; land rights for the full debt life plus decommissioning.

Red flags: capture forecast stated at the hub only; a fixed-price contract settled at a node different from injection; curtailment assumed equal to history in a market adding capacity faster than transmission; evacuation built by a third party with no deemed-energy cover (Lake Turkana); first-of-a-kind technology sized on P50 (Ivanpah); a bid tariff below the recent auction trend with no explanation (Chile, REIPPPP late rounds); a hedge with fixed hourly volume on a wind farm without force-majeure or volume relief (Uri); policy support that is statutory rather than contractual (Spain).

Rules of thumb and their limits: the US DSCR ladder (contracted solar 1.25x to 1.30x, wind 1.35x to 1.40x, merchant 1.75x to 2.0x) is a dated US bank view, not a global rule; capture ratios fall with penetration, but storage and transmission can reverse the trend locally (Chile's batteries); a higher ILR raises energy cheaply until clipping and inverter warranties bite.

Common mistakes: modeling curtailment as a fixed percentage across the debt life; applying one-year P90 to a portfolio without correlation (Chapter 9); treating tax-credit proceeds as project revenue available to senior lenders when tax equity ranks ahead; ignoring the cost of hedge collateral or LCs (Case R's R3 USD 12.0 million LC).

Questions experts ask: At which node is this settled, and what was the basis last year? What share of curtailment is compensated? Who builds the line, and what happens to our revenue if it is late? What does the OEM guarantee, on what basis, and who stands behind it? If the tax credit failed, what would equity lose and what would lenders lose?

## 70.12 Judgment drill

The situation. You are the lead arranger on a 300 MWac solar plant in the Atacama region of northern Chile, a nodal market with fast-growing solar (Illustrative; fictional sponsor), in May 2026. The sponsor won a 20-year auction contract at a record-low price, settled at the load node 900 km south; it asks for 75% gearing sized at 1.30x on P50 with no basis hedge and no storage. The market advisor's base case shows injection-node prices near zero for 40% of solar hours by year 5 and curtailment rising from 4% to 15%. The term sheet goes to the sponsor tomorrow.

Reasoning it through: apply Framework 70.1; the contract fixes price but leaves basis and volume with the project (Example 70.3 mechanism); the record-low bid embeds an assumption that basis stays low; the expert answer: do not size the contracted bucket at a contracted ratio until basis is covered; either (a) require co-located storage or a financial transmission right or basis hedge for the contract volume, or (b) size the basis-exposed revenue at merchant ratios and cap gearing well below the request; add a curtailment stress, a liquidity reserve for negative settlement, and a covenant tied to the commissioning date of the planned transmission line. Show the sponsor the debt amount under each.

What would change the answer: a firm, financed transmission line with a delivery date before COD (basis risk falls); a contract settled at the injection node (basis moves to the buyer); storage sized to shift the contract volume (shape and some basis covered); a creditworthy buyer willing to take curtailment risk (volume risk moves).

## 70.13 Exercise set (14 exercises)

Tier 1 (4):
- 70.1 Explain why falling solar capex raised, rather than lowered, the share of revenue risk in a solar project's credit analysis.
- 70.2 Name the three separate exposures a fixed-price block contract leaves with a solar plant and give the market variable that drives each.
- 70.3 Explain why wind is usually split-contracted while solar is often built under a single EPC.
- 70.4 State two reasons lenders size merchant renewable revenue at higher DSCRs, citing the dated US ladder.

Tier 2 (6):
- 70.5 Recompute Example 70.2 year 10 in the low case (decline 1.5 times as fast, 2.7 points a year; floor 0.04 lower, 0.60) (answer: ratio 0.637; revenue EUR 11.52 million).
- 70.6 In Example 70.3, find the withdrawal-node price at which net revenue is zero (answer: USD 65.28/MWh).
- 70.7 Size Example 70.5's cash flow at 1.40x and compare with 1.30x (answer: USD 163.8 million; USD 12.6 million less).
- 70.8 Recompute Example 70.4 with a credit price of USD 0.90 (answer: proceeds USD 58.17 million).
- 70.9 Draft a turbine availability guarantee (energy-based) with LDs and an annual cap; annotate each paragraph.
- 70.10 Build the bridge table of Exhibit 70.5 for Potiguar Ventos SA (fictional), a 214 MW wind farm in Rio Grande do Norte, Brazil, in 2025 (prices as USD equivalents, Illustrative). Inputs: P50 net yield at the meter 812.4 GWh (losses and availability already inside); grid curtailment 3.2% and economic curtailment 1.8% of P50, neither compensated; 46% of energy in the day block and 54% in the night block; hub prices USD 31.80/MWh (day) and USD 44.60/MWh (night); node price equals 0.93 times hub; 60% of energy sold as-produced under a PPA at USD 38.25/MWh settled at the node; the rest merchant at the node (answer: curtailment 26.0 GWh plus 14.6 GWh; energy sold 771.8 GWh; PPA 463.1 GWh, USD 17.71 million; merchant day 142.0 GWh at USD 29.57/MWh, USD 4.20 million; merchant night 166.7 GWh at USD 41.48/MWh, USD 6.91 million; total USD 28.83 million, USD 37.35/MWh realized; the largest downside per MWh is the 7% hub-to-node basis on the merchant 40%).

Tier 3 (4):
- 70.11 Using Case Bible ledger R-F06, write a half-page note explaining which of R4's and R5's revenue changes from 2022 to 2025 came from capture rather than volume; no new running-case computation.
- 70.12 Model task: add an hour-block capture layer to Example 70.2's year-1 revenue in Excel. Inputs: energy 404.8 GWh, curtailment 3.5%, hub EUR 46.30/MWh; three blocks taking 30%, 45% and 25% of energy (morning shoulder, midday, evening shoulder); block price ratios to hub 1.02, 0.71 and 1.18 in the base case and 0.97, 0.55 and 1.12 in the low case; a curtailment sensitivity of +3 points. Layout: blocks in rows, cases in columns, one formula per row (answer: base EUR 16.65 million, capture ratio 0.920; low EUR 14.80 million, 0.819; base with curtailment at 6.5%, EUR 16.13 million; the midday block's ratio drives the gap, so the expert models the block, not an annual ratio).
- 70.13 Case problem: a Lake Turkana-type project (Illustrative: a fictional 312 MW wind farm in East Africa, 2026) whose evacuation line, built by a state transmission company, is 18 months late. Inputs: P50 1,437.6 GWh a year; tariff EUR 75.20/MWh; the PPA pays deemed energy at 80% of P50 revenue after a six-month grace period; senior debt service EUR 58.7 million a year. Specify the deemed-energy cover, the government support and the lender conditions you would require, and quantify lost revenue (answer: revenue at risk EUR 162.16 million over 18 months; the grace period costs EUR 54.05 million; deemed energy pays EUR 86.49 million for the remaining 12 months, leaving EUR 75.68 million lost; deemed energy covers 12 months of debt service with EUR 27.79 million to spare, so the gap is the six-month grace, which a DSRA of at least six months' debt service, EUR 29.35 million, or a government-backed grace payment must cover).
- 70.14 Case problem: a US solar project that began construction on July 1, 2026 and expects to be placed in service in 2029; explain the tax-credit status under the law as of October 3, 2026, what diligence the lenders need, and where the current rule is found.

## 70.15 Glossary entries owned

| Term | Abbreviation | Definition | Home section |
|---|---|---|---|
| inverter loading ratio | ILR | The ratio of a solar plant's DC module capacity to its AC inverter capacity, also called the DC/AC ratio. | 70.2.2 |
| clipping loss | – | Energy a solar plant's modules could produce but its inverters cannot deliver because DC output exceeds AC capacity. | 70.2.2 |
| hybrid plant | – | A wind or solar plant with co-located storage behind a shared grid connection. | 70.2.3 |
| module supply agreement | – | The contract for supply of solar modules with product and performance warranties. | 70.5.2 |
| nodal decoupling | – | A persistent gap between prices at a generator's injection node and its customers' withdrawal node, caused by transmission congestion. | 70.4.2 |
| resource-to-revenue bridge | – | Framework 70.1: the step-by-step conversion of a resource estimate into captured revenue with each loss and price adjustment assigned to a risk owner. | 70.9 |

Removed (homed elsewhere; used without bold and cross-referenced): turbine supply agreement and balance of plant (ssec:23.2.3; R-067); full-service agreement (ssec:24.3.1; R-121; never abbreviated, R-114).

## 70.16 Neighbors

Chapter 69 (Thermal power) teaches the plants whose fuel chain and dispatch decide their credit: screening curves, capacity-plus-energy PPAs, tolling, merchant spark spreads, fuel take-or-pay, and the thermal IPPs that failed with the plant running. Chapter 71 (Offshore wind) takes the wind resource offshore, where the revenue contract is usually firm (CfDs, ORECs) but construction becomes the dominant risk: marine installation, vessel and weather windows, multi-contract interfaces, multi-billion financings with ECAs, and the pre-FID exposure that cancelled Ocean Wind.

## 70.17 Coverage check

- "Sector deep dives… onshore wind; … solar" (coverage P): Sections 70.1 to 70.8.
- Coverage P preamble items (economics, asset, revenue, risks, contracts, terms, modeling, landmark deals): Sections 70.1 to 70.8.
- Real cases from standards Section 7: Chile's northern solar build-out, Winter Storm Uri (asset angle), Lake Turkana, REIPPPP, Ivanpah, Spain (one paragraph); second-wave sheets placed as listed in 70.9.

# Chapter 71. Offshore wind

## 71.0 Rulings applied

- R-121: serial defect is homed in Chapter 22 (ssec:22.7.1); Chapter 71 applies it to turbine platforms and cables and drops it from its glossary.
- R-015: decommissioning obligations and security are owned by Chapter 65 (sec:65.2); Chapter 71 applies the offshore rules and cites sec:65.2.
- R-073: US credit rules are owned by sec:67.10 and sec:32.9; Chapter 71 states only their offshore consequences.
- R-001: LCOE cited from sec:11.8.
- R-101, R-105, R-113: Part XVI ownership; Case Bible Part 6 row with Annex TR R.9 (the Lattimer IC meets on Tuesday, May 20, 2025); architecture title.

## 71.1 Header

- Chapter: 71
- Title: Offshore wind
- File: `chapters/71-offshore-wind.tex`
- Label: `ch:71`
- Target length: 9,000 words (raised in round 1 for the Vineyard Wind and 2025 US policy material; a floor, not a cap)
- Dominant expert lens: the independent engineer and the insurance advisor for marine construction, paired with the senior banker who has syndicated multi-billion ECA-supported offshore wind loans. The sponsor lens is carried by the pre-FID decision (Ocean Wind); the government lens by auction design and revenue support.

## 71.2 Purpose and capability statement

Purpose. Offshore wind turned the risk profile of onshore wind inside out: revenue is usually firm, because governments award long CfDs or certificate contracts, while construction becomes a marine campaign worth billions, split across a handful of contractors and a few dozen specialized vessels, and exposed to weather, supply chains and serial defects in ever-larger turbines. This chapter teaches how offshore wind projects are built, contracted and financed, how lenders protect themselves through construction (contingency, interfaces, insurance, ECA support, phase ring-fencing), and why the most expensive failure of the 2020s, Ocean Wind, happened before any debt was signed: a fixed revenue price locked years before costs and interest rates were.

What you will be able to do:
- Explain what drives offshore wind economics and construction risk, and read an offshore wind contract package for interfaces and gaps (Capability 3, Capability 13).
- Structure the debt for an offshore wind phase, including two-part gearing for generation and transmission assets and ECA participation (Capability 5, Capability 8).
- Measure a developer's pre-FID exposure between revenue award and financial close, and decide what to lock and when (Capability 12, Capability 15).

## 71.3 Concepts owned and concepts assumed

### Owned

1. Offshore wind industry economics: capex per MW, the components (turbines, foundations, array and export cables, offshore and onshore substations, installation), scale economics and their limits, supply-chain concentration (vessels, cables, monopiles, turbine factories).
2. How an offshore wind farm works for a financier: the physical system from turbine to onshore grid, access and availability at sea, wake effects (yield modeling stays in Chapter 45).
3. Revenue models for offshore wind: two-sided CfDs (UK, Poland), state certificate contracts (New Jersey ORECs), corporate PPAs and merchant tails; how the award timing relative to FID creates exposure.
4. Construction risk specific to offshore wind: weather windows, installation vessel availability, multi-contract interfaces (structure in Chapter 23), serial defects in new turbine platforms (term homed in ssec:22.7.1; Vineyard Wind 2024), cable failures, ground conditions on the seabed; and political and regulatory risk after permitting in US offshore wind (stop-work orders and lease suspensions of 2025 and 2026, `us-offshore-wind-2025`) as a lender exposure distinct from Ocean Wind's economic risk.
5. The offshore transmission split: transmission assets financed or sold separately (the UK OFTO model), and two-part gearing (Dogger Bank).
6. The pre-FID exposure clock (Framework 71.1, original): the gap between revenue price lock, capex lock, interest-rate lock and tax-credit certainty.
7. The offshore wind contract set: turbine supply, foundations, cables, substations, transport and installation (T&I), marine warranty surveyor, insurance (cross-references), and Clause 71.1 on weather downtime sharing in a T&I contract.
8. Financing terms for offshore wind 2020 to 2026: verified deal anchors (Dogger Bank A, B and C; Baltic Power; Inch Cape; East Anglia THREE; Vineyard Wind 1; Empire Wind 1) and indicative ranges under D-011.
9. Sponsor sell-downs at or around financial close as a capital-recycling technique specific to large offshore phases (Dogger Bank, Eni and Vårgrønn).
10. Modeling specifics: availability as a function of access, cable outage scenarios, delay-to-revenue mechanics under milestone or long-stop dates, construction contingency at P90 (cross-reference Chapter 45 for yield).
11. Decommissioning of offshore structures as a sector-specific obligation (security and timing from `t-decommissioning-liabilities`; principles in sec:65.2).

Opening (no heading). See 71.5.

`\section*{What you will be able to do}`.

**71.1 The economics of building at sea** (`sec:71.1`)
- 71.1.1 Where the money goes (`ssec:71.1.1`). Capex components and the shift of value from turbines to installation and transmission as farms move farther offshore. Exhibit 71.1 (Illustrative capex breakdown for a 1.2 GW phase, shares only). Verified US new-build benchmark from `t-power-tech-norms` (Lazard LCOE+ 2026): capex of about USD 5,600 to 7,100/kW, LCOE of about USD 105 to 167/MWh and O&M of about USD 60 to 92/kW-year, which is why offshore wind depends on CfDs or other revenue support.
- 71.1.2 Scale, supply chain, and the vessel bottleneck (`ssec:71.1.2`). Larger turbines cut cost per MW and raise serial-defect risk; few installation vessels; slot reservations (cross-reference Chapter 23's supply-chain section).

**71.2 How an offshore wind farm works for a financier** (`sec:71.2`)
- 71.2.1 From turbine to onshore grid (`ssec:71.2.1`). Foundations (monopile, jacket, floating as frontier), array cables, offshore substation, export cable, onshore substation. Exhibit 71.2 (TikZ schematic: turbines, array, offshore substation, export cable, onshore substation, grid; at most 8 nodes).
- 71.2.2 Access, availability, and losses (`ssec:71.2.2`). Weather-limited access, wake loss, electrical loss; Example 71.1.

**71.3 CfDs, certificates, and corporate offtake offshore** (`sec:71.3`)
- 71.3.1 Two-sided CfDs (`ssec:71.3.1`). Cross-reference Chapter 19; Dogger Bank's 15-year CfDs at GBP 39.65/MWh (A) and GBP 41.61/MWh (B, C) in 2012 prices, CPI-indexed (`dogger-bank`); AR7 offshore results of January 14, 2026: about 8.4 GW at GBP 91.20/MWh (England and Wales) and GBP 89.49/MWh (Scotland) in 2024 prices, contracts lengthened to 20 years (`t-uk-cfd-rab`).
- 71.3.2 Certificate contracts and fixed escalators (`ssec:71.3.2`). New Jersey ORECs: Ocean Wind 1 first-year price USD 98.10/MWh, fixed escalation, no capex or rate indexation (`ocean-wind`).
- 71.3.3 Corporate offtake and merchant tails (`ssec:71.3.3`). Brief; cross-reference Chapter 20. Verified contract-length contrast from `t-market-norms-2`: Baltic Power holds a 25-year CfD and Hai Long (Taiwan, 1.0 GW) a 30-year corporate PPA, against the 15-year CfDs of the UK projects (Dogger Bank, Inch Cape, East Anglia THREE); the writer asks how contract length should change tenor and gearing.

**71.4 Construction risk at sea** (`sec:71.4`)
- 71.4.1 Weather, vessels, and the installation season (`ssec:71.4.1`). Example 71.2 (cost of a lost installation season).
- 71.4.2 Interfaces in a multi-contract build (`ssec:71.4.2`). Applies Chapter 23; the T&I contract and weather downtime; Clause 71.1.
- 71.4.3 Serial defects and cables (`ssec:71.4.3`). Why a defect in one turbine model or one cable batch hits every unit (serial defect defined in ssec:22.7.1); warranty caps, serial-defect clauses; lenders' insurance requirements (cross-reference Chapter 27). Landmark case from `vineyard-wind-2024`: Vineyard Wind 1 (806 MW, 62 GE Haliade-X turbines, 50:50 Avangrid Renewables and CIP funds; nearly USD 2.3 billion of senior debt from nine banks at financial close on September 15, 2021, the first for a commercial-scale US offshore wind farm; a USD 1.2 billion tax equity package on October 25, 2023). On July 13, 2024, during GE's commissioning and before the owner took operational control, a blade on turbine AW-38 failed; BSEE suspended operations; GE Vernova's root cause analysis preliminarily identified a manufacturing deviation that its quality assurance should have caught; remediation re-reviewed over 8,300 ultrasound records per blade, added crawler inspections and fiber-optic monitoring and removed some blades; blade installation resumed under conditions from October 22, 2024; Vineyard Wind and GE Vernova settled all litigation on confidential terms on September 16, 2026, with all turbines capable of producing power. `oem-fleet-defects` adds the OEM side: GE Vernova recorded approximately USD 0.9 billion of incremental contract losses in the second half of 2024 across Vineyard Wind and Dogger Bank. Lesson: one component failure became a fleet inspection and a regulatory shutdown; lenders rely on the OEM's credit, the warranty and liability terms, and the certified verification agent (DNV's load analysis supported the partial restart). Not stated: the Gaspé bonding detail as a final finding, the claim amounts in the litigation, insurance recoveries, lender waivers, or a full-COD date (Do not state).
- 71.4.4 Dogger Bank A's delays (`ssec:71.4.4`). Weather, vessel availability, supply chain and blade incidents in 2024 pushed full completion of A into 2026; all 95 A turbines installed by February 2026; B installation expected to run into Q2 2027 (`dogger-bank`). No overrun, waiver or CfD amendment stated (Do not state).

**71.5 Pre-FID exposure and the Ocean Wind cancellation** (`sec:71.5`)
- 71.5.1 The exposure clock (`ssec:71.5.1`). Framework 71.1; Exhibit 71.3 (timeline: award, reservation payments, FID, financial close, COD; Illustrative dates).
- 71.5.2 Ocean Wind 1 and 2 (`ssec:71.5.2`). Facts from `ocean-wind`: OREC award June 2019 at USD 98.10/MWh first-year; long-dated US rates up about 300 bps (Ørsted's figure); PSEG's 25% bought out; July 2023 state law on ITCs; cessation decision October 31, 2023; 2023 impairments DKK 26.8 billion for the year of which DKK 19.9 billion Ocean Wind 1, DKK 9.6 billion cancellation fees, DKK 15.0 billion onerous-contract provision; USD 125 million paid to New Jersey in May 2024 against USD 300 million of escrow and guarantee at stake. No project finance had closed. Example 71.3.
- 71.5.3 Indexation, longer contracts, and US political risk after 2023 (`ssec:71.5.3`). Indexation of strike prices to inflation; longer contracts (UK AR7, 20 years). US state indexation mechanisms are not covered by a fact sheet and are stated only as a category. Then the second US shock, from `us-offshore-wind-2025`: the January 20, 2025 presidential memorandum and Interior's pause on new approvals; BOEM's rescission of all designated Wind Energy Areas (July 30, 2025); the stop-work order on Revolution Wind in August 2025 (described by the Connecticut and Rhode Island governors as more than 80% complete; work allowed to resume September 22, 2025); Interior's December 22, 2025 pause of the leases of all five large projects under construction (Vineyard Wind 1, Revolution Wind, CVOW-Commercial, Sunrise Wind, Empire Wind 1), which courts stayed or enjoined for Empire Wind (January 15, 2026) and Vineyard Wind (January 27, 2026); and the 2026 lease buy-backs (TotalEnergies, about USD 928 million; Invenergy, USD 765 million). The writer contrasts this political risk after permitting with Ocean Wind's economic risk before FID, calls the December 2025 action a pause (not a cancellation), and states nothing about Sunrise Wind or CVOW outcomes, Equinor's impairments or Ørsted's financing terms (Do not state).

**71.6 The offshore wind contract set** (`sec:71.6`)
- 71.6.1 Packages and their interfaces (`ssec:71.6.1`). Cross-reference Chapter 23; Exhibit 71.4 (TikZ contract map: project company, turbine supplier, foundation supplier, cable supplier, T&I contractor, offshore substation contractor, transmission owner or OFTO buyer, revenue counterparty, lenders, ECAs, marine warranty surveyor; at most 12 nodes).
- 71.6.2 The marine warranty surveyor and insurers (`ssec:71.6.2`). What the surveyor approves and why insurers require it; cross-reference Chapter 27.
- 71.6.3 Weather downtime in a transport and installation contract (`ssec:71.6.3`). Clause 71.1 variants a (sponsor-friendly), b (lender-friendly) and c (contractor-friendly), set in a T&I contract for a 2027 UK North Sea phase with fictional parties, and an indicative landing paragraph (D-011, labeled).

**71.7 Financing offshore wind** (`sec:71.7`)
- 71.7.1 Phase ring-fencing and two-part gearing (`ssec:71.7.1`). Dogger Bank: each 1.2 GW phase a separate project company; A and B financed together (about GBP 4.8 billion senior plus about GBP 0.7 billion ancillary, 29 banks and three ECAs, November 26, 2020); C on December 2, 2021 (about GBP 2.5 billion senior plus about GBP 435 million ancillary); generation gearing about 65% to 70% (A and B) and about 70% (C); transmission at 90% of forecast OFTO proceeds (`dogger-bank`). Example 71.4.
- 71.7.2 ECAs, DFIs, and new markets (`ssec:71.7.2`). Baltic Power (Poland, 1.14 GW), financial close September 22, 2023, approximately CAD 5.2 billion from 25 institutions including EIB, EBRD and three ECAs; trade press described a construction-plus-20-year facility of approximately EUR 4.2 to 4.4 billion, hedged for the full amortization period at an all-in rate of about 5%; CfD at PLN 319.6/MWh (`t-market-norms`; CfD term not stated). Bpifrance cover tied to turbine exports at Dogger Bank (trade press, `dogger-bank`). Baltic Power's CfD term is now verified at 25 years (Northland Power's Q2 2026 report, `t-market-norms-2`); its debt is stated as approximately CAD 5.2 billion (Northland) or approximately EUR 4.2 to 4.4 billion (trade press and a law-firm headline), with the attribution.
- 71.7.3 US offshore wind under tax credits and federal stop orders (`ssec:71.7.3`). The US capital stack as a template: Vineyard Wind 1's nearly USD 2.3 billion of senior construction debt plus USD 1.2 billion of tax equity (`vineyard-wind-2024`). Empire Wind 1: financial close at the end of December 2024 on a project financing package of over USD 3 billion against expected capital investment of approximately USD 5 billion including expected ITCs, with a 25-year NYSERDA agreement at USD 155.00/MWh for 810 MW and a lender group led by Crédit Agricole CIB as administrative agent and 15 coordinating lead arrangers (`us-offshore-wind-2025`). What the stop orders did to its lenders, from Equinor's disclosure: at the April 16, 2025 order about USD 1.5 billion had been drawn under the term loan; Equinor US Holdings had guaranteed the equity commitment, so in a full stop the drawn debt would be repaid from that commitment and Empire would owe supplier termination fees; the order was lifted May 19, 2025; at the December 2025 pause about USD 2.8 billion had been drawn (USD 2.7 billion in a later release; the writer notes the two figures come from different Equinor releases). Lesson: in a US offshore financing the equity-commitment guarantee, not the project, carried the political risk. No tenor, margin, impairment or COD status stated (Do not state). Ocean Wind's ITC dependence; credit rules cross-referenced to sec:67.10.
- 71.7.4 Sell-downs and equity recycling (`ssec:71.7.4`). Eni's 20% at Dogger Bank around each close, later passed to Vårgrønn (`dogger-bank`; do not state the GBP 405 million figure).
- 71.7.5 Offshore wind tenor, gearing, and cover ranges, 2020 to 2026 (`ssec:71.7.5`). Exhibit 71.5 (deal anchors, verified: Dogger Bank A to C, Baltic Power, Inch Cape GBP 2.7 billion announced January 27, 2025, East Anglia THREE GBP 3.6 billion announced July 31, 2025, Vineyard Wind 1, Empire Wind 1, each with only the terms its sheet verifies; plus indicative ranges under D-011 for tenor and DSCR in European CfD-backed offshore wind, each labeled with market and period and drivers; never "1.15x to 1.20x"). Fraunhofer ISE's July 2024 modeling assumption (offshore wind 70% debt at 7.0%) printed only as a modeling assumption, not a market term. Decommissioning security as a financing term (`t-decommissioning-liabilities`; principles in sec:65.2): UK guidance of March 2019 expects ring-fenced security in favor of the Secretary of State, posted upfront or accrued mid-life (for example starting by year 10 and complete by year 15 for a 15-year CfD), with parent company guarantees not normally accepted; BOEM's Renewable Energy Modernization Rule (effective July 15, 2024) lets US lessees post decommissioning financial assurance before installation and fund it incrementally on an approved schedule, and BOEM's August 2025 review covers financial assurance and decommissioning estimates (`us-offshore-wind-2025`).

## 71.5 Opening

Real deal moment from `ocean-wind`: on October 31, 2023 Ørsted decided to cease development of Ocean Wind 1 and 2 "in the form" awarded by the New Jersey BPU (the writer may quote only the words the fact sheet marks as exact; otherwise paraphrase). Three short paragraphs: in June 2019 the state had awarded a fixed-price OREC contract starting at USD 98.10/MWh; four years later the developer was booking billions of kroner of impairments and cancellation fees on a project that had never reached FID and had no project-finance debt; the loss sat with the sponsor. The question the chapter answers: how can a project with a firm revenue contract become uninvestable before construction starts?

## 71.6 Worked examples

**Example 71.1. From gross to net energy off Taiwan** (`ex:71.1`, Illustrative; Section 71.2.2). Setting: Fengtai Offshore Wind Co. (fictional), a 1,200 MW phase off the Changhua coast, Taiwan, whose lenders' technical adviser reviews the yield in 2025. The phase: gross 4,812.0 GWh a year; wake loss 9.8%; electrical loss 2.6%; availability 94.1%; other losses 1.5%. Net 3,918.5 GWh (net capacity factor 37.3%). At availability 96.0% (better access vessels): 3,997.6 GWh, a gain of 79.1 GWh a year. Lesson: offshore availability is partly a logistics decision, and lenders ask what it costs to buy.

**Example 71.2. The cost of a lost installation season off Scotland** (`ex:71.2`, Illustrative; Section 71.4.1). Setting: Firth Bank Offshore Wind Ltd (fictional), a 950 MW phase in the outer Moray Firth, Scotland, installing turbines in 2026 under a CfD; a fictional jack-up vessel owner holds the installation slot. The phase expects 3,478.6 GWh a year (net capacity factor 41.8%) under a CfD at GBP 58.20/MWh. A late vessel pushes turbine installation past the weather window and full generation slips seven months. Lost revenue GBP 118.1 million; additional interest during construction on GBP 2,930 million of drawn debt at 6.4% for seven months GBP 109.4 million; total GBP 227.5 million before vessel standby and remobilization costs and before any delay LDs recovered. With a four-month slip (Exercise 71.7): lost revenue GBP 67.5 million, extra interest GBP 62.5 million. Lesson: the delay cost is dominated by revenue and interest, not by the contractor's extra costs; delay LDs and DSU insurance (Chapter 27) must be measured against this number. The writer notes that where a revenue contract's term runs from a fixed date, delay also shortens contracted life (principle only).

**Example 71.3. A New Jersey fixed price meets cost inflation and higher rates** (`ex:71.3`, Illustrative, modeled on the mechanism in `ocean-wind`; Section 71.5.2). Setting: Barnegat Shoals Wind LLC (fictional), a 1,000 MW project off New Jersey that won a certificate contract in 2019 and faces FID in 2023; the prices and costs are the example's. The project wins a 25-year contract at USD 96.40/MWh in year 1 escalating 2.0% a year; net capacity factor 44.5% (3,898.2 GWh a year); opex USD 112/kW-year escalating 2.5%; capex spent evenly over three years. At bid capex of USD 3,950/kW, the unlevered pre-tax project IRR is 5.56%. If capex rises to USD 5,180/kW before FID (31% higher), the IRR falls to 3.33%. Restoring 5.56% needs a first-year price of USD 117.01/MWh (21.4% higher). If financing cost moves the required return up by 2.0 percentage points as well (a stylized proxy for a 300 bps rise in long rates on a leveraged structure; the writer states the simplification), the price needed is USD 138.42/MWh (43.6% higher). Lesson: the developer was short inflation and rates from award to FID; the contract had no indexation for either. (Ignore tax credits; state that the ITC would raise the IRR but not change the mechanism.)

**Example 71.4. Two-part gearing for a 1.2 GW North Sea phase** (`ex:71.4`, Illustrative, modeled on the structure described in `dogger-bank`; Section 71.7.1). Setting: Haisborough Bank Wind Ltd (fictional), a 1.2 GW phase off the English east coast reaching financial close in 2021 with a fictional bank and ECA group. Generation assets cost GBP 2,684.3 million; transmission assets GBP 561.8 million; the forecast regulated transfer value of the transmission assets is GBP 598.4 million. Generation debt at 67.5% gearing: GBP 1,811.9 million. Transmission bridge debt at 90% of forecast transfer proceeds: GBP 538.6 million. Total debt GBP 2,350.5 million against total capex GBP 3,246.1 million: blended gearing 72.4%; equity GBP 895.6 million. The 90% advance leaves a 10% cushion: a transfer value 12% below forecast (GBP 526.6 million) leaves the transmission debt GBP 12.0 million short. At 70.0% generation gearing (Exercise 71.6): total debt GBP 2,417.6 million, blended 74.5%. Lesson: the headline gearing of a phase mixes two credits; read each separately. (State GBP as the currency code per style sheet.)

## 71.7 Walkthrough

**Walkthrough: reading an offshore wind construction risk register** (`sec:71.9`). The artifact is Exhibit 71.6, an Illustrative extract of the IE's construction risk register for a 1.2 GW phase (rows: vessel availability; weather downtime; monopile delivery; cable lay and burial; offshore substation topside lift; turbine serial defect; export cable fault during commissioning; port congestion; ground conditions; grid connection date). Columns: description; package and contract; who bears it under the contracts; probability and impact (P50 and P90 cost and days); mitigation; residual to the project company; covered by contingency, LDs, insurance, sponsor support. Steps:
1. Check each row maps to a package and a contract clause (Chapter 23 interface matrix).
2. Check the residual-to-project column against the contingency and the sponsor's cost-overrun commitment.
3. Recompute the P90 total and compare with contingency.
4. Check delay rows against the revenue contract's milestone and long-stop dates.
5. Check insurance: which rows are insurable (marine, CAR, DSU) and which are not (supply-chain delay, weather beyond contract allowances).
6. Write the lenders' conditions: contingency size, sponsor support, insurance deductibles, reporting.

## 71.8 Frameworks

**Framework 71.1: Pre-FID exposure clock** (`fw:pre-fid-exposure-clock`; original). Purpose: measure how long and how much a developer is exposed between fixing its revenue price and fixing its costs and financing. Steps: (1) list each price-setting date: revenue award, turbine and vessel reservations, EPC or package price fixing, interest-rate hedge, tax-credit qualification, FID, financial close; (2) for each, record whether the price or rate is fixed, indexed or open; (3) chart the windows where revenue is fixed but a cost or rate is open; (4) estimate the sensitivity of project IRR to each open item (Example 71.3 method); (5) decide what to lock early (reservations, hedges), what to index (seek contract indexation), and what exit costs are acceptable (completion security, reservation fees). When to use: at bid, at every reservation commitment, and at FID. Demonstrated on Ocean Wind and in the judgment drill.

## 71.9 Real cases

| Slug | Use | Transferable lesson |
|---|---|---|
| `ocean-wind` | Opening; Section 71.5.2; Example 71.3 | A revenue contract fixed years before FID, without capex or rate indexation, leaves the developer short inflation and rates; low completion security makes exit cheaper than completion. |
| `dogger-bank` | Sections 71.4.4, 71.7.1, 71.7.4; Example 71.4 | Phase ring-fencing; generation versus OFTO-bound transmission gearing; ECA participation tied to turbine supply; sell-downs at close; construction delays from weather, vessels, supply chain and blade incidents. |
| `t-market-norms` (Baltic Power, Empire Wind 1, Fraunhofer) | Section 71.7.2, 71.7.3, 71.7.5 | DFI and ECA anchoring of a first offshore market; long tenor with full-tenor hedging; debt about 60% of capex in a US ITC-supported structure. |
| `t-uk-cfd-rab` | Section 71.3.1 | AR7 lengthened contracts to 20 years; strike prices quoted in different base years must be converted before comparison. |
| `vineyard-wind-2024` | Section 71.4.3; Section 71.7.3 | A single blade failure on a new turbine platform became a fleet inspection and a regulatory shutdown; lenders rely on the OEM's credit, the warranty and liability terms, and the certified verification agent; the US senior-debt-plus-tax-equity stack. |
| `us-offshore-wind-2025` | Sections 71.5.3, 71.7.3, 71.7.5; Case R installment | Permitted and project-financed is not safe from political stop orders; in Empire Wind's stack the equity-commitment guarantee carried the risk of a full stop. |
| `oem-fleet-defects` | Section 71.4.3 | The OEM absorbed about USD 0.9 billion of incremental losses across Vineyard Wind and Dogger Bank in the second half of 2024. |
| `t-market-norms-2` | Sections 71.3.3, 71.7.2, 71.7.5 | Contract length (15-year UK CfDs, Baltic Power's 25-year CfD, Hai Long's 30-year corporate PPA) and recent UK financings (Inch Cape, East Anglia THREE). |
| `t-decommissioning-liabilities` | Section 71.7.5 | Offshore decommissioning security is accrued in the contract's second half and rarely accepted as a parent guarantee. |
| `t-power-tech-norms` | Section 71.1.1 | US offshore capex and LCOE explain the dependence on revenue support. |

All fact sheets this chapter needs are delivered; the earlier request for US federal actions in 2025 is met by `us-offshore-wind-2025`.

## 71.10 Running-case installment

**Case R: Lattimer declines an offshore stake** (`sec:71.10`). Case Bible Part 6, Chapter 71 row: Case R (small), 2025; Rafael; no figures. Annex TR R.9 fixes the date: Tuesday, May 20, 2025.

Short installment (about 500 words). First sentence places it on May 20, 2025. Scene (`casescene`, "Lattimer Infrastructure Partners investment committee, New York, May 2025"; no real project or developer is named as the target). A sponsor offers Lattimer Energy Transition Fund II a minority stake in a US offshore wind project before FID. Rafael presents; the committee asks what the fund would control as a minority holder, what is locked and what is open on the exposure clock, and what the fund's 11% to 13% net target (Case Bible 3.2) implies for a pre-FID offshore stake. One committee member notes that BOEM had ordered a project-financed, permitted project under construction to stop work on April 16, 2025 and lifted the order only the day before, May 19, 2025 (Empire Wind 1, `us-offshore-wind-2025`; the real project is named only as the news the committee discusses, never as the target). Rafael's own case is weak on construction: his strength is merchant price and shape, not marine installation (character sheet). The committee declines. Narration after the box applies Framework 71.1 to the offer qualitatively and contrasts it with Mesa Corta's onshore assets; it adds a row the 2019 Ocean Wind clock did not have, federal permits and leases as an open item even after permitting, and notes in one sentence that the December 2025 lease pause later proved the point. No Case R figures; no real project facts beyond the fact sheets.

## 71.11 Practitioner's notebook (outline)

Checklist: package map and interface matrix (Chapter 23); vessel contracts and their weather and standby terms; installation schedule against weather windows with P50 and P90; serial-defect provisions and warranty caps in turbine and cable contracts; marine warranty surveyor scope; CAR and DSU program with offshore-specific deductibles; transmission arrangement (owned, OFTO sale, or third-party); revenue contract milestone, long-stop and delay mechanics; ECA eligibility and content; sponsor completion support and sell-down terms; decommissioning security.

Red flags: a revenue price fixed with no indexation and FID more than two years away; reservation payments that are non-refundable and large relative to equity; one vessel on the critical path with no substitute; a turbine model with little operating history at the rated size; export cable with no spare length or repair plan; contingency below the IE's P90; a sell-down at close that leaves no sponsor with a controlling stake through construction.

Rules of thumb and their limits: generation and transmission gearing differ (Dogger Bank: about 65% to 70% versus 90% of OFTO proceeds), but only where a regulated sale exists; ECAs follow turbine and cable content; delay cost is mostly revenue and interest (Example 71.2).

Common mistakes: comparing strike prices in different base years (2012 versus 2024 prices); reading headline debt as gearing of generation assets; treating weather delay as insurable; ignoring the OFTO transfer value risk.

Questions experts ask: What is fixed today and what is still open? Which vessel installs the turbines, and what happens if it is late? What does the turbine supplier owe us if a blade defect appears in year three across every unit? Who owns the export cable after COD? What do we lose if we walk away now?

## 71.12 Judgment drill

The situation. You sit on the investment committee of a sponsor that won a 20-year CPI-indexed CfD in Great Britain's AR7 round for a 1.4 GW offshore phase (Illustrative; fictional sponsor), in March 2026. FID is 26 months away. The only suitable installation vessel for the planned turbine size offers a slot reservation requiring a non-refundable deposit equal to 12% of the T&I contract price now, with the rest at FID. Without the reservation, the next slot is a year later, after the CfD's milestone date. Turbine price is indexed to steel until FID; financing will be hedged only at financial close. The committee meets Monday.

Reasoning: apply Framework 71.1; the CfD is indexed to CPI (better than Ocean Wind's fixed escalator), but capex, steel and rates remain open; the vessel reservation trades a certain sunk cost for the schedule that protects the revenue contract; an expert would reserve the slot if the deposit is small relative to the cost of missing the milestone, negotiate a step-down refund if FID fails for reasons outside the sponsor's control, seek a pre-hedge or deal-contingent hedge for rates, and set an internal kill point with a known exit cost.

What would change the answer: a second suitable vessel (lower value of reservation); a CfD with no milestone risk (less urgency); a turbine supplier willing to fix price now (less open exposure); a deposit larger than the equity the sponsor can lose without board approval.

## 71.13 Exercise set (12 exercises)

Tier 1 (4):
- 71.1 Explain why offshore wind revenue is usually more certain than onshore wind revenue while construction risk is greater.
- 71.2 Explain the purpose of the 90% advance against forecast OFTO proceeds and what the 10% protects against.
- 71.3 Name four items on the pre-FID exposure clock and say which ones a CPI-indexed CfD covers.
- 71.4 Explain why ECA participation in offshore wind tracks turbine and cable supply.

Tier 2 (5):
- 71.5 Using the AR6 and AR7 strike prices quoted in 2012 prices in `t-uk-cfd-rab` (GBP 58.87/MWh and GBP 65.45/MWh), compute the change between rounds and explain why comparing Dogger Bank's 2012-price strike with AR7's 2024-price strike of GBP 91.20/MWh without conversion misleads (answer: +11.2% in constant 2012 prices).
- 71.6 Recompute Example 71.4 at 70.0% generation gearing (answer: total debt GBP 2,417.6 million; blended 74.5%).
- 71.7 Recompute Example 71.2 for a four-month slip (answer: lost revenue GBP 67.5 million; extra interest GBP 62.5 million; total GBP 130.0 million).
- 71.8 In Example 71.3, find the capex per kW at which the bid IRR of 5.56% holds at the original price if opex falls 10% (answer: USD 4,130.4/kW, only 4.6% above the bid capex, so opex savings cannot absorb a 31% capex rise; timing convention as in Example 71.3: capex in three equal amounts at years 0, 1 and 2, operations in years 3 to 27).
- 71.9 Draft a weather downtime clause for a T&I contract with a weather allowance and sharing above it; annotate.

Tier 3 (3):
- 71.10 Build Framework 71.1 for Cape Ann Offshore Wind LLC (fictional), a 1,000 MW Massachusetts project. Inputs: certificate contract awarded June 2025 at USD 103.65/MWh first-year, escalating 2.0% a year, no capex or rate indexation; net capacity factor 44.5%; opex USD 112/kW-year escalating 2.5%; base capex USD 4,280/kW spent evenly over three years (Example 71.3 convention), split turbines 36%, transport and installation 12%, foundations 16%, cables 11%, substations 10%, other 15%; turbine price indexed to steel until FID in January 2027 (stress +14%); T&I reserved but price open until FID (stress +20%); foundations fixed in August 2026; cables open (stress +8%); interest-rate hedge only at financial close in February 2027 (stress: a 150 bps rise, which the exercise treats as raising the required unlevered return by 1.0 percentage point); federal leases and permits open after the 2025 actions (qualitative). Identify the three largest open exposures and propose locks (answer: base unlevered pre-tax IRR 5.76%; turbines +14% lowers it to 5.34% (−0.43 points), T&I +20% to 5.56% (−0.21 points), cables +8% to 5.69% (−0.08 points), all three together to 5.08%; restoring a return 1.0 point higher at base capex needs a first-year price of USD 112.32/MWh, 8.4% above the award; the three largest are rates, turbines and T&I; locks: a pre-hedge or deal-contingent swap, a fixed turbine price or a steel hedge before FID, and a fixed T&I price with a capped standby rate; the political item cannot be locked and is priced through equity commitments and an exit plan).
- 71.11 Case problem: a Dogger Bank-style phase (Illustrative; fictional Silverpit Wind Phase C Ltd, 2022). Inputs: generation capex GBP 3,412.7 million; transmission capex GBP 618.4 million; forecast OFTO transfer value GBP 652.9 million; headline financing GBP 2,694.8 million senior plus GBP 386.5 million of ancillary facilities (letters of credit and a DSRA facility, not funding capex); the transmission bridge is sized at 90% of forecast OFTO proceeds. Reconcile the headline with capex and two-part gearing (answer: transmission bridge GBP 587.6 million; generation debt GBP 2,107.2 million, 61.7% of generation capex; senior debt is 66.9% of total capex; headline senior plus ancillary over total capex is 76.4%, which overstates gearing because the ancillary facilities do not fund capex and the transmission debt is a separate, OFTO-backed credit).
- 71.12 Case R problem: write the half-page IC memo Lattimer's committee would have read before declining the 2025 offshore stake, using only Framework 71.1 and Case Bible 3.2 fund terms.

## 71.15 Glossary entries owned

| Term | Abbreviation | Definition | Home section |
|---|---|---|---|
| array cable | – | A medium-voltage cable linking offshore wind turbines to each other and to the offshore substation. | 71.2.1 |
| export cable | – | The high-voltage cable carrying power from an offshore substation to the onshore grid connection. | 71.2.1 |
| offshore substation | – | A platform that collects array-cable output and steps up its voltage for export to shore. | 71.2.1 |
| offshore transmission owner | OFTO | In Great Britain, the licensed owner to which an offshore wind farm's transmission assets must be sold after construction, earning a regulated revenue. | 71.7.1 |
| offshore renewable energy certificate | OREC | A certificate representing one MWh of offshore wind output, bought by a US state program at a contracted price. | 71.3.2 |
| transport and installation contract | T&I contract | The contract under which a marine contractor transports and installs turbines, foundations or cables with specialized vessels. | 71.6.1 |
| weather window | – | A period in which sea and wind conditions allow a given marine operation to proceed safely. | 71.4.1 |
| marine warranty surveyor | MWS | An independent surveyor appointed for insurers who approves marine operations before they proceed. | 71.6.2 |
| pre-FID exposure clock | – | Framework 71.1: a map of the windows in which a developer's revenue price is fixed but costs, rates or tax benefits remain open. | 71.5.1 |

## 71.16 Neighbors

Chapter 70 (Onshore wind and solar) teaches plants with no fuel bill whose revenue depends on resource, capture price, curtailment and policy, using the resource-to-revenue bridge, the dated US DSCR ladder, and the Chile, REIPPPP and Lake Turkana cases. Chapter 72 (Hydropower and geothermal) turns to the two renewable technologies whose risk lies in the ground and the water: long-lived, civil-heavy hydro plants with hydrology and geology risk, financed by DFIs on long tenors; and geothermal plants whose resource is proven only by drilling.

# Chapter 72. Hydropower and geothermal

## 72.0 Rulings applied

- R-027: run-of-river plant is homed in Chapter 11 (ssec:11.5.2); Chapter 72 uses it without bold and drops it from its glossary.
- R-102 (CR-u15-2): pumped storage and long-duration storage are taught in Chapter 73 (sec:73.1, sec:73.3); Chapter 72 is cited there for civil and geological risk.
- R-001: LCOE cited from sec:11.8.
- R-101, R-105, R-113: Part XVI ownership; Case Bible Part 6 row as amended by Annex P 2.2 (characters Abdoulaye as minister and Prosper Adikpéto; figures add P-F58) and Annex P 4.11; architecture title.

## 72.1 Header

- Chapter: 72
- Title: Hydropower and geothermal
- File: `chapters/72-hydropower-and-geothermal.tex`
- Label: `ch:72`
- Target length: 11,000 to 13,000 words (raised in round 1: about 7,000 on hydropower and 4,000 on geothermal; the target is a floor for the core teaching of each sector, not a cap)
- Dominant expert lens: the independent engineer (hydrology, geology, reservoir engineering) paired with the DFI specialist who has structured long-tenor, multi-source financings in frontier markets. The government lens is strong: water rights, resettlement and the state's role as offtaker and guarantor.

## 72.2 Purpose and capability statement

Purpose. Hydropower and geothermal are the renewable technologies whose risks sit underground or upstream: the river's flow over decades, the rock under a dam or tunnel, the heat and fluid in a reservoir no one can see until it is drilled. Both are long-lived and capital-heavy, both are often built in frontier markets with DFI money, and both have produced landmark financings (Nam Theun 2, Bujagali, Sarulla) whose structures exist mainly to decide who carries hydrology, geology and resource risk. This chapter teaches the economics, the revenue structures that allocate those risks, the construction contracts that price ground risk, the financing terms that long lives allow, and the modeling that turns flow records and reservoir data into bankable cash flow.

What you will be able to do:
- Allocate hydrology, geological and geothermal resource risk between project, offtaker, contractor and state, and quantify the effect on debt service cover (Capability 3, Capability 13).
- Structure the financing of a long-lived hydro or geothermal project to match tenor to asset life and resource confidence (Capability 5, Capability 8).
- Read a hydro or geothermal resource and construction risk package and challenge it (Capability 7).
- Decide, as a DFI or lender, which party should hold resource risk at each stage of development (Capability 15).

## 72.3 Concepts owned and concepts assumed

### Owned

1. Hydropower industry economics: capex dominance, very long life, low opex, site-specificity, civil works share, why hydro tariffs are front-loaded by debt service and fall after repayment.
2. Hydro plant types as financial profiles: run-of-river, storage (reservoir), cascade, trans-basin diversion; firm and secondary energy; export hydro selling across a border. (Pumped storage is placed in Chapter 73 as storage.)
3. Hydrology risk and its allocation: energy-based tariffs, capacity-based tariffs, tiered tariffs (primary, secondary and excess energy with take-or-pay), deemed energy for spill caused by the buyer, and the hydrology allocation test (Framework 72.1, original).
4. Geological and geotechnical risk in hydro construction: the "fixed-price" EPC that leaves ground risk with the owner, baselines, cost pass-through caps (Bujagali's 70% capped pass-through), contingent financing (NT2's USD 200 million).
5. Water rights, transboundary rivers, dam safety and sediment as financing issues; climate change as a hydrology-distribution shift.
6. E&S as a hydro schedule and cost item (standards and resettlement owned by Chapter 50; this chapter treats their financial consequences).
7. Hydro financing structures and terms: DFI-led multi-source packages, political risk cover, currency-matched tranches (NT2's USD and THB), tenor extension by refinancing (Bujagali 2018).
8. Geothermal economics: exploration, confirmation drilling, field development, power plant; the resource-risk curve and the matching of capital types to stages (Framework 72.2, original).
9. Geothermal resource risk: drilling success rates, well productivity, reservoir decline, make-up drilling, reinjection; contract structures that split steam field and power plant (joint operation contracts, steam sales).
10. Geothermal financing: Sarulla's JBIC and ADB package with a sovereign guarantee and contingent equity; public exploration-risk instruments compared by design (up-front grant, contingent grant with a success fee, loan with grant conversion) through three verified facilities in `geothermal-risk-facilities`: GRMF in East Africa, Turkey's Risk Sharing Mechanism and Indonesia's GREM.
11. Modeling specifics: hydrology series and flow-duration-based energy; reservoir operation; tiered-tariff revenue; geothermal well count, success rate, decline and make-up schedule (cross-reference Chapter 45 for the P-value method).
12. Sector clauses: Clause 72.1 (unforeseen ground conditions in a hydro EPC) and Clause 72.2 (steam shortfall in a geothermal steam supply arrangement).

| Risk taxonomy; bankability ladder | Chapters 14, 15 |
| Concessions and government support | Chapter 17 |
| PPAs, capacity and energy payments, deemed energy, take-or-pay | Chapter 18 |
| EPC contract; LDs | Chapter 22 |
| Multi-contract structures; civil and E&M package split and interface matrix for run-of-river hydro | Chapter 23 |
| Sponsor support; contingent equity | Chapters 26, 32 |
| Lenders: DFIs, ECAs, A/B loans, OECD Arrangement and CCSU | Chapter 29 |
| Blended finance, partial risk guarantees, climate funds | Chapter 34 |
| Sizing, sculpting, tenor | Chapter 36 |
| Resource and reserves diligence | Chapter 48 |
