## 72.4 Locked table of contents

Opening (no heading). See 72.5.

`\section*{What you will be able to do}`.

**72.1 The economics of hydropower** (`sec:72.1`)
- 72.1.1 Capital now, energy for a century (`ssec:72.1.1`). Cost structure; long life; front-loaded revenue requirement; Example 72.2.
- 72.1.2 Plant types as cash-flow profiles (`ssec:72.1.2`). Run-of-river, storage, cascade, trans-basin diversion; firm, secondary and excess energy; export hydro (NT2: about 95% of output exported to Thailand, `nam-theun-2`). Exhibit 72.1 (hydro plant types and their cash-flow profiles).

**72.2 Hydrology risk and who carries it** (`sec:72.2`)
- 72.2.1 Energy tariffs, capacity tariffs, and tiers (`ssec:72.2.1`). Example 72.1; NT2's 95% take-or-pay with primary, secondary and excess tiers, 50% USD and 50% THB; Bujagali's availability-based capacity payments leaving hydrology with UETCL (`bujagali`).
- 72.2.2 The hydrology allocation test (`ssec:72.2.2`). Framework 72.1; Exhibit 72.2 (hydrology allocation under three tariff forms, from Example 72.1's numbers).
- 72.2.3 Droughts, climate, and cascades (`ssec:72.2.3`). A dry year in a cascade hits every plant at once; upstream regulation; climate change shifts the distribution, not just the variance (one-sentence forward reference to Chapter 84).

**72.3 Ground risk and the hydro construction contract** (`sec:72.3`)
- 72.3.1 A fixed price that does not fix ground risk (`ssec:72.3.1`). Bujagali's EPC: bid USD 467 million, negotiated USD 557 million, final USD 616 million; capped 70% cost pass-through to the offtaker; NT2's turnkey price-capped EPC with EDF and USD 200 million contingent financing; NT2's karst-geology episode (`nam-theun-2`, `bujagali`).
- 72.3.2 Baselines, sharing, and contingency (`ssec:72.3.2`). How a geotechnical baseline divides foreseen from unforeseen conditions; Clause 72.1 variants a (sponsor-friendly), b (lender-friendly) and c (contractor-friendly), set in the civil works contract of a 2025 run-of-river plant in Nepal with fictional parties, with an indicative landing paragraph. One sentence points forward to Snowy 2.0 in ssec:73.3.3 as the pumped-storage case of geology overwhelming a wrapped contract.
- 72.3.3 E&S on the critical path (`ssec:72.3.3`). Resettlement timetables tied to impoundment (NT2 moved 600 families early to temporary housing; E&S cost about 1.6 to 1.8 times appraisal; `nam-theun-2`); Bujagali's Inspection Panel findings and the failed first attempt (`bujagali`). Standards themselves stay in Chapter 50.

**72.4 Financing hydropower** (`sec:72.4`)
- 72.4.1 DFI-led packages and political risk cover (`ssec:72.4.1`). NT2: 26 financiers, IDA PRG up to USD 50 million, ADB political risk guarantee up to USD 50 million, MIGA; about USD 186 million of debt covered by IDA, ADB and MIGA guarantees (`nam-theun-2`). Bujagali: USD 798 million appraisal plan, about 78% debt, almost all DFI, USD 115 million of commercial bank debt covered by an IDA PRG, MIGA cover of USD 120.3 million on equity (`bujagali`).
- 72.4.2 Currency matching (`ssec:72.4.2`). NT2's debt mix matched to a USD/THB tariff.
- 72.4.3 Tenor, refinancing, and the long-life mismatch (`ssec:72.4.3`). Bujagali's senior loans 16 years door to door; the 2018 refinancing extended DFI tenor by 10 years to cut the tariff (`bujagali`). ECA terms: CCSU sectors listed in Appendix I carry up to 22 years, some sub-types 18 years (`t-oecd-arrangement`); the writer checks the hydro entry in Appendix I before stating a hydro-specific term. Example 72.2.
- 72.4.4 Hydro tenor and cover ranges and their drivers (`ssec:72.4.4`). No verified 2023 to 2026 hydro norms exist in the fact sheets (`t-market-norms`, `t-market-norms-2`); the section teaches the drivers and prints any range only as indicative under D-011 (market and period stated). Verified capacity factors from `t-power-tech-norms` frame site-specificity: EIA reports US geothermal at 66% and hydro at 35%. Exhibit 72.3 (hydro financings compared: NT2, Bujagali; amounts, lenders, tenor where verified, guarantees, outcome).

**72.5 Geothermal power** (`sec:72.5`)
- 72.5.1 From exploration to power plant (`ssec:72.5.1`). Stages: surface exploration, exploration drilling, confirmation drilling, field development, plant construction, operation with make-up drilling; the resource-risk curve; Exhibit 72.4 (pgfplots chart: cumulative spend against resource confidence by stage, Illustrative).
- 72.5.2 Drilling risk and public risk-sharing facilities (`ssec:72.5.2`). Example 72.3. Then three verified facility designs from `geothermal-risk-facilities`: GRMF (approximately USD 60 million from Germany's BMZ and the EU-Africa Infrastructure Trust Fund through KfW, run by the African Union Commission for 15 East African countries; non-reimbursable grants of 80% of surface study costs, 40% of exploration drilling and testing costs and 20% of infrastructure costs, plus a capped Continuation Premium; the 8th POWER round opened January 22, 2025); Turkey's Risk Sharing Mechanism (a USD 39.8 million CTF contingent recovery grant administered by TKYB that paid 60% of acceptable well costs when both Round 1 projects failed, USD 5.1 million in total, against a success fee cut in February 2021 from 10% to 5% of eligible drilling spend for the first three wells; alongside USD 550 million of IBRD credit lines that had mobilized USD 631 million of private capital by December 2024, while the RSM itself had confirmed zero MW); and Indonesia's GREM (a USD 455 million facility approved by the World Bank in September 2019 and run by PT SMI, lending for exploration with possible grant conversion, which by October 2024 had approved no sub-projects and disbursed none of its USD 150 million IBRD loan). Lesson: an up-front grant lowers cost but not outcome risk, a contingent grant works like insurance priced by the success fee, and a loan-based facility leaves the developer with debt unless forgiveness is triggered. Not stated: GRMF outcome totals, GREM's grant-conversion terms or CTF amounts, results after January 2025, or typical well costs and success rates (Do not state).
- 72.5.3 Reservoir decline and make-up wells (`ssec:72.5.3`). Example 72.4; reinjection and sustainability.
- 72.5.4 Contract structures for geothermal (`ssec:72.5.4`). Steam field and power plant under one owner or split; Sarulla's 30-year joint operation contract with Pertamina Geothermal Energy and 30-year energy sales contract with PLN (`sarulla`); the split model from `kenya-steam-sales` (delivered, D-127; placed in round 2): at Menengai the state's Geothermal Development Company carries exploration and drilling risk and sells steam under 25-year Project Implementation and Steam Supply Agreements to three 35 MW IPPs, which sell power to Kenya Power under 25-year PPAs; the tariff splits into a generation component capped at USD 0.05/kWh and a steam component of USD 0.02/kWh that Kenya Power pays through to GDC; contrast Olkaria III, where the private developer developed the field itself; Clause 72.2 (steam shortfall).
- 72.5.5 Financing geothermal (`ssec:72.5.5`). Framework 72.2; Sarulla's approximately USD 1.17 billion package (JBIC direct USD 492 million; JBIC-guaranteed commercial USD 329 million; ADB USD 250 million; CTF USD 80 million; Canadian Climate Fund USD 20 million), about 4 years of construction plus a 16-year term, up to USD 0.96 billion swapped at 3.4565%, a 20-year Ministry of Finance guarantee, an equity support deed (`sarulla`). How the facilities of ssec:72.5.2 hand projects on to senior debt: Turkey's RSM sits beside IBRD credit lines for later-stage drilling and plants, and Turkey's feed-in tariff history (USD 105/MWh for 10 years for plants commissioned 2011 to June 2021; about USD 72/MWh in lira from June 2021 to April 2023; about USD 102/MWh for 15 years from May 2023) shows the World Bank linking the low-tariff period to a pause in the pipeline (`geothermal-risk-facilities`). Kenya's steam-sales model (`kenya-steam-sales`, round 2): the African Development Fund backed a partial risk guarantee of approximately USD 11.27 million, through a standby letter of credit for 15 years, that covers both Kenya Power's non-payment and GDC's failure to deliver steam, beside Climate Investment Funds concessional loans and DFI senior debt; Globeleq's Menengai II (USD 117 million; AfDB, Finnfund and TDB as senior lenders) reached financial close in January 2024, about ten years after the first PISSAs, which shows how long the two-sided security package took to negotiate.

**72.6 Modeling hydro and geothermal** (`sec:72.6`)
- 72.6.1 Hydrology series to energy (`ssec:72.6.1`). Flow records, flow-duration curves, synthetic extension, P-values from long series (method in Chapter 45), reservoir operation rules.
- 72.6.2 Tiered tariffs in the model (`ssec:72.6.2`). Primary, secondary and excess energy; carry-forward of shortfalls where contracts allow.
- 72.6.3 Geothermal wells and decline (`ssec:72.6.3`). Steam supply as a function of wells online, decline, make-up schedule and capex; Example 72.4 as the build.

**72.7 Nam Theun 2, Bujagali, and Sarulla** (`sec:72.7`). Three short real-case sections with transferable lessons, each cross-referencing Chapter 50 for E&S and Chapter 60 for political risk.
- 72.7.1 Nam Theun 2 and the export hydro template (`ssec:72.7.1`). 1,070 MW; financial close June 2005; COD April 2010 with a 4.5-month delay and an overrun well inside the USD 200 million contingency; above contracted volumes; guarantees never triggered (`nam-theun-2`).
- 72.7.2 Bujagali and the second attempt (`ssec:72.7.2`). First attempt collapsed 2003; financial close December 21, 2007; COD August 1, 2012 about 13.6 months late; final cost about USD 891.5 million (12% over appraisal); 2018 refinancing (`bujagali`).
- 72.7.3 Sarulla and resource risk after COD (`ssec:72.7.3`). Units COD March 2017 to May 2018; drilling overran in time and cost; generation fell from wellfield problems at one plant and equipment failures; 2022 remediation framework with lenders; make-up drilling planned from 2026 (`sarulla`; no unit named, no percentages).

**72.8 Walkthrough: reading a hydrology report for a run-of-river plant** (`sec:72.8`). See 72.7.

**72.9 Case P: a drought on the Moraba** (`sec:72.9`). See 72.10.

**72.10 Practitioner's notebook** (`sec:72.10`). **72.11 Judgment drill** (`sec:72.11`).

**72.12 Underground and upstream risk must sit with someone who can carry it** (`sec:72.12`). Close; open problem for Chapter 73.

**72.13 Exercises** (`sec:72.13`). **72.14 Solutions to exercises** (`sec:72.14`). `\section*{Sources}`.

## 72.5 Opening

Real deal moment from `nam-theun-2` (Chapter 50 owns the E&S story; this opening uses the financing angle). June 2005: 26 financiers sign for a 1,070 MW plant on the Nakai Plateau in Laos, a country whose own credit could not support the debt, selling about 95% of its power to Thailand; dollar lenders would not lend without multilateral political risk cover. Two to three paragraphs: the plant would earn half its tariff in dollars and half in baht, from a river whose flow no one controls, through a turnkey contract that capped the price and a USD 200 million contingency for what the ground might hold. Close with the chapter's question: when the risk is the river and the rock, who in this structure was holding it?

## 72.6 Worked examples

**Example 72.1. Who carries the dry year on a Nepali river** (`ex:72.1`, Illustrative; Section 72.2.1). Setting: Tamor Khola Hydro Ltd (fictional), a 250 MW run-of-river plant in eastern Nepal negotiating a USD-denominated PPA with the national single buyer in 2024; the tariffs and flows are the example's. The plant produces 1,192 GWh in a dry year (probability 25%), 1,418 GWh in an average year (50%) and 1,605 GWh in a wet year (25%); expected energy 1,408.25 GWh. Opex USD 21.9 million; annual debt service USD 58.4 million. Structure A, energy tariff of USD 84.60/MWh: revenue dry USD 100.84 million, average 119.96 million, wet 135.78 million; DSCR dry 1.35x, average 1.68x, wet 1.95x. Structure B, capacity payment set to the same expected revenue (USD 119.14 million a year, paid if available): DSCR 1.67x in every year. Structure C (Exercise 72.6), 60% capacity and 40% energy: dry-year revenue USD 111.82 million, DSCR 1.54x. Lesson: the tariff form decides who bears hydrology; under B the offtaker pays for water it did not receive, which it accepts only if it values firm capacity or is backed by the state.

**Example 72.2. What tenor does to a hydro tariff in Cameroon** (`ex:72.2`, Illustrative; Section 72.4.3). Setting: Sanaga Aval Hydro SA (fictional), a 250 MW plant on the Sanaga basin in Cameroon, whose DFI lenders compare tenors in 2025; the costs are the example's. The plant costs USD 862.4 million, 75% debt at 7.2%, 25% equity with a 14% required return recovered as a 30-year annuity (USD 30.79 million a year), opex USD 19.6 million, average energy 1,418 GWh. With 16-year annuity debt, debt service is USD 69.38 million and the revenue requirement during the debt life USD 119.77 million (USD 84.46/MWh). With 25-year debt, debt service USD 56.51 million and the requirement USD 106.89 million (USD 75.38/MWh), 10.8% lower. With 20-year debt (Exercise 72.7): USD 112.39 million (USD 79.26/MWh). Lesson: a long-lived asset financed short pays for its youth; tenor extension is how Bujagali's 2018 refinancing cut the tariff (`bujagali`). The writer states that the equity annuity is a simplification for comparison.

**Example 72.3. How many wells to prove the steam in Kenya's Rift Valley** (`ex:72.3`, Illustrative; Section 72.5.2). Setting: Suswa Geothermal Ltd (fictional), a 110 MW development in Kenya's Rift Valley planning its drilling budget in 2024; the success rate and well cost are the example's assumptions, not market norms (`geothermal-risk-facilities` verifies none). The development needs 14 successful production wells. The development-phase success rate is 68%; each well costs USD 7.85 million. Expected wells 14 / 0.68 = 20.59 (expected cost USD 161.6 million). Under a negative binomial model, 20 wells give a 53.1% chance of reaching 14 successes (P50 cost USD 157.0 million); 24 wells give 89.0%; 25 wells give 93.0%, so the P90 count is 25 wells (USD 196.2 million). At a 60% success rate (Exercise 72.8): expected 23.33 wells (USD 183.2 million), P90 29 wells (USD 227.6 million). Lesson: drilling contingency must be sized to the P90 well count, and a few points of success rate move it by tens of millions; this is why early drilling is financed with equity, grants or risk-sharing facilities rather than senior debt.

**Example 72.4. Make-up wells to hold output in North Sumatra** (`ex:72.4`, Illustrative; Section 72.5.3). Setting: Toba Highlands Geothermal (fictional), a 110 MW plant in North Sumatra, Indonesia, planning make-up drilling from 2026 with its lenders' consent. The plant's steam supply declines 4.0% a year without new wells; each make-up well adds 7.5 MW of steam capacity at USD 8.2 million; the operator drills whenever capacity falls below 95% of nameplate (104.5 MW). Over ten years the rule triggers six wells (years 2, 4, 5, 7, 9 and 10) costing USD 49.2 million. The writer prints the full year-by-year table (computed: year 1 105.6 MW, no well; year 2 108.9 with one well; year 3 104.52, no well; year 4 107.8, one; year 5 111.0, one; year 6 106.6, none; year 7 109.8, one; year 8 105.4, none; year 9 108.7, one; year 10 111.9, one; the 4.0% decline is applied to total steam capacity including earlier make-up wells, a simplification the writer states). Lesson: make-up drilling is a recurring capex line that the model, the MMRA-style reserve and the lenders' covenant package must carry (Sarulla's 2022 remediation framework shows what happens when it is under-provided).

## 72.7 Walkthrough

**Walkthrough: reading a hydrology report for a run-of-river plant** (`sec:72.8`). The artifact is an Illustrative summary of a hydrology consultant's report on Example 72.1's Tamor Khola plant in Nepal, 2024 (Exhibit 72.5: gauge record length and gaps, correlation with a long reference station, synthetic extension method, flow-duration curve, compensation-flow deduction, plant flow range, annual energy series, P50, P90 one-year and ten-year, climate sensitivity). Steps:
1. Check the record: length, gaps, station location relative to the intake, rating-curve changes.
2. Check the extension: correlation with the reference station, period overlap, any regime shift (upstream dams, land use).
3. Check deductions: compensation and environmental flows, upstream abstractions, sediment flushing downtime.
4. Convert flows to energy through the plant's flow range and efficiency curve; check spill at high flows.
5. Read the P-values and the dry-sequence analysis (three dry years in a row), not only single-year P90.
6. Read the climate sensitivity and ask what distribution shift it assumes.
7. Map the result to the tariff structure (Framework 72.1) and to DSRA sizing (cross-reference Chapter 37).
8. Write the finding: the dry sequence the debt can survive and the reserve or tariff change needed if it cannot.

## 72.8 Frameworks

**Framework 72.1: Hydrology allocation test** (`fw:hydrology-allocation`; original). Purpose: decide whether a hydro plant's tariff and reserves leave hydrology risk with the party able to bear it. Steps: (1) obtain the annual energy distribution and the worst plausible dry sequence; (2) compute DSCR in the dry year and the dry sequence under the proposed tariff; (3) identify who bears the shortfall (project, offtaker, state) under the tariff form; (4) test that party's capacity to bear it (offtaker credit, state budget, sponsor support); (5) choose among tariff form (energy, capacity, tiered), DSRA size, a hydrology reserve or a sponsor liquidity line; (6) record the residual risk to debt. When to use: at tariff negotiation and lender sizing for any hydro project. Demonstrated on Example 72.1 and the Case P installment.

**Framework 72.2: Geothermal resource staging** (`fw:geothermal-staging`; original). Purpose: match each development stage to the capital that can bear its risk. Steps: (1) place the project on the resource-confidence curve (surface work, exploration wells, confirmation wells, proven reserve); (2) estimate the success rate and well count to the next stage (Example 72.3); (3) assign capital: grants and public risk-sharing for exploration, sponsor equity for confirmation, senior debt only after resource certification; (4) set lender conditions for entry (resource report, number of tested wells, reinjection plan); (5) carry make-up drilling into the operating budget and reserves (Example 72.4). When to use: from first exploration to financial close. Demonstrated on Sarulla.

## 72.9 Real cases

| Slug | Use | Transferable lesson |
|---|---|---|
| `nam-theun-2` | Opening; Sections 72.1.2, 72.2.1, 72.3.1, 72.4.1, 72.4.2, 72.7.1 | Tiered take-or-pay converts hydrology risk into a bankable revenue line; currency-matched debt; turnkey price-capped EPC plus contingent financing; geotechnical risk is the residual hydro risk lenders size contingency for. |
| `bujagali` | Sections 72.2.1, 72.3.1, 72.4.1, 72.4.3, 72.7.2 | Hydrology risk left with the offtaker through availability-based capacity payments; ground risk under a "fixed-price" EPC ends with the owner; a capped cost pass-through shares overruns with the offtaker; refinancing extends tenor for long-life assets. |
| `sarulla` | Sections 72.5.4, 72.5.5, 72.7.3 | Resource risk persists after COD; lenders keep tenor shorter than the sale contract and require contingent equity; make-up drilling and remediation need lender approval. |
| `t-oecd-arrangement` | Section 72.4.3 | CCSU terms up to 22 years for listed sectors (sub-types at 18 years). |
| `t-market-norms` | Section 72.4.4 | No verified 2023 to 2026 hydro or geothermal norms; teach the principle. |
| `geothermal-risk-facilities` | Sections 72.5.2, 72.5.5 | Public money buys down exploration risk, and the instrument matters: GRMF's up-front grants, Turkey's contingent grant with a success fee (paid out when both Round 1 projects failed) and Indonesia's loan-based GREM (no uptake after five years). |
| `t-power-tech-norms` | Section 72.4.4 | Geothermal and hydro capacity factors (66% and 35% in the US) show site-specificity; thin data makes estimates fragile. |
| `kenya-steam-sales` (delivered, D-127; placed in round 2) | Sections 72.5.4, 72.5.5 | Splitting the steam field from the plant lets a private IPP finance only the surface plant, but it creates a two-sided interface that lenders insure: the AfDF-backed partial risk guarantee covers both the power buyer's non-payment and the state steam seller's failure to deliver. |

Respect the `kenya-steam-sales` "Do not state" list: no KenGen steam sale to an operating private IPP at Olkaria, no Olkaria VI award or close, no commercial operation date for Menengai II or OrPower 22, no per-lender amounts or PRG drawings, and the 6.9 US cents per kWh is not the contract tariff.

## 72.10 Running-case installment

**Case P: a drought on the Moraba** (`sec:72.9`). Case Bible Part 6, Chapter 72 row as amended by Annex P 2.2: Case P (small), 2022; characters Abdoulaye Ndao-Sylla (as minister) and Prosper Adikpéto (shift supervisor, SEKA national control center); inputs: hydro 640 MW, Annex P 4.11; figure P-F58.

Short installment (500 to 700 words). First sentence: in 2022, the first full year after Bélanou's December 1, 2021 COD. Scene (`casescene`, "SEKA national control center, Dabakro, April 2022"). Prosper Adikpéto shows the minister the dry-season dispatch report: the Moraba cascade (640 MW of Kessara's 2015 mix) is running at about 55% of its average output (Annex P 4.11), Bélanou is dispatched harder to fill the gap, and Abdoulaye wants MEH's planned Moraba Falls project fast-tracked; he translates megawatts into towns (verbal habit, once). Prosper's question is operational (what happens to the evening peak if Bélanou trips); no other new character speaks.

Narration outside the box: from Case Bible ledger: P-F58, Bélanou's dispatch factor when available was 84.0% in 2022 H1 and 81.5% in 2022 H2 against the 76.5% base, with gas burn of 13.53 and 13.11 million MMBtu against 12.32 and 12.30 million at 76.5%, and a fuel charge of USD 85.7 million and USD 82.9 million against USD 78.0 million and USD 77.9 million (Exhibit 72.6, `\casep`). The reading: SEKA's fuel bill in KCR rose in the very half-years its arrears began, while the capacity payment, and so Bélanou's debt service, was unaffected (pointer to Chapter 59 for SEKA's 2022 arrears, which the reader has already seen). Then Framework 72.1 applied to Moraba Falls (Annex P 4.11: 240 MW run-of-river with seasonal storage, estimated cost USD 720 million, studies only): the drought shows that Kessara's hydro and the state buyer are exposed to the same dry years, so an energy-only tariff would leave a private Moraba Falls exposed exactly when SEKA most needs firm capacity, while a capacity tariff moves the drought onto SEKA's already strained cash; the expert answer is that a cascade hydro IPP in Kessara would need a tiered tariff, a hydrology reserve and DFI support. No Moraba Falls tariff or debt figure is invented.

Ledger figures displayed: P-F58. Inputs cited: Case Bible 1.1; Annex P 4.11.

## 72.11 Practitioner's notebook (outline)

Checklist: hydrology record length and extension method; dry-sequence analysis; compensation flows and upstream abstractions; transboundary water agreements; dam safety review and panel; geotechnical investigation scope and baseline report; EPC ground-risk allocation and contingency at P90; E&S plan tied to the construction and impoundment schedule (Chapter 50); tariff form and hydrology allocation (Framework 72.1); currency of tariff and debt; DFI and PRI cover; for geothermal: resource report, number and results of tested wells, success-rate basis, decline model, make-up drilling budget, reinjection plan, steam field and plant contract interface.

Red flags: a short gauge record extended without a long reference station; an energy-only tariff in a drought-prone cascade with no hydrology reserve; a "fixed-price" EPC with no geotechnical baseline; resettlement scheduled after impoundment works start; a geothermal financing before confirmation drilling; make-up drilling absent from the operating budget; a sale contract longer than the resource's demonstrated life.

Rules of thumb and their limits: long assets justify long tenor only if the offtake and currency match it; DFIs lead hydro in frontier markets because they can hold long tenor and E&S risk, not because commercial banks cannot price it; geothermal debt enters after the resource is proven.

Common mistakes: sizing on one-year P90 when dry years cluster; treating a cascade's plants as diversified; counting the contingency as covering ground risk the EPC price excludes; modeling geothermal output flat.

Questions experts ask: What is the worst three-year dry sequence in the record, and what is our DSCR in it? Who pays if the tunnel hits a fault zone? How many wells have been tested, and what was the success rate? Who owns the steam field, and what happens to the power plant if the steam falls short?

## 72.12 Judgment drill

The situation. You are the DFI investment officer leading a 180 MW run-of-river IPP in Zambia (Illustrative; fictional sponsor and state utility), in February 2026. The state utility, rated well below investment grade, insists on an energy-only tariff ("we pay for what we get"). The sponsor proposes a 65% capped pass-through of tunnel overruns to the tariff, modeled on the Bujagali precedent. The hydrology report has 14 years of record extended to 40 years with a reference station 120 km away. Your investment committee meets in ten days.

Reasoning: apply Framework 72.1; an energy-only tariff leaves hydrology with the project; with a weak offtaker, moving it to a capacity tariff would just swap hydrology risk for credit risk; the expert answer is a tiered tariff (capacity component sized to debt service in the P90 dry year, energy component above it), a hydrology reserve funded from wet years, a DSRA sized to the dry sequence, and a PRG or government guarantee behind the capacity component; on ground risk, a capped pass-through is acceptable only with a geotechnical baseline and an IE-approved contingency, because pass-through cost lands on a utility that cannot pay; require extended hydrology work before financial close.

What would change the answer: a longer local record (less uncertainty, smaller reserve); an investment-grade offtaker or export buyer (capacity tariff acceptable); a contractor willing to take ground risk against a baseline at a price the tariff can bear; climate projections showing a drying trend (bigger reserve or lower debt).

## 72.13 Exercise set (14 exercises)

Tier 1 (4):
- 72.1 Explain why a hydro plant's revenue requirement falls sharply after debt repayment.
- 72.2 Compare energy, capacity and tiered tariffs by who bears a dry year.
- 72.3 Explain why geothermal exploration drilling is rarely financed with senior debt.
- 72.4 Explain how NT2's tariff currency mix related to its debt currency mix.

Tier 2 (6):
- 72.5 Compute Example 72.1's DSCR in a dry year if debt service were USD 66.2 million (answer: energy tariff 1.19x; capacity 1.47x).
- 72.6 Compute the dry-year DSCR under a 60% capacity, 40% energy tariff (answer: 1.54x).
- 72.7 Recompute Example 72.2 with 20-year debt (answer: USD 112.39 million; USD 79.26/MWh).
- 72.8 Recompute Example 72.3 at a 60% success rate (answer: expected 23.33 wells, USD 183.2 million; P90 29 wells, USD 227.6 million).
- 72.9 Draft Clause 72.1 (unforeseen ground conditions against a geotechnical baseline) for a hydro EPC; annotate.
- 72.10 Extend Example 72.4 to a 3.0% decline rate and recompute the wells and cost over ten years (answer: four wells, in years 2, 4, 7 and 9; USD 32.8 million).

Tier 3 (4):
- 72.11 Model task: build a hydrology-to-revenue sheet for Example 72.1's plant with this 40-year synthetic annual energy series (GWh, years 1 to 40, Illustrative): 1,541.9; 1,535.3; 1,565.2; 1,478.0; 1,349.0; 1,359.7; 1,381.6; 1,245.3; 1,111.3; 1,116.4; 1,254.8; 1,254.3; 1,237.9; 1,436.9; 1,400.5; 1,341.9; 1,424.4; 1,316.4; 1,378.6; 1,355.7; 1,381.9; 1,419.5; 1,350.3; 1,426.9; 1,380.4; 1,460.7; 1,524.4; 1,348.5; 1,315.8; 1,469.9; 1,539.4; 1,698.3; 1,655.2; 1,449.7; 1,577.6; 1,699.1; 1,551.7; 1,512.0; 1,372.9; 1,446.9 (mean 1,416.7). Tiered tariff: a capacity component of USD 58.15 million a year plus USD 41.30/MWh on all energy; opex USD 21.9 million; debt service USD 58.4 million. Compute DSCR by year and the worst three-year average (answer: lowest single-year DSCR 1.41x in year 9; worst three-year average 1.44x, years 8 to 10; mean 1.62x; the dry sequence, not the single dry year, sets the DSRA).
- 72.12 Case problem: a Bujagali-type overrun (Illustrative; a fictional 250 MW plant in Uganda, 2024). Inputs: EPC price USD 563.7 million; overrun 32%; 70% of the overrun passes to the tariff base under the capped pass-through; the pass-through is recovered as a level annuity at 9.4% over 25 years on 1,245 GWh a year. Compute the tariff effect and explain why the offtaker and lenders accepted it (answer: overrun USD 180.4 million; USD 126.3 million passed through and USD 54.1 million left with the sponsor and contractor; USD 13.27 million a year, USD 10.66/MWh).
- 72.13 Case problem: a Sarulla-type decline after COD (Illustrative; a fictional 330 MW three-unit plant in Indonesia, 2026). Inputs: steam capacity falls 6.0% a year from 330 MW; the operator must hold at least 95% of nameplate (313.5 MW) at each year end; each make-up well adds 9.0 MW at USD 8.9 million. Compute the wells and cost over five years and propose the lender-approved remediation framework (make-up wells, reserve, sweep, sponsor support) (answer: one well in year 1 and two in each of years 2 to 5, nine wells costing USD 80.1 million; a make-up reserve holding the next 12 months' drilling, two wells or USD 17.8 million, funded ahead of distributions, a cash sweep while the reserve is short, and sponsor support for drilling beyond the plan).
- 72.14 Case P: write the memo Abdoulaye's ministry would need before procuring Moraba Falls as an IPP, applying Framework 72.1 and stating which Kessaran institutions bear which risk (no invented figures).

Check for 72.5: (100.84 − 21.9) / 66.2 = 1.19x and (119.14 − 21.9) / 66.2 = 1.47x.

## 72.15 Glossary entries owned

| Term | Abbreviation | Definition | Home section |
|---|---|---|---|
| storage hydro plant | – | A hydro plant with a reservoir that stores water to generate when it is most valuable. | 72.1.2 |
| trans-basin diversion | – | A hydro scheme that diverts water from one river basin to another to gain head and generate. | 72.1.2 |
| firm energy | – | The energy a hydro plant can deliver with high reliability in dry conditions, often contracted as primary energy. | 72.1.2 |
| secondary energy | – | Energy above firm energy, available in average or wet years and sold at a lower or contingent price. | 72.1.2 |
| hydrology risk | – | The risk that river flows, and so a hydro plant's output, fall below forecast. | 72.2.1 |
| geotechnical baseline | – | A contractual description of expected ground conditions against which unforeseen conditions are measured for time and cost relief. | 72.3.2 |
| confirmation drilling | – | Drilling after exploration that tests whether a geothermal reservoir can supply enough steam for a plant of a given size. | 72.5.1 |
| make-up well | – | A geothermal well drilled during operations to replace declining steam supply. | 72.5.3 |
| reinjection | – | Returning spent geothermal fluid to the reservoir to sustain pressure and dispose of brine. | 72.5.3 |
| joint operation contract | JOC | In Indonesian geothermal practice, the contract under which a private operator develops a concession holder's field and plant. | 72.5.4 |

Removed (homed elsewhere; used without bold and cross-referenced): run-of-river plant (ssec:11.5.2; R-027).

## 72.16 Neighbors

Chapter 71 (Offshore wind) teaches marine construction risk, multi-contract interfaces, two-part gearing with OFTO-bound transmission, ECA-supported mega-financings and the pre-FID exposure that cancelled Ocean Wind. Chapter 73 (Storage, transmission, and interconnectors) moves from generators to the assets that move and shift energy: batteries and pumped storage earning from tolls, capacity contracts and arbitrage; regulated, competitive and merchant transmission; and interconnectors financed under cap-and-floor regimes.

# Chapter 73. Storage, transmission, and interconnectors

## 73.0 Rulings applied

- R-023 (CR-u15-4): augmentation is defined in Chapter 11 (ssec:11.7.2) and modeled in Chapter 45 (sec:45.3); Chapter 73 owns the economic and contractual decision between overbuild and augmentation (Example 73.1, Clause 73.1) and drops "augmentation" from its glossary. Sequencing defect 30: the storage sense of "overbuild" is homed here (ssec:73.2.2).
- R-027: storage duration and thermal runaway are homed in Chapter 11 (ssec:11.7.1, ssec:11.7.3); Chapter 73 drops them from its glossary.
- R-102 (CR-u15-2): pumped storage hydro and long-duration storage are homed here (sec:73.1, sec:73.3) with the GB LDES cap and floor; Chapter 72 is cited for civil and geological risk and Chapter 19 (sec:19.6) for cap-and-floor mechanics.
- R-054: battery tolls are owned by Chapter 20 (sec:20.6).
- R-067 and R-114: "TSA" means only turbine supply agreement; a transmission service agreement is always spelled out.
- R-073: US credit rules for storage are cited from sec:67.10.
- R-116: Example 73.3 applies the canonical availability deduction (eq:58.2) as a specialized application and cites it.
- R-101, R-105, R-113: Part XVI ownership; Case Bible Part 6 row with Annex TR R.4 and R.5 (figures add R-F18 and R-F19); architecture title with serial comma. Standards defect 1: the combined "Case R and Case P:" heading becomes "Case R: battery tolls and floors", with Case P's 225 kV line kept as narration inside the same section.

## 73.1 Header

- Chapter: 73
- Title: Storage, transmission, and interconnectors
- File: `chapters/73-storage-transmission-and-interconnectors.tex`
- Label: `ch:73`
- Target length: 11,000 to 13,000 words (raised in round 1: about 5,500 on storage and 6,000 on transmission and interconnectors; the target is a floor for the core teaching of each sector, not a cap)
- Dominant expert lens: the market advisor (battery revenue stacks, price spreads, interconnector flows) paired with the regulator and government official who designs transmission revenue regimes. The lender lens enters through revenue-quality sizing; the contractor lens through battery integrators, cell suppliers and HVDC vendors.

## 73.2 Purpose and capability statement

Purpose. Batteries, pumped storage, transmission lines and interconnectors do not make energy; they move it in time or space and earn from the price difference or from a regulated or contracted payment for being available to do so. That makes their finance a pure exercise in revenue design: the same 200 MW battery can be financed like a contracted utility asset or like a merchant trader depending on whether it holds a toll, a capacity contract, a floor or nothing; the same transmission line can be a regulated rate base, a competitively bid availability contract or a merchant bet on price spreads. This chapter teaches the economics and technology risks of storage, the revenue models of transmission and interconnectors, and how lenders size and protect each, with Moss Landing as the storage casualty case and Chile's Kimal–Lo Aguirre and the GB cap-and-floor regimes as the transmission references.

What you will be able to do:
- Value and finance a battery under a toll, a floor or merchant revenue, including augmentation and safety risk (Capability 5, Capability 13).
- Explain the revenue models for transmission and interconnectors (regulated, competitive availability-based, OFTO, merchant, cap and floor) and the risks each leaves with lenders (Capability 3, Capability 13).
- Read a battery or transmission project's contract set and diligence reports for technology, interface and counterparty gaps (Capability 7).
- Choose between contracted and merchant structures for a storage asset and defend the choice (Capability 12, Capability 15).

## 73.3 Concepts owned and concepts assumed

### Owned

1. Storage industry economics: power and energy ratings, duration, cycle life, cost per kWh and per kW, falling cell costs and their effect on augmentation decisions; why storage revenues fell as fleets grew (Case R's illustrative battery revenue path).
2. Battery technology risks for financiers: cell and integrator supply, warranties and capacity maintenance, degradation (modeling in Chapter 45), thermal runaway and fire propagation (thermal runaway defined in ssec:11.7.3), the safety standards stack a lender's engineer checks (NFPA 855, UL 9540, UL 9540A, from `t-storage-safety`), indoor versus containerized layouts (Moss Landing), decommissioning and clean-up.
3. Storage revenue models compared: tolls, capacity or resource-adequacy contracts, ancillary services, energy arbitrage, floors and collars, cap-and-floor for long-duration storage (mechanics of tolls and floors in Chapter 20, cap-and-floor model in Chapter 19); the storage revenue stack screen (Framework 73.1, original).
4. Sizing storage debt by revenue quality: contracted storage at the bottom of the DSCR ladder and merchant storage at the top (verified US ranges); bucket sizing for floor plus merchant revenue.
5. Augmentation versus overbuild as an economic and contractual decision under a capacity-maintenance obligation.
6. Pumped storage hydro and other long-duration storage as financing problems (long build, civil and geological risk from Chapter 72 as Snowy 2.0 shows, transmission dependence, cap-and-floor support under the GB LDES scheme).
7. Transmission revenue models: regulated cost of service and RAB; competitive tenders paying an annual availability-based value (Kimal–Lo Aguirre); offshore transmission sold to an OFTO; merchant lines; offtaker-built interconnection for IPPs; the transmission revenue model selector (Framework 73.2, original).
8. Availability regimes and deductions in transmission contracts.
9. Interconnectors: cross-border price spreads, regulatory approval on both sides, cap-and-floor regimes and their project financing (Greenlink, the first GB cap-and-floor interconnector financed through project finance, from `t-cap-and-floor`), HVDC converter technology risk, and cross-border political risk.
10. Transmission interface risk for generators (Lake Turkana, Chile, Case P's 225 kV connection), applied from Chapter 25.
11. Financing terms for storage (verified US) and for transmission and interconnectors (verified deal anchors; CCSU 22-year eligibility for transmission, distribution and storage; indicative ranges under D-011).
12. Sector clauses: Clause 73.1 (capacity maintenance in a battery toll) and Clause 73.2 (availability deduction in a transmission service agreement).

## 73.4 Locked table of contents

Opening (no heading). See 73.5.

`\section*{What you will be able to do}`.

**73.1 The economics of storage** (`sec:73.1`)
- 73.1.1 Power, energy, and duration in a storage project's contracts (`ssec:73.1.1`; retitled in round 2 so it does not repeat ssec:11.7.1). MW versus MWh, duration and round-trip efficiency (from ssec:11.7.1 and ssec:11.7.2), cycle limits; cost per kW and per kWh; why two-hour batteries dominated early markets and longer durations follow; Exhibit 73.1 (storage durations, costs, and revenue lines, Illustrative). Verified efficiency figures from `t-power-tech-norms`: EIA's 2019 US fleet round-trip efficiency of 82% against Lazard's 86% to 92% new-build range; no single annual degradation rate is stated (Do not state).
- 73.1.2 Revenues that shrink as fleets grow (`ssec:73.1.2`). Arbitrage and ancillary prices fall as storage capacity rises; Case R's illustrative path (USD 142.5/kW-year in 2023, 61.8 in 2024, 52.4 in 2025; Case Bible 3.5, labeled Illustrative) used to show the shape, not as market data (Exhibit 73.2). Verified market evidence of the same shape from `t-capacity-ancillary-markets`: ERCOT batteries exceeded 17,000 MW and 31,500 MWh by end-2025, ancillary-service costs fell to USD 0.39/MWh of load in 2025, and battery revenue per kW fell 37%; ERCOT introduced ECRS in June 2023 and went live with real-time co-optimization on December 5, 2025. Lesson: ancillary revenue is a shallow, quickly saturating pool that lenders should not treat as a long-term base case.

**73.2 Battery technology and safety risk** (`sec:73.2`)
- 73.2.1 Cells, integrators, and warranties (`ssec:73.2.1`). Cell supply agreement, integrator's EPC or supply-and-install, performance warranty, capacity maintenance agreement; supplier credit (cross-reference Chapter 23); prohibited-foreign-entity rules for US credits (cross-reference sec:67.10).
- 73.2.2 Degradation, augmentation, and overbuild (`ssec:73.2.2`). Example 73.1; Clause 73.1 variants a (sponsor-friendly), b (lender-friendly) and c (offtaker-friendly), set in a 2025 battery toll in California with fictional parties.
- 73.2.3 Fire, propagation, and clean-up (`ssec:73.2.3`). Moss Landing: January 16, 2025 fire in a 300 MW indoor facility of about 100,000 modules, about 55% damaged; whole site shut; write-off of approximately USD 400 million; approximately USD 155 million impairment of the 100 MW facility; clean-up estimate raised to approximately USD 175 million by June 2026; insurance limits USD 500 million net of deductibles, fully collected by February 2026; a second fire on September 18, 2026 (`moss-landing`; no root cause, no supplier fault, no MWh rating stated). The standards stack from `t-storage-safety`: NFPA 855 (installation standard; editions 2020, 2023 and 2026, the 2026 edition adding Annex G.11 on fire spread between BESS units; next edition 2029), UL 9540 (product listing) and UL 9540A (a fire test method run at cell, module, unit and installation level that produces data; it is not a pass/fail certification; its 6th edition assumes a developed fire after a deflagration at installation level); California's CPUC proposed in January 2025 to amend General Order 167 for BESS maintenance, operation and emergency plans (adoption not verified). Disclosed Moss Landing write-offs, impairments and clean-up already exceeded approximately USD 700 million against USD 500 million of combined limits. Lessons for a non-recourse lender: the edition of NFPA 855 the authority having jurisdiction adopted, UL 9540 listing and UL 9540A data in technical diligence, layout and spacing, maximum foreseeable loss across co-located units, remediation security, community and permitting risk after a fire. No NFPA clause thresholds, spacing distances, EPRI statistics or insurance pricing are stated (Do not state).

**73.3 Storage revenue models and how lenders size them** (`sec:73.3`)
- 73.3.1 The revenue stack (`ssec:73.3.1`). Tolls, capacity contracts, ancillary services, arbitrage, floors; Moss Landing's contracted-merchant model (fixed monthly RA capacity payments from PG&E plus retained arbitrage; PG&E's Chapter 11 required court and CPUC approval to keep the contract; `moss-landing`). Framework 73.1; Exhibit 73.3 (storage revenue stack and sizing buckets).
- 73.3.2 Sizing by revenue quality (`ssec:73.3.2`). Verified US ladder (`t-market-norms`): contracted storage 1.15x to 1.20x on P50 (2024 to 2026); merchant storage at least 2.0x; merchant batteries priced SOFR plus 275 to 350 bps (early 2025); a battery premium of about 25 bps over wind and solar in operations. Example 73.2.
- 73.3.3 Long-duration storage, pumped hydro, and Snowy 2.0 (`ssec:73.3.3`). GB LDES cap and floor from `t-cap-and-floor` (which supersedes the shorter `t-frontier-data` entry where they differ): a 25-year regime; a floor that recovers invested capital at a return comparable to the cost of debt and a soft cap with sharing; eligibility of at least 8 hours and 100 MW (stream 1) or 50 MW (stream 2); default returns of 4.47% at the floor and 7.48% at the cap, CPIH-real; Window 1 drew 171 applications, 77 were eligible and 73 were assessed; Ofgem's minded-to decision of June 26, 2026 provisionally selected 16 projects, 7,645 MW and about 136.9 GWh, with durations of 8 to 32 hours across pumped storage hydro, compressed air, lithium-ion and flow batteries; consultation closed August 14, 2026; final awards expected autumn 2026 (only minded-to positions are stated). Then the construction-risk landmark from `pumped-storage`: Snowy 2.0 (Snowy Hydro, wholly owned by the Commonwealth of Australia; 2,200 MW and 350 GWh, about 160 hours at full output, linking Tantangara and Talbingo through about 27 km of tunnels under a single wrapped EPC with the Future Generation joint venture). After bushfires, COVID-19, supply chain inflation, design issues and poor geology (the TBM "Florence" bogged in soft ground in 2022), the 2023 reset raised the estimated cost to AUD 12 billion, targeted full operation in December 2028 and moved the contract to an incentivized target-cost model; on October 3, 2025, at 67% complete, Snowy Hydro ordered a line-by-line cost reassessment. The feasibility-stage estimate of about AUD 3.8 to 4.5 billion (2017) is cited only as reported in secondary sources. The project is financed on Snowy Hydro's balance sheet (bank facilities of AUD 7.675 billion at June 2025, an AUD 4.5 billion shareholder construction loan of March 2025, Commonwealth equity), not as project finance, and its value depends on transmission others build (HumeLink; Sydney Ring South; VNI West revised to 2030). Lesson: what a lender would have needed (a geotechnical baseline, contingency, contract form, shareholder completion support) is the Chapter 72 ground-risk package applied to storage. No cost above AUD 12 billion, new completion date or ANAO finding is stated (Do not state).

**73.4 Transmission revenue models** (`sec:73.4`)
- 73.4.1 Regulated transmission (`ssec:73.4.1`). Cost of service and RAB from Chapter 19 applied to a transmission owner; what lenders lend against (the regulatory settlement).
- 73.4.2 Competitive transmission and availability payments (`ssec:73.4.2`). Kimal–Lo Aguirre: 3,000 MW HVDC, regulated concession paid an annual value set by the bid (USD 116.3 million a year, 2021 dollars), capex about USD 1.5 billion, due 2029 (`chile-solar`; equity split and the 2023 financing not stated). Example 73.3; Clause 73.2.
- 73.4.3 Offshore transmission and OFTO sales (`ssec:73.4.3`). Cross-reference Chapter 71 (Dogger Bank's 90% advance on OFTO proceeds).
- 73.4.4 Offtaker-built interconnection for IPPs (`ssec:73.4.4`). The generator's connection built by the utility (Case P), by a state transmission company with separate financing (Lake Turkana), or by the IPP and transferred; the interface each creates (cross-reference Chapter 25).
- 73.4.5 Merchant lines (`ssec:73.4.5`). Revenue from congestion rents or price spreads; why merchant transmission is rare in project finance; the framework for evaluating one.
- Framework 73.2 placed at the end of 73.4, with Exhibit 73.4 (transmission and interconnector revenue models compared).

**73.5 Interconnectors** (`sec:73.5`)
- 73.5.1 How an interconnector earns (`ssec:73.5.1`). Flows times price spread, capacity auctions; volatility of spreads.
- 73.5.2 Cap and floor for interconnectors and Greenlink (`ssec:73.5.2`). Mechanics owned by Chapter 19 (sec:19.6); the regime's history from `t-cap-and-floor` (Nemo Link pilot 2013, rolled out 2014; 25-year regimes; nine interconnectors with 10.9 GW awarded regimes in principle by December 2021; a hard cap with a 2% availability adjustment against a soft floor, assessed over five-year periods); Example 73.4 applies the mechanics to an interconnector's annual revenue; the floor as the debt-sizing line. Landmark: Greenlink (500 MW HVDC, Pembrokeshire to County Wexford; Equitix and Baltic Cable AB; financial close August 16, 2022; commercial operation January 29, 2025), the first GB cap-and-floor interconnector financed through project finance. Its preliminary levels for the GB share in 2020/21 prices were a cap of GBP 31.24 million a year, a notional floor of GBP 15.58 million and an Actual Floor Level of GBP 18.60 million, set from the project finance model on the day of financial close as opex and other costs plus debt service at an agreed (redacted) DSCR; if consumers pay the higher actual floor, the excess must be repaid before any distribution; Ofgem's minded-to post-construction review (consultation September 18 to October 16, 2026) proposes a cap of GBP 31.45 million, a notional floor of GBP 16.16 million and an Actual Floor of GBP 17.34 million. Lesson: a floor set at debt service times a DSCR turns a merchant link into a debt credit. Greenlink's DSCR, gearing and total cost, and any cap payments of other links, are not stated (Do not state).
- 73.5.3 Two regulators, two governments (`ssec:73.5.3`). Approvals on both sides, change in market design, cross-border political risk; HVDC converter and cable technology risk.

**73.6 Financing storage, transmission, and interconnectors** (`sec:73.6`)
- 73.6.1 Storage terms (`ssec:73.6.1`). Verified US terms above; tax credits (storage keeps §48E eligibility under OBBBA subject to prohibited-foreign-entity rules; `t-us-tax-credits`; rules in sec:67.10); ITC transfer proceeds as a funding source (Case R R7 and R8); the LDES floor's default 4.47% CPIH-real return as the GB debt-sizing reference (`t-cap-and-floor`).
- 73.6.2 Transmission and interconnector terms (`ssec:73.6.2`). CCSU eligibility for "transmission, distribution and storage of energy" with up to 22 years for listed sectors (`t-oecd-arrangement`); the cap-and-floor regimes' 25-year term as the tenor ceiling and Greenlink's floor-based sizing (`t-cap-and-floor`); no verified 2023 to 2026 margins or DSCRs for transmission in the fact sheets, so ranges are indicative under D-011 with drivers (regulatory model, construction risk, counterparty, currency).
- Exhibit 73.5: financing terms and anchors for storage and transmission (verified and indicative marked).

**73.7 Modeling storage and transmission** (`sec:73.7`)
- 73.7.1 Storage in the model (`ssec:73.7.1`). Cross-reference Chapter 45 for degradation and augmentation; add revenue stack by bucket, availability, cycling limits under warranty, augmentation capex and reserve.
- 73.7.2 Transmission in the model (`ssec:73.7.2`). Availability-based revenue with deductions; indexation of annual value; construction milestones.
- 73.7.3 Interconnector revenue with a cap and floor (`ssec:73.7.3`). Annual true-up, timing lag of floor payments, liquidity.

## 73.5 Opening

Real deal moment from `moss-landing`: on January 16, 2025 fire broke out inside a converted building at Moss Landing in California holding a 300 MW lithium-ion battery facility of about 100,000 modules. Two to three paragraphs: about 55% of modules were damaged; around 1,200 residents were evacuated; every facility on the site, including two other battery facilities and a gas plant, stopped; the owner wrote off approximately USD 400 million and later impaired a second facility; clean-up estimates rose to approximately USD 175 million; insurance paid its full USD 500 million limit, below the disclosed losses. The asset had been "contracted merchant" with long-term capacity payments from a utility. Close: one event turned a contracted asset into a stranded one; what should a lender to a battery have asked before it lent? (No cause, no supplier named, no MWh figure.)

## 73.6 Worked examples

**Example 73.1. Overbuild or augment at a Chilean battery** (`ex:73.1`, Illustrative; Section 73.2.2). Setting: Desierto Storage SpA (fictional), a battery in the Antofagasta region of Chile absorbing midday solar under an 8-year toll with a fictional generator from 2025; costs are the example's, in USD. The toll requires 400 MWh usable at the end of every year for 8 years. State of health of any battery vintage after k years: 0.968, 0.941, 0.917, 0.895, 0.874, 0.855, 0.837, 0.820 (k = 1 to 8). Installed cost of energy capacity USD 118/kWh today, falling 6% a year; discount rate 8%. Option A, overbuild at COD so year-8 usable capacity is 400 MWh: install 487.8 MWh, 87.8 MWh extra, cost USD 10.36 million at COD. Option B, install 436.2 MWh (36.2 MWh extra, USD 4.27 million) and augment at the start of any year whose year-end usable energy would fall below 400 MWh, adding enough that usable energy is exactly 400 MWh at the end of the third year after the year of installation, or at the end of the toll if earlier: augment 30.2 MWh at the start of year 3 (to reach 400 MWh at the end of year 6) at USD 104.26/kWh (USD 3.15 million) and 17.5 MWh at the start of year 7 at USD 81.40/kWh (USD 1.43 million, to reach 400 MWh at the end of the toll in year 8); augmentation costs are discounted from the start of the year (years 2 and 6 from COD); present value of augmentation USD 3.60 million; total present value USD 7.87 million, USD 2.49 million less than Option A. Usable MWh by year under B: 422.2, 410.5, 429.3, 418.8, 409.0, 400.0, 408.5, 400.0. Lesson: augmentation is cheaper when cell costs fall, but it is a future capex obligation that the model, an augmentation reserve and the capacity-maintenance clause must secure; the lender's question is who pays if cell prices rise instead.

**Example 73.2. The same California battery under a toll and under a floor** (`ex:73.2`, Illustrative; Section 73.3.2). Setting: Mojave Gate Storage LLC (fictional), a 200 MW, two-hour battery in Kern County, California, choosing in 2024 between a toll with a fictional utility and merchant revenue with a floor from a fictional insurer-backed provider; the prices are the example's. Inputs: opex USD 14.6/kW-year (USD 2.92 million); debt over 8 years at 6.4% (annuity factor 6.1127). Structure A, toll at USD 10.40/kW-month: revenue USD 24.96 million, CFADS USD 22.04 million, sized at 1.20x: debt USD 112.3 million. Structure B, merchant with a floor: P50 merchant revenue USD 78.0/kW-year (USD 15.6 million); floor USD 61.0/kW-year (USD 12.2 million) for a premium of USD 5.80/kW-year (USD 1.16 million); the floor bucket's CFADS (floor less opex less premium) USD 8.12 million sized at 1.30x, and the upside above the floor (USD 3.40 million) at 2.00x: debt USD 48.6 million. Structure C, pure merchant at 2.00x on P50 CFADS (USD 12.68 million): debt USD 38.8 million. If the floor bucket were sized at 1.20x (Exercise 73.6): USD 51.8 million. Lesson: the toll more than doubles debt capacity; the floor buys about a quarter more debt than pure merchant. The ratios are the US ladder (verified) except the 1.30x floor ratio, which the writer labels as an illustrative choice between the contracted and merchant rungs.

**Example 73.3. An availability deduction on a Brazilian transmission line** (`ex:73.3`, Illustrative; Section 73.4.2; a specialized application of eq:58.2). Setting: Linhas do Cerrado SA (fictional), winner of a 2024 transmission auction lot in Goiás, Brazil, under a 30-year concession; amounts are USD equivalents of the BRL annual value. The line earns an annual value of USD 86.35 million (USD 7.196 million a month). The contract deducts 2.0% of monthly revenue per full day of unavailability, capped at 10% of the month's revenue. A three-day outage costs USD 0.432 million; a seven-day outage would cost USD 1.007 million uncapped but is capped at USD 0.720 million. Lesson: availability regimes cap monthly exposure, so lenders size on the cap, not the average; the deduction must be passed down to the O&M contractor where its fault caused the outage (cross-reference Chapter 24).

**Example 73.4. A GB interconnector under a cap and floor** (`ex:73.4`, Illustrative, GBP; Section 73.5.2). Setting: Celtic Sea Link Ltd (fictional), a GB to Ireland link in its third regime year, 2030, whose lenders sized debt on the floor as Greenlink's did. Floor GBP 61.8 million a year; cap GBP 93.4 million. Market revenue scenarios: GBP 48.2 million (floor top-up GBP 13.6 million; retained 61.8 million); GBP 77.5 million (no adjustment); GBP 118.9 million (GBP 25.5 million above the cap returned; retained 93.4 million). Lesson: the floor, not expected revenue, carries the senior debt; the writer cross-references Chapter 19 for the mechanics (true-up periods, assessment over multi-year windows) and does not restate them.

## 73.7 Walkthrough

**Walkthrough: reading a battery storage technical due diligence report** (`sec:73.8`). The artifact is Exhibit 73.6, an Illustrative contents summary of an IE's 2025 report on Example 73.2's battery, Mojave Gate Storage's 200 MW, 400 MWh project in California (sections: site and layout; cells and integrator; warranties and capacity maintenance; degradation and augmentation plan; availability and round-trip efficiency; fire safety and propagation testing; grid connection and compliance; O&M; decommissioning and remediation; construction schedule). Steps:
1. Check the layout: containerized or indoor, spacing, separation from other assets (Moss Landing's site-wide shutdown).
2. Check the safety standards stack (`t-storage-safety`): UL 9540 listing of the system, UL 9540A test data at cell, module, unit and installation level, and the edition of NFPA 855 the authority having jurisdiction applies (2026 is current); the IE reports results, and the writer states no clause threshold or spacing distance.
3. Read the warranty: what it guarantees (capacity, efficiency), under which cycling limits, backed by whom.
4. Compare the degradation curve with the revenue model's cycling assumption; a merchant battery that cycles more than the warranty allows loses warranty cover.
5. Check the augmentation plan against the toll's capacity obligation (Example 73.1).
6. Read availability and efficiency guarantees against the toll's availability guarantee (Case R's R6 toll: 97.0%).
7. Check decommissioning and remediation cost and security.
8. Check insurance: maximum foreseeable loss across co-located units versus limits (cross-reference Chapter 27).
9. Write the lender conditions.

## 73.8 Frameworks

**Framework 73.1: Storage revenue stack screen** (`fw:storage-stack-screen`; original). Purpose: classify each revenue line of a storage asset by certainty and counterparty, and assign it a sizing treatment. Steps: (1) list every revenue line (toll, capacity, ancillary, arbitrage, floor payments, cap-and-floor top-ups); (2) for each, record tenor, counterparty and credit, and whether it depends on availability, cycling or market prices; (3) check for conflicts (a toll that gives the offtaker dispatch rights cannot also be stacked with the owner's arbitrage); (4) check that the operating strategy implied by the stack stays within warranty cycling limits; (5) bucket each line as contracted, floor-protected or merchant for sizing (Example 73.2); (6) stress the merchant lines with the low case and a fleet-growth scenario. When to use: valuation, sizing and hedge selection for any storage asset. Demonstrated on Example 73.2 and the Case R installment.

**Framework 73.2: Transmission revenue model selector** (`fw:transmission-revenue-selector`; original). Purpose: identify how a transmission or interconnector asset is paid and what risk that leaves with lenders. Steps: (1) who pays and under what instrument (tariff regulation, competitive contract, OFTO license, PPA connection schedule, market spreads, cap and floor); (2) what the payment depends on (spend, availability, flows, price spreads); (3) construction risk allocation and milestone dates; (4) counterparty and regulatory risk, including two-country exposure for interconnectors; (5) the debt-sizing line (regulated return, availability payment at full deduction cap, floor, or nothing); (6) residual risk and mitigants. When to use: any grid asset, and any generator whose connection depends on one. Demonstrated on Kimal–Lo Aguirre, Dogger Bank's OFTO split, Example 73.4 and Case P's 225 kV line.

## 73.9 Real cases

## 73.10 Running-case installment

**Case R: battery tolls and floors** (`sec:73.9`). Case Bible Part 6, Chapter 73 row: Cases R and P, 2023 to 2024; characters Carmen Villarreal-Ochoa and Thandeka Mabuza; figures R-F07, plus R-F18 (R7 floor settlements, Annex TR R.4) and R-F19 (R6 and R7 usable energy and augmentation, Annex TR R.5).

Scene (`casescene`, "Lattimer Infrastructure Partners, Houston, January 2024"; the first sentence of the installment places it a month before A3 was signed on February 15, 2024). Thandeka leads diligence on Kerrigan Storage (R7, 150 MW / 300 MWh, Houston hub, merchant with Galloway's floor of USD 74.00/kW-year for 8 years at a premium of USD 5.80/kW-year, Galloway taking 20% of revenue above USD 140/kW-year) and Barlow Gap Solar (R8). Carmen compares R7 with Redfern (R6, acquired August 31, 2023, toll from Orchard at USD 9.15/kW-month for 7 years, 97.0% availability guarantee, owner keeps 200 MWh usable) and pushes her augmentation plan for both (6% of nameplate MWh at the start of COD+5 and COD+9; Case Bible 3.3 and Annex TR R.5). Thandeka asks what happens if 2024 battery revenue halves ("What's the counterfactual?", once); the underwriting case rests on 2023 revenues, which the Case Bible says Rafael relied on (he is wrong; narration shows it).

Narration outside the box: Framework 73.1 applied to R6 and R7 (R6's toll is a contracted bucket; R7's floor is a floor-protected bucket; its upside is merchant). Annex TR R.4 sets the floor's reference: a benchmark two-hour ERCOT battery revenue index, which in the book is the Case Bible 3.5 illustrative path, times R7's measured availability, settled quarterly with an annual true-up, so the floor protects against market revenue, not against R7's own performance. The illustrative path (2023 USD 142.5/kW-year, 2024 USD 61.8, 2025 USD 52.4) sits below R7's USD 74.00 floor from 2024, so Galloway pays every year in the base case; the payments are printed only from Case Bible ledger: R-F18 (reference revenue, floor payments, premium and upside share by contract year). Then Exhibit 73.7 (`\caser`): from Case Bible ledger: R-F07 (A2 and A3 valuation and funding; ITC transfer proceeds at USD 0.925 per USD 1 of credit on 92% and 94% eligible basis) and R-F19 (usable energy and augmentation by year for R6 and R7, showing the 8% overbuild at COD and the toll requirement held through its term). No new computation.

Case P paragraph (narration, no scene, inside the same section): Bélanou's 225 kV connection to SEKA's Bélanou substation, built by SEKA under the PPA's interconnection schedule (Case Bible 1.2), placed in Framework 73.2 as the "offtaker-built interconnection" model; one sentence recalls that this interface produced the June 2021 grid event already told in Chapter 61 (cross-reference, no retelling); Thandeka's earlier role at the ABDB on Case P may be mentioned in one sentence to connect the two cases (Case Bible 4.4). No new Case P figures.

Ledger figures displayed: R-F07, R-F18, R-F19. Inputs cited: Case Bible 3.3, 3.4, 3.5, 3.6; Annex TR R.4 and R.5; Case Bible 1.2.

## 73.11 Practitioner's notebook (outline)

Checklist: revenue stack screen completed and conflicts resolved; warranty cycling limits against the dispatch strategy; augmentation plan, cost basis and reserve; capacity maintenance obligation in any toll; fire-safety design, spacing and propagation evidence; maximum foreseeable loss and insurance limits across co-located units; decommissioning and remediation security; supplier and integrator credit and prohibited-foreign-entity compliance for US credits; for transmission: revenue instrument, deduction regime and caps, milestone and long-stop dates, regulatory reset risk, construction interface with generators; for interconnectors: approvals on both sides, cap-and-floor terms, converter technology track record.

Red flags: merchant battery revenue underwritten on one year's prices; a toll whose availability guarantee exceeds the integrator's; indoor dense layouts; no remediation security; a transmission concession whose annual value is unindexed in an inflationary currency; an interconnector depending on a market design one government can change alone; a generator financed before its evacuation line has a contractor and funding (Lake Turkana).

Rules of thumb and their limits: contracted storage sized near 1.15x to 1.20x and merchant at 2.0x in the US 2024 to 2026 (dated bank view); floors carry debt, upside carries equity; availability-based transmission is sized on revenue net of the maximum deduction.

Common mistakes: double-counting stacked revenues that need the same capacity; holding round-trip efficiency constant over life; ignoring the time lag in floor or cap-and-floor top-ups; treating a regulated return as risk-free when the regulator resets it.

Questions experts ask: Who can dispatch this battery, and does that conflict with any other revenue line? What does the warranty cover if we cycle twice a day? What happens to the site and to us if one unit burns? Who pays the transmission owner if generation never arrives? Which government can change the interconnector's revenue?

## 73.12 Judgment drill

The situation. You are a lender asked to finance a 250 MW, 2-hour battery in ERCOT's energy-only market (Illustrative; fictional sponsor), in November 2025. The sponsor has a revenue floor from an insurance-backed provider rated A- for 8 years at about 80% of forecast P50 revenue, and asks for 10-year debt sized at 1.20x on the floor and 1.75x on the merchant upside. The integrator's warranty assumes one cycle a day; the sponsor's revenue case assumes 1.4 cycles a day. Credit approval is due Friday.

Reasoning: apply Framework 73.1; the floor counterparty is strong but the floor runs 8 years against 10-year debt, so years 9 and 10 are merchant; size the floor bucket only to the floor tenor and the tail at merchant ratios; the cycling assumption breaks the warranty, so either the revenue case drops to the warranty cycling or the lender sizes degradation without warranty support and requires a larger augmentation reserve; the expert answer: debt amortizing within the floor tenor (or a sweep after year 8), floor bucket at the contracted-storage rung, upside at 2.0x, augmentation reserve funded from cash flow, and a fire-separation and insurance package confirmed by the IE.

What would change the answer: floor tenor matching the debt (longer contracted bucket); a warranty amended for 1.4 cycles (degradation risk returns to the integrator); a capacity contract from a regulated utility (contracted rung); falling local battery revenue as fleets grow (lower merchant sizing).

## 73.13 Exercise set (13 exercises)

Tier 1 (4):
- 73.1 Explain why battery arbitrage revenue tends to fall as more batteries join a market.
- 73.2 Distinguish a toll, a floor and a cap and floor by who bears price risk.
- 73.3 Name three transmission revenue models and the debt-sizing line for each.
- 73.4 Explain why an interconnector carries political risk from two governments.

Tier 2 (5):
- 73.5 Recompute Example 73.2's toll case at USD 9.20/kW-month (answer: revenue USD 22.08 million; debt USD 97.6 million).
- 73.6 Recompute Example 73.2's floor case with the floor bucket at 1.20x (answer: USD 51.8 million).
- 73.7 Recompute Example 73.1 Option B at a 6% discount rate and say whether the ranking changes (answer: USD 8.08 million; Option B remains cheaper than Option A's USD 10.36 million).
- 73.8 For Example 73.3, compute the deduction for a five-day outage (answer: USD 0.720 million; five days reach the 10% cap exactly).
- 73.9 Draft a capacity maintenance clause for a battery toll with a measurement test, cure period and price reduction; annotate.

Tier 3 (4):
- 73.10 Using Case Bible ledger R-F07, R-F18 and R-F19 and Case Bible 3.4 and 3.5, write a half-page note comparing R6 and R7 as credits for a lender in 2024; no new running-case computation.
- 73.11 Model task: build a storage revenue sheet for a fictional 250 MW, 500 MWh battery in California in 2025 (Illustrative) with three buckets. Inputs: toll on 100 MW at USD 10.15/kW-month for 8 years; floor on 100 MW at USD 59.40/kW-year for 8 years at a premium of USD 5.65/kW-year, with merchant upside above the floor at a P50 of USD 76.20/kW-year; 50 MW pure merchant at the same P50; opex USD 14.6/kW-year on 250 MW, allocated by MW (40%, 40%, 20%); an augmentation reserve contribution of USD 0.74 million a year, allocated the same way and deducted before CFADS; debt over 8 years at 6.4% as a level annuity, sized by bucket at 1.20x (toll), 1.30x (floor bucket, floor less premium) and 2.00x (all merchant revenue). Layout: buckets in rows, years in columns, DSCR in a check row (answer: bucket CFADS USD 10.42 million, 3.62 million and 4.61 million; debt USD 84.2 million; annual debt service USD 13.78 million; P50 DSCR 1.35x; with merchant prices 40% lower, the floor pays and the DSCR falls to 1.12x).
- 73.12 Case problem: apply Framework 73.2 to a proposed merchant line (Illustrative; a fictional 500 MW line in 2026 linking the Texas Panhandle wind zone to a load center, with no regulated or contracted revenue). Inputs: capex USD 742.6 million; expected flow 2,630 GWh a year; price-spread scenarios USD 14.20, 8.60 and 3.10/MWh; opex 1.4% of capex a year; any debt would amortize over 15 years at 7.1%. Decide whether senior debt is available and on what structure (answer: congestion rent USD 37.35 million, 22.62 million and 8.15 million; net of opex of USD 10.40 million, USD 26.95 million, 12.22 million and minus 2.24 million; even the base case at 2.00x supports only USD 122.0 million, 16.4% of capex, and the middle case at 1.30x USD 85.1 million; the low case does not cover opex; senior debt needs a contracted or regulated revenue line such as capacity rights sold to generators or a cap and floor, otherwise the line is equity-funded).
- 73.13 Case problem: apply the walkthrough to a battery adjacent to an existing gas plant; specify the separation, insurance and remediation conditions a lender should require after Moss Landing.

Check for 73.8: 5 days × 2.0% = 10% of USD 7.196 million = USD 0.720 million, which equals the cap.

## 73.15 Glossary entries owned

| Term | Abbreviation | Definition | Home section |
|---|---|---|---|
| overbuild (storage) | – | Installing more battery energy capacity at COD than a contract requires, so that usable capacity still meets the requirement after degradation without later augmentation. | 73.2.2 |
| capacity maintenance agreement | – | A contract under which a battery supplier or integrator keeps usable capacity at a guaranteed level, by augmentation or replacement, for a fee. | 73.2.1 |
| long-duration energy storage | LDES | Storage that can discharge at rated power for many hours (in the GB scheme, eight hours or more). | 73.3.3 |
| pumped storage hydro | – | Storage that pumps water to an upper reservoir when power is cheap and generates when it is valuable. | 73.3.3 |
| annual value (transmission) | – | A fixed yearly payment, set by competitive bid and usually indexed, paid to a transmission concessionaire for an available line. | 73.4.2 |
| merchant transmission | – | A transmission line or interconnector whose revenue depends on price differences or congestion rents rather than regulated or contracted payments. | 73.4.5 |
| interconnector | – | A transmission link between two power markets or countries that earns from flows driven by price differences. | 73.5.1 |
| HVDC converter station | – | The installation at each end of a high-voltage direct current link that converts between alternating and direct current. | 73.5.3 |

Added: overbuild (storage), the storage sense split from the networks sense at ssec:82.3.2 (sequencing defect 30; central change requested).

## 73.16 Neighbors

Chapter 72 (Hydropower and geothermal) teaches assets whose risk sits in the water and the rock: hydrology allocation through tariff design, ground risk in "fixed-price" EPCs, DFI-led long-tenor packages, and geothermal drilling and decline, through Nam Theun 2, Bujagali and Sarulla. Chapter 74 (Nuclear power) takes construction risk to its extreme: decade-long builds, first-of-a-kind cost growth, and the state support packages (CfD, RAB, sovereign loans, regulated rate base, guarantees) that decide whether any private capital can be repaid, through Hinkley Point C, Sizewell C, Vogtle, Barakah and the first SMR projects.

# Chapter 74. Nuclear power

## 74.0 Rulings applied

- R-042: first-of-a-kind and nth-of-a-kind are homed in Chapter 14 (ssec:14.5.1); Chapter 74 applies them to nuclear and drops them from its glossary; Chapter 88 owns the FOAK financing ladder (fw:foak-ladder).
- R-027: overnight cost is homed in Chapter 11 (ssec:11.6.2); Chapter 74 drops it from its glossary.
- R-121: government support package is homed in Chapter 19 (sec:19.7); Chapter 74 applies it to nuclear and drops it from its glossary.
- R-015: decommissioning obligations and security are owned by Chapter 65 (sec:65.2) and the accounting by Chapter 66 (ssec:66.4.7); Chapter 74 owns nuclear decommissioning and waste funding and cites both.
- R-001: LCOE cited from sec:11.8.
- R-101, R-105, R-113: Part XVI ownership; Case Bible Part 6 row as amended by Annex P 2.2 (MEH's minister in 2025 is Sylvestre Ahouansou, who may appear); architecture title.
- Standards defect 18: two numbered examples added (Examples 74.4 and 74.5).

## 74.1 Header

- Chapter: 74
- Title: Nuclear power
- File: `chapters/74-nuclear.tex`
- Label: `ch:74`
- Target length: 9,500 words (raised in round 1 for the liability and waste material and two added examples; a floor, not a cap)
- Dominant expert lens: the government official (finance ministry and energy ministry) who must decide what risk the state takes, paired with the ECA specialist and the sponsor-side investor. The lender lens is the skeptic's: why almost no commercial lender takes nuclear construction risk, and what changes that.

## 74.2 Purpose and capability statement

Purpose. Nuclear power is the hardest test of project finance's core idea, that risks go to the party best able to bear them, because the largest risk in a new nuclear plant, construction cost and time on a first or early build, is one almost no private party can bear at a price consumers will pay. Every financed plant of the last decade solved this differently: sponsors' balance sheets under a CfD (Hinkley Point C), a regulated asset base with consumers paying during construction and a state backstop (Sizewell C), regulated utilities with federal loan guarantees (Vogtle), a sovereign lending to itself with a vendor-country ECA and vendor equity (Barakah), and state-sponsored SMR programs (Darlington). This chapter teaches why the cost of capital dominates nuclear economics, how each model allocates construction, operating, decommissioning and liability risk, and how to judge a new nuclear proposal, including small modular reactors, from the government's chair as well as the lender's.

What you will be able to do:
- Explain why financing cost and construction duration dominate nuclear costs, and compute the effect (Capability 1, Capability 13).
- Map who bears construction, operating, decommissioning, waste and liability risk under the main nuclear financing models (Capability 3, Capability 9).
- Read a nuclear RAB or CfD support package and the state's contingent liabilities in it (Capability 8, Capability 9).
- Advise a finance ministry on a nuclear or SMR proposal and name the conditions that would make it financeable (Capability 15).

## 74.3 Concepts owned and concepts assumed

### Owned

1. Nuclear industry economics: overnight cost (term from ssec:11.6.2), construction duration, financing cost during construction, very long operating life, low fuel cost, high fixed cost; first-of-a-kind against nth-of-a-kind applied to nuclear (terms from ssec:14.5.1).
2. Why commercial project finance avoids nuclear construction risk: size relative to sponsor and lender balance sheets, duration, regulatory licensing risk, contractor capacity (Vogtle's contractor bankruptcy), political risk of shutdown.
3. The nuclear financing models compared: CfD with sponsor-financed construction; nuclear RAB with allowed revenue during construction and cost sharing; regulated utility rate base with construction cost recovery and federal guarantees; sovereign-financed with vendor-country ECA and vendor equity; state-sponsored SMR programs (Framework 74.1, original).
4. The legal plumbing of a nuclear RAB as lenders see it: designation and license, revenue collection through suppliers, special administration, decommissioning finance (structure from `t-uk-cfd-rab`; the RAB model itself is owned by Chapter 19).
5. Government support packages applied to nuclear (the general term is homed in sec:19.7): shutdown compensation, debt guarantees, cost-overrun backstops, uninsurable-risk cover (HPC's SoSIA and Treasury guarantee; SZC's support package), and how a finance ministry records them as contingent liabilities.
6. Nuclear third-party liability as a precondition for financing: channeling to the operator, the tiers of the Paris and Brussels Conventions as amended in 2004 (EUR 700 million, EUR 1.2 billion, EUR 1.5 billion), the Vienna Convention and the CSC, and the US Price-Anderson Act (USD 500 million primary insurance per site plus a retrospective assessment of up to approximately USD 158 million per reactor), from `t-nuclear-liability`; Example 74.5.
7. Decommissioning and waste funding: funded decommissioning programs (HPC published September 29, 2016; SZC July 22, 2025) and how their design differs under a CfD and a RAB; US decommissioning funding assurance under 10 CFR 50.75; UK waste transfer contracts priced under the 2011 methodology with a cap fixed in the contract (from `t-nuclear-decom-waste`); sinking-fund arithmetic; Examples 74.3 and 74.4; principles of decommissioning in sec:65.2.
8. ECA support for nuclear exports under the Nuclear Sector Understanding (up to 22 years; Arrangement detail in Chapter 29), vendor-country financing as an export tool.
9. Small modular reactors as a financing problem: FOAK cost and subscription risk (NuScale/UAMPS), state sponsorship and milestone-gated equity (Darlington), vendor development funding (Wylfa), data-center offtake as a frontier topic (forward reference to Chapter 88).
10. Nuclear-specific modeling: construction S-curves and IDC over long builds, cost-sharing thresholds, allowed return on a RAB during construction, decommissioning fund contributions.
11. Sector clause: Clause 74.1 (government compensation for a politically motivated shutdown).
