## 74.4 Locked table of contents

Opening (no heading). See 74.5.

`\section*{What you will be able to do}`.

**74.1 The economics of nuclear power** (`sec:74.1`)
- 74.1.1 Overnight cost, time, and money (`ssec:74.1.1`). Example 74.1; Equation 74.1 (cost at COD from spend profile and financing rate).
- 74.1.2 First-of-a-kind and the learning curve (`ssec:74.1.2`). Vogtle's cost waterfall for Georgia Power's share (USD 4.418 billion certified in 2009; USD 7.3 billion net forecast in 2017; USD 10.73 billion actual; `vogtle`); HPC from GBP 18 billion (2016 prices) at approval to about GBP 35 billion (2015 prices) by February 2026 with Unit 1 expected in 2030 (`hinkley-sizewell`; price bases stated each time).
- 74.1.3 Long life and low marginal cost (`ssec:74.1.3`). Why nuclear's value depends on decades of operation and how revenue design must cover that life (Barakah's 60-year PPA; HPC's 35-year CfD).

**74.2 What keeps commercial lenders out of nuclear construction** (`sec:74.2`)
- 74.2.1 Size, duration, and contractor capacity (`ssec:74.2.1`). Vogtle: "substantially fixed price" EPC; Westinghouse Chapter 11 on March 29, 2017; Toshiba parent guarantee settled at USD 3.68 billion; cost-reimbursable completion by Bechtel (`vogtle`; EPC lesson already in Chapter 23, here the financing consequence).
- 74.2.2 Licensing, politics, and shutdown risk (`ssec:74.2.2`). Regulatory approvals over a decade; political shutdown risk and why HPC's sponsors required compensation for it (SoSIA, up to GBP 22 billion in 2012 prices; `hinkley-sizewell`).
- 74.2.3 Liability and insurance (`ssec:74.2.3`). From `t-nuclear-liability`: the operator is exclusively and strictly liable, so suppliers, EPC contractors and lenders are channeled out; liability is limited in amount and time and must be insured or secured; state funds sit above the operator tier; jurisdiction is concentrated in one country's courts. Paris and Brussels as amended by the 2004 Protocols (in force January 1, 2022): operator at least EUR 700 million, operator or installation state to EUR 1.2 billion, a EUR 300 million collective tier, EUR 1.5 billion in total; the UK raises standard-site operator liability from EUR 700 million by EUR 100 million a year to EUR 1.2 billion; the Vienna Convention's 1997 Protocol (in force October 4, 2003); the CSC (in force April 15, 2015). The US is outside Paris and Vienna and a CSC party; Price-Anderson channels liability through USD 500 million of private primary insurance per site plus a retrospective assessment of up to approximately USD 158 million per reactor, together more than USD 16 billion, extended to December 31, 2065 (NRC figures dated July 2025); 10 CFR 50.54(w) requires at least USD 1.06 billion of onsite property insurance per site, currently provided only by NEIL. Example 74.5 (in the walkthrough, sec:74.7) traces a claim through the tiers. What an EPC contractor, an ECA and a lender require for a new-build: convention accession or conforming law, operator insurance, and a state indemnity above the cap. Not stated as primary-verified: individual states' SDR amounts or accession dates, India's SHANTI Act detail, Germany's and Japan's security figures (Do not state).

**74.3 Five ways nuclear plants have been financed** (`sec:74.3`)
- 74.3.1 Hinkley Point C on sponsor balance sheets under a CfD (`ssec:74.3.1`). EDF 66.5% and CGN 33.5% at the 2016 deal; 35-year CPI-indexed CfD at GBP 92.50/MWh in 2012 prices, now GBP 89.50/MWh after SZC's FID; nothing paid until generation; overruns on EDF; HM Treasury bond guarantee up to GBP 2 billion initially with up to GBP 13.1 billion possible later; funded decommissioning program (`hinkley-sizewell`).
- 74.3.2 Sizewell C under a nuclear RAB (`ssec:74.3.2`). FID July 22, 2025; financial close November 2025; baseline GBP 38.2 billion (2024-25 prices); equity DESNZ 44.9%, La Caisse 20%, Centrica 15%, EDF 12.5%, Amber 7.6%; NWF up to GBP 36.6 billion and GBP 5 billion commercial debt 94% covered by Bpifrance; gearing cap 65% of RAB; construction WACC 6.73% (CPIH-real); equity IRR 10.8% to 13.0% nominal post-tax; 50:50 sharing between GBP 40.5 billion and GBP 47.7 billion; NAO's view that finance cost is about 5 percentage points lower than HPC's after adjusting for rates but consumers may still pay more per MWh (`hinkley-sizewell`). Legal plumbing from `t-uk-cfd-rab` (designation and license; revenue collection through suppliers; special administration; decommissioning). Example 74.2 applies the sharing logic to an Illustrative scheme.
- 74.3.3 Vogtle 3 and 4 under rate regulation and federal guarantees (`ssec:74.3.3`). Joint ownership (Georgia Power 45.7%, Oglethorpe 30%, MEAG 22.7%, Dalton 1.6%); financing cost recovery during construction under 2009 state law; DOE guarantees up to USD 12 billion of FFB loans; COD July 31, 2023 and April 29, 2024; December 2023 Georgia Public Service Commission decision (the commission's name is spelled out; PSC is reserved, R-114) allowing USD 7.562 billion of capital into rates against about USD 10.75 billion expected spend (`vogtle`).
- 74.3.4 Barakah and a sovereign lending to itself (`ssec:74.3.4`). Four APR-1400 units; financial close October 20, 2016; about USD 24.4 billion, of which up to USD 16.2 billion from the Abu Dhabi Department of Finance, USD 2.5 billion from KEXIM, USD 250 million commercial, USD 4.7 billion equity; KEPCO 18% in both the financing company and the operator; 60-year PPA with EWEC; KEXIM refinanced by ADCB and FAB in July 2023 (`barakah`; the published figures sum to about USD 23.65 billion, which the writer notes).
- 74.3.5 SMRs and state sponsors (`ssec:74.3.5`). Darlington (Ontario FID May 8, 2025; four BWRX-300 units, C$20.9 billion; first unit C$7.7 billion; Canada Growth Fund C$2 billion for 15% and Building Ontario Fund C$1 billion for 7.5% in milestone-gated tranches); Wylfa (three Rolls-Royce SMR units, contract April 13, 2026, NWF loan of GBP 599 million to the vendor at market rates, FID still to come); NuScale/UAMPS terminated November 8, 2023 after its target price rose from USD 58/MWh to USD 89/MWh and subscribers withdrew (`t-frontier-data`).
- Exhibit 74.1: the five models compared (who funds construction; who bears overruns; when revenue starts; state support; debt providers; outcome to date).

**74.4 Decommissioning, waste, and the long tail** (`sec:74.4`)
- 74.4.1 Funded decommissioning (`ssec:74.4.1`). HPC's funded decommissioning program (`hinkley-sizewell`); from `t-nuclear-decom-waste`: the UK Energy Act 2008 requires an approved FDP made of a Decommissioning and Waste Management Plan and a Funding Arrangements Plan building a segregated fund; HPC's FDP was published September 29, 2016 and SZC's July 22, 2025; NLFAB's account of the differences (for SZC an independent FDP Implementation Company controls investment and contributions are funded through an allowed-revenue building block, so consumers effectively underwrite them, with shortfalls recoverable for up to 10 years after early closure; for HPC the operator controls investment because its investors bear more fund risk); the US model in one paragraph (NRC funding assurance under 10 CFR 50.75 by prepayment, external sinking fund or surety, with section 468A tax treatment; cross-reference sec:65.2 for the formula). Example 74.3 (sinking-fund contribution); early shutdown risk to fund adequacy. No HPC or SZC fund target, contribution amount or US aggregate fund balance is stated (Do not state).
- 74.4.2 Waste transfer pricing and the state as long-term holder (`ssec:74.4.2`). From `t-nuclear-decom-waste`: under waste transfer contracts the UK government takes title to spent fuel and intermediate-level waste at a price set under the 2011 Waste Transfer Pricing Methodology, above expected cost with a risk premium and subject to a cap fixed in the contract, with setting deferred so it can rest on actual cost data; HPC's figures (expected spent fuel price GBP 585,475/tU in September 2012 money, cap GBP 1,159,250/tU in December 2012 money, risk fee GBP 3,020/tU; ILW GBP 23,034/m³ expected, cap GBP 65,850/m³); the cap is about twice the expected spent fuel price. The US contrast: the Nuclear Waste Policy Act fee of 0.1 cent/kWh, set to zero in 2014, and DOE's failure to take fuel from 1998 paid through the Judgment Fund. Example 74.4. Not stated: that HPC's waste transfer price has been set, any total of Judgment Fund payments, or the UK GDF timetable (Do not state).

**74.5 Financing terms and ECA support for nuclear** (`sec:74.5`)
- 74.5.1 What the market shows (`ssec:74.5.1`). Verified deal terms only (SZC, HPC, Barakah, Vogtle, Darlington); Fraunhofer ISE's July 2024 modeling assumption of 60% debt at 8.0% printed as an assumption, never a market term (`t-market-norms`). Operating performance against new-build cost from `t-power-tech-norms`: US nuclear ran at about 91% capacity factor and the global fleet at 83% in 2024, against Lazard's Vogtle-based new-build capex of USD 12,300 to 17,570/kW and LCOE of USD 175 to 255/MWh, with WNA construction times of 61 to 204 months.
- 74.5.2 ECAs and the Nuclear Sector Understanding (`ssec:74.5.2`). NSU up to 22 years (`t-oecd-arrangement`); vendor-country ECAs at Barakah (KEXIM) and SZC (Bpifrance cover on commercial debt).
- Exhibit 74.2: nuclear financings, sources, and terms (verified only).

**74.6 Modeling a nuclear project** (`sec:74.6`)
- 74.6.1 Long construction and IDC (`ssec:74.6.1`). S-curve spend, IDC or allowed return during construction, sensitivity to delay (Example 74.1).
- 74.6.2 RAB revenue and cost sharing (`ssec:74.6.2`). Allowed revenue on RAB during construction; sharing thresholds (Example 74.2); cross-reference Chapter 19 for the RAB formula.
- 74.6.3 Decommissioning and fuel (`ssec:74.6.3`). Fund contributions, fuel cycle costs as a category.

**74.7 Walkthrough: reading a nuclear government support package** (`sec:74.7`). See 74.7.

**74.8 Case P: Kessara signs a small modular reactor memorandum** (`sec:74.8`). See 74.10.

**74.9 Practitioner's notebook** (`sec:74.9`). **74.10 Judgment drill** (`sec:74.10`).

**74.11 In nuclear, the state chooses how much risk to keep before anyone else can lend** (`sec:74.11`). Close; open problem for Chapter 75.

**74.12 Exercises** (`sec:74.12`). **74.13 Solutions to exercises** (`sec:74.13`). `\section*{Sources}`.

## 74.5 Opening

Real deal moment from `hinkley-sizewell` in two beats. September 29, 2016: the UK government approves Hinkley Point C at an expected construction cost of GBP 18 billion (2016 prices), with the sponsors paying for construction and earning nothing until the station generates. July 22, 2025: Sizewell C, a near-identical station, reaches FID under a different model in which consumers pay during construction and the state is the largest shareholder and lender. Between the two, HPC's estimate rose to about GBP 35 billion (2015 prices) and no other developer would sign a CfD-only nuclear deal. Close with the question: what changed between the two deals, and who now carries the risk that EDF carried alone?

## 74.6 Worked examples

**Example 74.1. How much of a Dutch nuclear plant's cost is money** (`ex:74.1`, Illustrative; Section 74.1.1). Setting: Zeeland Kernenergie BV (fictional) evaluates a two-unit plant in the Netherlands in 2026 for its state shareholder; the costs are the example's, not the Dutch program's. The 3,260 MW plant with an overnight cost of USD 8,420/kW (USD 27.449 billion, real 2026), spent evenly over the build with spend at mid-year, compounded to COD at a real financing rate; capacity factor 90% (25.702 TWh a year); life 60 years. Six-year build at 9.0%: cost at COD USD 35.934 billion; financing USD 8.485 billion (23.6% of cost at COD); capital charge USD 126.55/MWh. Six years at 6.0%: USD 32.855 billion; financing USD 5.405 billion (16.5%); USD 79.10/MWh. Ten years at 9.0%: USD 43.540 billion; financing USD 16.090 billion (37.0%); USD 153.33/MWh. Ten years at 6.0%: USD 37.250 billion; financing USD 9.801 billion (26.3%); USD 89.68/MWh. Eight years at 7.5% (Exercise 74.6): USD 37.163 billion; financing USD 9.714 billion (26.1%); USD 109.88/MWh. Lesson: cutting the financing rate by three points saves more than cutting four years off the build at 9%; that is the economic case for RAB and state finance, and the NAO's reason for its comparison of SZC and HPC. Equation 74.1: $K_{\mathrm{COD}} = \sum_{k=1}^{B} \frac{K}{B}(1+w)^{B-k+0.5}$, with $K$ overnight cost, $B$ build years, $w$ financing rate; Excel on a row of build years: `=SUMPRODUCT(Spend_row*(1+w)^(B-Year_row+0.5))` (writer replaces range names with cell references per style sheet 5.4).

**Example 74.2. Who pays for an overrun under a GB nuclear RAB with sharing** (`ex:74.2`, Illustrative, modeled on the structure in `hinkley-sizewell`; Section 74.3.2). Setting: Wessex Nuclear plc (fictional), a GB nuclear RAB company licensed in 2027, with fictional thresholds. Baseline cost GBP 25.4 billion; costs up to a lower threshold of GBP 27.1 billion enter the RAB in full; between GBP 27.1 billion and a higher threshold of GBP 32.6 billion, 50% enters the RAB and investors bear 50%; above GBP 32.6 billion, a government support package applies (the government decides). Outturn GBP 26.3 billion: RAB 26.30, investors 0. Outturn GBP 30.8 billion: RAB 28.95, investors 1.85. Outturn GBP 34.1 billion: RAB 29.85 within the band, investors 2.75, and GBP 1.50 billion above the higher threshold falls to the support package. The writer states that the treatment below the lower threshold is an assumption of the example, because the fact sheet verifies only the 50:50 band between SZC's thresholds.

**Example 74.3. Funding decommissioning for a Finnish unit over its operating life** (`ex:74.3`, Illustrative; Section 74.4.1). Setting: Pohjola Ydinvoima Oy (fictional), Finland, sets its decommissioning contributions in 2026; the liability and returns are the example's, not Finland's fund rules. The 1,640 MW unit (capacity factor 91%) faces a decommissioning liability of USD 1,412 million in real terms at the end of a 60-year life. At a real fund return of 2.5%, the level annual contribution is USD 10.38 million (USD 0.794/MWh). At 1.5%, USD 14.68 million (USD 1.123/MWh). If the plant shuts after 30 years with contributions at the 2.5% rate, the fund holds USD 455.8 million, USD 956.2 million short. Lesson: the per-MWh cost is small, but early shutdown, not the return assumption, is the fund's real risk, which is why political shutdown compensation and funded decommissioning programs are negotiated together.

**Example 74.4. What the waste transfer cap costs per MWh** (`ex:74.4`, Illustrative, using HPC's verified prices from `t-nuclear-decom-waste`; Section 74.4.2). Setting: a fictional 1,630 MW unit in England with a waste transfer contract on HPC's terms, assessed in 2026; the annual discharge of 21.4 tU of packaged spent fuel and the 91% capacity factor are the example's assumptions; prices are in 2012 money as the contract states them. Output 12.99 TWh a year. At the expected price of GBP 585,475/tU the annual waste transfer cost is GBP 12.53 million, GBP 0.96/MWh; at the cap of GBP 1,159,250/tU it is GBP 24.81 million, GBP 1.91/MWh; the risk fee adds GBP 0.06 million a year. Lesson: a cap at about twice the expected price costs the project under GBP 1/MWh more in the worst case, yet it turns an open-ended century-long liability into a number lenders can model; that is why it is fixed at FID. The writer states that the money-value dates differ slightly, so the ratio is approximate, and that HPC's price has not yet been set.

## 74.7 Walkthrough

**Walkthrough: reading a nuclear government support package** (`sec:74.7`). The artifact is Exhibit 74.3, an Illustrative term-sheet summary of the support package for Example 74.2's Wessex Nuclear plc, as HM Treasury officials (in the example, fictional) would review it in 2027 (items: third-party liability indemnity above the operator's statutory limit; shutdown compensation; cost-overrun support above the higher threshold; debt guarantee or state lending; uninsurable-risk cover; special administration; decommissioning fund oversight; discontinuation compensation if the project is abandoned). Steps, from the finance ministry's chair:
1. List each instrument and its trigger.
2. Classify each as funded (cash now), contingent (cash on an event), or regulatory (a power to set revenue).
3. Estimate the maximum exposure of each and the event that triggers it.
4. Ask which risk each transfers from investors and lenders to the state or consumers.
5. Check whether the package plus consumer payments make the debt investment grade without an explicit guarantee.
6. Record the contingent liabilities as a fiscal-risk statement would (cross-reference Chapter 57).
7. Then change chairs to the lender: which instrument is the debt's real credit support?

**Example 74.5. Who pays for a nuclear accident in a Paris and Brussels state** (`ex:74.5`, Illustrative, using the verified tiers in `t-nuclear-liability`; Section 74.7). Setting: an incident in 2031 at a fictional plant in a Paris and Brussels Convention state that has set operator liability at the EUR 700 million minimum; compensable claims (Illustrative) total EUR 2,148.6 million. Tier 1, the operator (insured): EUR 700.0 million. Tier 2, the operator or installation-state public funds up to EUR 1.2 billion: EUR 500.0 million. Tier 3, the collective contribution of Brussels parties: EUR 300.0 million. Uncompensated under the conventions: EUR 648.6 million, which falls on victims unless national law sets higher or unlimited liability or the state chooses to pay. If the state had set operator liability at EUR 1.2 billion (as the UK is phasing in for standard sites), the operator would bear EUR 1,200.0 million, the collective tier EUR 300.0 million, and EUR 648.6 million would still be uncovered. Lesson: channeling protects the vendor, the EPC contractor and the lenders, but the tiers cap what anyone pays; the state's indemnity above the cap is part of the support package the walkthrough reads, and lenders check that the regime exists before they lend.

## 74.8 Frameworks

**Framework 74.1: Nuclear risk-bearer map** (`fw:nuclear-risk-bearer`; original). Purpose: show who bears each nuclear risk under a proposed financing model. Steps: (1) list the risks: construction cost, construction delay, licensing, technology performance, operating, market price, political shutdown, decommissioning, waste, third-party liability; (2) for the proposed model, assign each to sponsors, lenders, contractor, consumers, state budget or vendor; (3) compare with the five reference models (HPC, SZC, Vogtle, Barakah, Darlington); (4) identify any risk assigned to a party that cannot bear it (a private contractor's parent guarantee for a multi-billion overrun; a municipal subscriber group for FOAK cost); (5) state what instrument would move it and at what cost. When to use: any nuclear or SMR proposal, from either chair. Demonstrated on Exhibit 74.1, the Case P installment and the judgment drill.

| `vogtle` | Sections 74.1.2, 74.2.1, 74.3.3 | First-of-a-kind nuclear was not project-financed: risk sat with regulated utilities, their customers and federal guarantees; a fixed price is worth the contractor's credit. |
| `barakah` | Section 74.3.4 | Sovereign finance with a vendor-country ECA and vendor equity in the financing company and operator; ECA construction debt refinanced locally after operations. |

| `t-nuclear-decom-waste` | Sections 74.4.1, 74.4.2; Example 74.4 | A century-long back end is ring-fenced by a segregated fund and a capped waste transfer price fixed at FID; under a RAB consumers underwrite the fund, under a CfD investors do. |
| `t-power-tech-norms` | Section 74.5.1 | Existing plants run at about 91% (US) and 83% (global); new-build cost and construction times frame completion risk. |

All fact sheets this chapter needs are delivered; the earlier requests are met by `t-nuclear-liability` and `t-nuclear-decom-waste`.

## 74.10 Running-case installment

**Case P: Kessara signs a small modular reactor memorandum** (`sec:74.8`). Case Bible Part 6, Chapter 74 row as amended by Annex P 2.2: Case P (small), 2025; Clémentine Agbo-Lawson (Director-General of Public Debt at MEF since 2020); MEH's minister in 2025, Sylvestre Ahouansou (technocrat appointed after the protests; verbal habit "What does the household pay?"), may appear; no figures.

Short installment (500 to 700 words). First sentence: in 2025, seven years after Bélanou's financial close and a year after the SEKA arrears settlement. Scene (`casescene`, "Ministry of Economy and Finance, Dabakro, 2025"; the writer chooses and states a month after June 2025, consistent with the sovereign's B- stable rating from November 2024 and the 2023 stabilization program). MEH, under Sylvestre Ahouansou, has signed a non-binding memorandum with an unnamed reactor vendor (no real vendor is named; D-007) for an SMR; the character sheet says he signed before MEF had counted the contingent liabilities, which is where he is wrong. If he appears, it is for a few lines in which he asks his habitual question and Clémentine answers it with the state's exposure, not the household tariff. Clémentine's team reviews what the vendor's proposal implies for the state: a long PPA with SEKA, a sovereign guarantee and, implicitly, construction overruns. She asks where each obligation is written ("Where is that written?", once). Her 2023 experience paying guarantee demands late, after never budgeting for a call on the Bélanou guarantee (Case Bible 4.1), shapes her questions; the narration states that history in one sentence with a pointer to Chapter 59.

Narration outside the box applies Framework 74.1 qualitatively: in Kessara, a B- sovereign with a strained single buyer, every risk the vendor does not take lands on SEKA or the Treasury, including third-party liability above any operator cap if Kessara has joined no liability convention (Example 74.5); the NuScale/UAMPS and Darlington contrasts show what a credible SMR financing has needed so far (state sponsorship with funded equity, or subscribers able to absorb FOAK cost). No numbers; the scene ends with the memorandum unresolved.

## 74.11 Practitioner's notebook (outline)

Checklist: price base of every cost figure; construction schedule and its FOAK assumptions; contractor and vendor credit and guarantee caps; licensing path and regulator capacity; revenue model and when revenue starts; cost-sharing thresholds and the support package; shutdown compensation; decommissioning fund and waste arrangements; liability regime and insurance; ECA and NSU eligibility; state contingent liabilities quantified; offtaker or subscriber commitment strength.

Red flags: cost figures quoted without price base; FOAK schedule assumed equal to NOAK; a fixed-price EPC from a vendor whose parent guarantee is small relative to plausible overruns; subscription-based offtake with exit rights before FID (NuScale/UAMPS); a sovereign guarantee covering overruns in a country without fiscal space; no special administration or continuity regime.

Rules of thumb and their limits: financing rate matters more than build time at high rates (Example 74.1), but delay compounds both; vendor equity aligns the vendor with operation (Barakah) but does not cap overruns; RAB lowers the cost of capital but moves payment to consumers before power is delivered (NAO's caution).

Common mistakes: comparing HPC and SZC costs in different price bases; describing SZC's equity as majority government-owned (the state holds 44.9%); treating Vogtle as project finance; stating Barakah's financing envelope as its outturn cost.

Questions experts ask: Who pays if the plant costs 50% more? When does the first pound or dollar of revenue arrive? Who decides to stop if costs explode, and who pays then? What happens to the debt if a future government orders a shutdown? Who holds the waste in year 80?

## 74.12 Judgment drill

The situation. You are the head of the public debt directorate in a middle-income Southeast Asian country rated BB (Illustrative; fictional vendor and utility), in June 2026. A reactor vendor proposes a two-unit plant under a build-own-operate structure: the vendor takes 30% equity, its home ECA lends 50% of cost for 18 years, and the state utility signs a 25-year PPA at a fixed tariff; the vendor asks for a sovereign guarantee of the utility's payments and of termination amounts, "including if construction costs rise". The cabinet wants your recommendation in two weeks.

Reasoning: apply Framework 74.1; the guarantee as drafted moves construction overrun risk to the state through the termination amount and tariff pressure; the 25-year PPA is short against the plant's life, making the tariff high; the expert recommends separating risks: no guarantee of overrun-driven termination amounts; a fixed price with a large, credit-backed vendor commitment and a pain-share band; state equity only with milestone gates (Darlington); a revenue model that starts payments at output (CfD-like) unless the state is prepared to accept a RAB with consumer payments during construction and an explicit, budgeted support package; and a fiscal-risk disclosure of the remaining contingent liabilities.

What would change the answer: a NOAK design with a demonstrated build record in the vendor's home market (smaller overrun distribution); a vendor willing to cap its price with a parent of sufficient size; a creditworthy offtaker outside the state (lower guarantee need); a country with fiscal space and a regulator able to run a RAB (RAB becomes viable).

## 74.13 Exercise set (12 exercises)

Tier 1 (4):
- 74.1 Explain why a lower financing rate matters more for nuclear than for a CCGT.
- 74.2 Compare who bears a construction overrun under HPC's CfD and SZC's RAB.
- 74.3 Explain why Vogtle is not an example of non-recourse project finance.
- 74.4 Explain the purpose of vendor equity at Barakah.

Tier 2 (5):
- 74.5 Recompute Example 74.1 for a seven-year build at 9.0% (answer: cost at COD USD 37.666 billion; financing 27.1% of it; USD 132.65/MWh).
- 74.6 Recompute for an eight-year build at 7.5% (answer: cost at COD USD 37.163 billion; financing 26.1%; USD 109.88/MWh).
- 74.7 Apply Example 74.2's sharing scheme to an outturn of GBP 32.6 billion and GBP 36.0 billion (answer: at 32.6, RAB GBP 29.85 billion, investors GBP 2.75 billion, nothing above the threshold; at 36.0, the same plus GBP 3.40 billion to the support package).
- 74.8 Recompute Example 74.3 at a 1.5% real return and say what the per-MWh cost becomes (answer: USD 14.68 million a year; USD 1.123/MWh); then recompute Example 74.5's uncovered amount if claims were EUR 1,386.2 million (answer: operator EUR 700.0 million, tier 2 EUR 500.0 million, collective tier EUR 186.2 million, nothing uncovered).
- 74.9 Draft a shutdown compensation clause for a nuclear support agreement covering a politically motivated closure, with sponsor-friendly, lender-friendly and government-friendly variants; annotate.

Tier 3 (3):
- 74.10 Using the SZC facts in `hinkley-sizewell`, build a one-page risk-bearer map (Framework 74.1) and compare it with HPC; state which risks moved to consumers and which to the state.
- 74.11 Model task: build a construction-period sheet for Example 74.1's plant (overnight cost USD 27.449 billion, even annual spend at mid-year, compounding to COD at the financing rate) with a delay switch that adds build years and a rate selector; report cost at COD under three schedules (answer: six years at 9.0%, USD 35.934 billion; eight years at 7.5%, USD 37.163 billion; ten years at 9.0%, USD 43.540 billion; the sheet's check row reproduces Equation 74.1).
- 74.12 Case P: draft Clémentine's two-page note to the Minister of Economy and Finance on the SMR memorandum, applying Framework 74.1 and listing the contingent liabilities the state would assume (no invented figures).

## 74.15 Glossary entries owned

| Term | Abbreviation | Definition | Home section |
|---|---|---|---|
| small modular reactor | SMR | A nuclear reactor of up to a few hundred megawatts designed for factory fabrication and serial deployment. | 74.3.5 |
| funded decommissioning program | FDP | An arrangement requiring a nuclear operator to accumulate a segregated fund during operations to pay for decommissioning and waste. | 74.4.1 |
| special administration regime | – | A statutory insolvency regime that keeps an essential asset operating under an administrator if its owner fails. | 74.3.2 |
| vendor equity | – | An equity stake taken by the reactor vendor in the project or its operator to align the vendor with long-term performance. | 74.3.4 |
| nuclear risk-bearer map | – | Framework 74.1: a table assigning each nuclear risk to the party that bears it under a given financing model. | 74.3 |

Removed (homed elsewhere; used without bold and cross-referenced): overnight cost (ssec:11.6.2; R-027); first-of-a-kind and nth-of-a-kind (ssec:14.5.1; R-042); government support package (sec:19.7; R-121).

## 74.16 Neighbors

Chapter 73 (Storage, transmission, and interconnectors) teaches assets that earn from moving energy in time or space: battery revenue stacks, augmentation and fire risk, transmission revenue models from regulated returns to competitive availability payments, and interconnectors under cap and floor. Chapter 75 (Upstream and midstream oil and gas, including reserve-based lending) moves from electricity to hydrocarbons: reserve-based lending against a borrowing base that is redetermined as prices and reserves change, production sharing contracts, and pipelines financed on ship-or-pay and cost-of-service tariffs.

## 74.17 Coverage check

- "Sector deep dives… nuclear (including RAB and CfD models)" (coverage P): Sections 74.1 to 74.6; RAB and CfD applied in 74.3.1 and 74.3.2 (models owned by Chapter 19).
- Coverage S "new nuclear" treated here as current practice; frontier framework in Chapter 88.
- Real cases from standards Section 7: Hinkley Point C and Sizewell C; plus Vogtle and Barakah.

# Chapter 75. Upstream and midstream oil and gas, including reserve-based lending

## 75.0 Rulings applied

- R-101 (CR-u15-1): Chapter 75 (sec:75.3) is the full home of reserve-based lending and its glossary home; Chapter 2 (ssec:2.4.3) gives the overview.
- R-103: the bank price deck is homed in ssec:75.3.2; Chapter 45 (sec:45.7) treats price-deck rows as model inputs and forward-references here.
- R-104: the reserve tail is homed in Chapter 45 (ssec:45.6.3); Chapter 75 calls the RBL condition the "tail test" as a synonym and does not re-enter it in its glossary; Chapter 36 owns the contract tail.
- R-067: field life cover ratio and the RBL use of loan-life cover are homed in ssec:75.3.2; LLCR itself stays with Chapter 35 (ssec:35.3.1).
- R-025: reserves and resources classifications are homed in Chapter 12 (ssec:12.1.1); Chapter 48 (ssec:48.3.3) owns reserves diligence; Chapter 75 owns their use in the borrowing base.
- R-048: the transporter's contract (reservation and commodity charges, ship-or-pay) is homed in Chapter 21 (sec:21.3); Chapter 75 applies it to midstream revenue models.
- R-114: production sharing contract is always spelled out (PSC means public sector comparator).
- R-015: decommissioning obligations and security are owned by sec:65.2; Chapter 75 owns their effect on borrowing-base capacity.
- R-113: title as in `architecture.md`.
- R-105: Case Bible Part 6 row as amended by Annex P 2.2 (add Tomasz; figures P-F46 and P-F59) and Annex P 1.8 and 4.13; sequencing defect 1: the brief's provisional P-F37 and P-F38 become P-F59 and P-F46.

## 75.1 Header

- Chapter: 75
- Title: Upstream and midstream oil and gas, including reserve-based lending
- File: `chapters/75-upstream-and-midstream-oil-and-gas.tex`
- Label: `ch:75`
- Target length: 11,000 to 13,000 words (raised in round 1: about 6,500 upstream and RBL, 4,500 midstream; the target is a floor for the core teaching of each sector, not a cap)
- Dominant expert lens: the RBL banker and reserves engineer (borrowing base, price decks, redeterminations), paired with the finance lawyer for the facility mechanics and the host-government fiscal regime. The midstream half takes the lens of the pipeline lender and the transit-state government.

## 75.2 Purpose and capability statement

Purpose. Oil and gas fields are among the oldest assets financed against their own output (production payments came before project finance had a name, and BP's 1972 Forties financing of USD 945 million from 66 banks is the usual North Sea milestone, from `h-north-sea-field-finance`; history told in Chapter 3), yet most upstream debt today is not classic project finance: it is reserve-based lending, a revolving facility whose size is recalculated twice a year from the reserves in the ground and a bank price deck. Midstream assets, pipelines, processing plants and storage, sit closer to classic project finance: long ship-or-pay or regulated tariffs, long tenors, and the risk that the molecules stop coming. This chapter teaches how upstream economics and fiscal regimes shape cash flow, how an RBL borrowing base is computed and redetermined and what a deficiency does to a borrower, how midstream assets are paid and financed, and where the boundary between upstream and midstream risk sits, using Jubilee, the Trans Adriatic Pipeline, the Chad–Cameroon pipeline and the Case P gas chain as the anchors.

What you will be able to do:
- Compute an RBL borrowing base from a reserves profile, price deck and cover ratios, and its change on redetermination (Capability 5, Capability 13).
- Read a production sharing contract's fiscal terms and compute the contractor's and the state's take (Capability 3, Capability 13).
- Structure the financing of a pipeline on ship-or-pay or cost-of-service revenue and test its throughput risk against the reserves behind it (Capability 5, Capability 7).
- Decide, as an RBL lender, how to respond to a borrowing-base deficiency in a falling market (Capability 11, Capability 15).

## 75.3 Concepts owned and concepts assumed

### Owned

1. Upstream oil and gas economics for financiers: exploration, appraisal, development and production phases; capex front-loading; decline curves; price exposure; why upstream development is mostly equity-financed and producing assets are financed by RBL.
2. Host-government fiscal regimes for upstream: concessions with royalty and tax, and production sharing contracts (always spelled out) with cost oil, profit oil, cost-recovery limits and state participation; the state's take; stabilization (legal side in Chapter 54).
3. Reserve-based lending in full (Chapter 2 gave the overview): borrowing base assets; reserves categories used in the base (classifications homed in ssec:12.1.1; how RBLs use 1P, PDP-heavy or 2P bases and how the PRMS and SEC five-year rules limit undeveloped reserves, from `t-reserves-codes`); the two documentary families, US proved-reserve facilities redetermined from a reserve report at the agent's price deck and international model-based facilities that can credit probable reserves and fields in development (`t-rbl`); bank price deck; NPV discount rate; cover ratios over loan life and field life; tail requirement; semiannual redetermination; borrowing-base deficiency and cure; amortizing commitments; hedging requirements; LC sublimits; decommissioning security; Framework 75.1, the borrowing base walk (original).
4. Other upstream financing structures and why each exists: field development finance as it is actually done, through partner-level model-based RBLs plus vessel finance rather than one field-level non-recourse loan (Jubilee, `upstream-field-pf`); prepayment and offtake-linked financing to national oil companies and states (Chad and Glencore, Kurdistan, Angola, `commodity-prepay`); streams and royalties (instrument owned by Chapter 21).
5. Upstream decommissioning (abandonment) obligations and security as a lender issue: UK section 29 liability that survives a sale, decommissioning security agreements sized as post-tax cost plus a risk margin less the post-tax value of remaining reserves, letters of credit that consume RBL capacity, and decommissioning relief deeds (`t-upstream-decom-security`; principles in sec:65.2).
6. Midstream asset types and their economics: gas and oil transmission pipelines, gathering and processing, storage, export terminals and FSOs; throughput and reserves dependence.
7. Midstream revenue models: ship-or-pay transportation contracts (contract form owned by Chapter 21), cost-of-service regulated tariffs, negotiated tariffs, throughput-linked fees; Example 75.4.
8. Cross-border pipelines: host government agreements in each state, transit risk, regulatory exemptions that make ship-or-pay contracting possible (TAP's 2013 EU exemption), revenue management conditions (Chad–Cameroon), sanctions on a named asset (Nord Stream 2).
9. The throughput credit test (Framework 75.2, original): reserves behind the shippers, shipper credit, contract tenor against debt tenor, tariff reset risk.
10. Financing terms for upstream and midstream: verified RBL terms from US and international filings (`t-rbl`: Talos 2026 and Kosmos 2025 and 2011), with generic advance rates and price-deck numbers never stated; midstream deal anchors (TAP's 2018 tranche structure, Chad–Cameroon); LNG-export covenant packages (`t-market-norms`) as the bridge to Chapter 76; indicative ranges under D-011 only where no anchor exists, labeled and flagged for reviewer sign-off.
11. Modeling specifics: production profiles by reserves category, price decks, fiscal-regime calculations, borrowing-base computation, pipeline tariff and throughput models (cross-reference Chapter 45 for commodity-linked revenue).
12. Sector clause: Clause 75.1 (borrowing-base redetermination and deficiency cure in an RBL facility), with the verified Talos mechanics as the landing reference.

## 75.4 Locked table of contents

Opening (no heading). See 75.5.

`\section*{What you will be able to do}`.

**75.1 The economics of oil and gas fields** (`sec:75.1`)
- 75.1.1 Phases and who funds them (`ssec:75.1.1`). Exploration and appraisal (equity), development (equity, sometimes project finance or prepayment), production (RBL). Exhibit 75.1 (phase, risk, typical capital).
- 75.1.2 Decline, price, and the shape of field cash flow (`ssec:75.1.2`). Production profiles, plateau and decline; price exposure; why the same field supports less debt each year.

**75.2 Fiscal regimes and the state's share** (`sec:75.2`)
- 75.2.1 Concessions, royalties, and taxes (`ssec:75.2.1`). Royalty-and-tax regimes against production sharing, using Annex P 4.13's license-and-royalty terms for Sombé West only in the installment.
- 75.2.2 Production sharing contracts (`ssec:75.2.2`). Cost oil, cost-recovery limit, profit oil split, state participation, income tax; Example 75.1.
- 75.2.3 Fiscal terms and lenders (`ssec:75.2.3`). How fiscal terms change the borrowing base; stabilization and change in law (cross-reference Chapters 17 and 54); the obsolescing bargain in upstream (pointer to Chapter 60 and Chad–Cameroon's 2023 nationalization).

**75.3 Reserve-based lending** (`sec:75.3`)
- 75.3.1 How a reserve-based loan differs from project finance (`ssec:75.3.1`). A revolving or amortizing facility sized by a borrowing base over a portfolio of producing and near-producing fields; how it differs from project finance (Chapter 2 overview, now in full); the two documentary families from `t-rbl`: the US family (Talos Energy's January 20, 2026 amended and restated facility with JPMorgan as agent: borrowing base and commitments of USD 700 million, a USD 250 million LC sublimit, maturity the earlier of January 20, 2030 and a springing date 91 days before its junior notes mature) and the international family (Kosmos Energy's Ghana and Equatorial Guinea facility, redetermined every March and September on the NPV of forecast cash flows and capex reduced by agreed percentages).
- 75.3.2 Computing the borrowing base (`ssec:75.3.2`). Reserves profile and the category it rests on (`t-reserves-codes`: PRMS 2018 categories by chance of commerciality and uncertainty; the SEC's 12-month average first-day-of-month price differs from a bank price deck, which in the US facility is the agent's forward curve), bank price deck, opex, capex, taxes and royalties, NPV at the facility discount rate, cover ratios on loan life and field life (Kosmos defines FLCR as the NPV of net cash flow through depletion plus the NPV of certain capex, divided by loans outstanding, and LLCR on the same numerator only to final maturity), the tail test (synonym of the reserve tail, ssec:45.6.3); Example 75.2; Framework 75.1; Equation 75.1 (borrowing base as the minimum of the cover-ratio tests). No generic advance rate (such as a share of PV-9 of proved developed producing reserves) and no bank price-deck number is stated as market fact (Do not state).
- 75.3.3 Redetermination and deficiency (`ssec:75.3.3`). Semiannual redetermination, taught on the verified Talos mechanics (`t-rbl`): reserve reports as of December 31 (by approved independent engineers) and June 30 (in-house allowed), new bases effective about April 30 and October 31, one interim redetermination each way, increases approved by lenders holding 100% of commitments and decreases by 66⅔%, 15 days to respond with silence as consent, automatic reductions for terminated hedges or disposals above 5% of the base, and a borrower's right to elect a lower base; reductions follow lower reserves, cuts to the lender's price deck and production; deficiency cure options (repay within 30 days, mortgage more properties within 30 days, four equal monthly instalments starting within 30 days, or a combination, elected within 10 days); Example 75.3; Clause 75.1 (variants a sponsor-friendly and b lender-friendly; no c variant because no third party is involved, as the style sheet permits), whose landing paragraph uses the Talos cure package as the verified reference.
- 75.3.4 Hedging, LCs, and decommissioning security (`ssec:75.3.4`). Hedging requirements tied to the borrowing base (cross-reference sec:37.7; Talos's automatic reduction when relied-on hedges are terminated); LC sublimits for decommissioning and other obligations (Talos USD 250 million); abandonment security as a claim on borrowing-base capacity, from `t-upstream-decom-security`: UK Petroleum Act 1998 section 29 notices can reach current and former licensees and parents and usually survive a sale; co-venturers post LCs, cash or on-demand bonds from A-rated institutions under decommissioning security agreements sized as post-tax decommissioning cost plus a risk margin less the post-tax value of remaining reserves, and parent company guarantees are not accepted as DSA security; decommissioning relief deeds (Finance Act 2013) allow post-tax sizing, and from November 26, 2025 DRD payments cannot be made by reference to the Energy Profits Levy. Lesson: the LC that secures decommissioning uses the same borrowing-base capacity that funds production.
- 75.3.5 Field development finance, prepayments, and streams (`ssec:75.3.5`). Landmark: Jubilee, Ghana (`upstream-field-pf`). The field was unitized on July 13, 2009 and reached first oil on November 28, 2010 through a MODEC-built FPSO; each partner financed its own share. Kosmos, with no production, funded its share with senior and junior commercial facilities including IFC, secured on the shares of its Ghana subsidiary and governed by borrowing-base tests on a forecast cash flow model: USD 1.25 billion from August 23, 2010 (USD 950 million senior, USD 200 million junior, USD 100 million IFC), refinanced in March 2011 by a USD 2.0 billion facility at LIBOR plus 3.25% to 4.75% with FLCR at least 1.30x and LLCR at least 1.10x. The FPSO was a second layer: the partners advanced funds to MODEC, MODEC's long-term financing was suspended in 2011 during an anti-corruption investigation, and in December 2011 the Jubilee Unit bought the vessel for USD 754.5 million. Lesson: "project-financed upstream" usually means partner-level model-based RBLs plus vessel finance, and a contractor's financing can fail onto the field partners (cross-reference ssec:76.8.4 for the FPSO side). Not stated: total Phase 1 cost, a single field-level loan, IFC's total investment, or the MODEC investigation's outcome (Do not state). Then prepayments (`commodity-prepay`): repayment from cargoes the lender lifts gives de facto seniority; Chad's USD 600 million of 2013 oil sales advances from Glencore at 6.9% plus a 2.75% fee and SHT's 2014 advance of about USD 1.4 billion became unsustainable when prices fell, were rescheduled in 2015 and restructured in 2018 with a price-linked cash sweep and a deferral right capped at USD 75 million, and were treated again under the G20 Common Framework in 2022; Angola's about USD 13 billion of oil-collateralized loans led to an IMF zero ceiling on new collateralized debt. Lesson: a commercially rational prepay can trigger a sovereign restructuring. Streams and royalties are cross-referenced to Chapter 21. Not stated: Kurdistan prepay counterparties, exact 2015 and 2018 Chad terms, or Angola's individual loan terms (Do not state).

**75.4 Midstream assets and how they are paid** (`sec:75.4`)
- 75.4.1 Pipelines, processing, and storage (`ssec:75.4.1`). Asset types; throughput dependence; Exhibit 75.2 (TikZ chain: field, processing, pipeline, export terminal or power plant; contracts on each link; at most 10 nodes).
- 75.4.2 Ship-or-pay, cost of service, and negotiated tariffs (`ssec:75.4.2`). Revenue models (the transporter's contract applied from sec:21.3); Example 75.4; Equation 75.2 (cost-of-service revenue requirement).
- 75.4.3 The throughput credit test (`ssec:75.4.3`). Framework 75.2.

**75.5 Cross-border pipelines** (`sec:75.5`)
- 75.5.1 Two states, one asset, and the TAP financing (`ssec:75.5.1`). Host government agreements in each state, transit fees and transit risk. Landmark: the Trans Adriatic Pipeline (`tap-pipeline`), the roughly 877 to 878 km final section of the Southern Gas Corridor through Greece, Albania and the Adriatic to Italy. Revenue rested on long-term ship-or-pay contracts with Shah Deniz II shippers, made possible by the European Commission's May 16, 2013 exemption from third-party access, tariff regulation and ownership unbundling (medium confidence; the writer attributes it). Financial close in December 2018, announced January 11, 2019 as EUR 3.9 billion; third-party senior debt of EUR 3,765 million with a 16.5-year door-to-door tenor, in tranches the reader can sum: EIB EUR 700 million with an EFSI guarantee, EBRD A loan EUR 500 million and B loan EUR 500 million, ECA-covered facilities from Bpifrance (EUR 450 million), Euler Hermes (EUR 280 million) and SACE (EUR 700 million), and an uncovered commercial loan of EUR 635 million; 17 commercial banks; shareholders funded all costs until close; commercial operations from November 15, 2020. Lesson: a regulatory exemption creates the contracted revenue, and a layered DFI, ECA and commercial structure spreads the EBRD's preferred-creditor umbrella over commercial banks through the B loan. Not stated: total capex, an EIB figure of EUR 1.5 billion, pricing, gearing or covenants (Do not state).
- 75.5.2 The Chad–Cameroon pipeline as a project-financed export system (`ssec:75.5.2`). Field system 100% consortium equity (USD 2,648 million to 2005); export system USD 2,169 million: ECA-supported loans USD 400 million (US Ex-Im 200, COFACE 200), IFC USD 200 million (A 100, B 100 with 18 banks), consortium equity and sponsor loans USD 1,450 million, Chad equity USD 49 million and Cameroon equity USD 70 million funded by IBRD and EIB loans; planned USD 400 million bond not issued; oil flowed July 2003, more than a year early; cost growth from USD 3.72 billion to USD 4.8 billion by 2005 (USD 6.5 billion by 2008) (`chad-cameroon`). Lesson: the pipeline lenders took a narrower risk than the upstream equity; the revenue-management story is told in Chapters 50 and 60 and is only pointed to here.
- 75.5.3 Nord Stream 2 and sanctions on a pipeline (`ssec:75.5.3`). Corporate-style sponsor financing (five Western energy companies lending 50% of the EUR 9.5 billion budget); write-offs after February 2022 (Uniper EUR 987 million impairment; Wintershall Dea about EUR 1 billion) (`sanctions-2022`). Lesson for lenders: a physically complete pipeline is worth what its certification and sanctions status allow; political angle in Chapter 60.

**75.6 Financing terms for upstream and midstream** (`sec:75.6`)
- 75.6.1 RBL terms in US and international facilities (`ssec:75.6.1`). Verified terms from `t-rbl`, each attributed and dated: Kosmos (September 2025 redetermination approving the full USD 1.35 billion facility; USD 1.2 billion drawn at December 31, 2025; final maturity December 31, 2029 with amortization from April 1, 2027; FLCR at least 1.50x; LLCR at least 1.10x through September 30, 2027 and 1.30x after; interest cover at least 2.25x; net debt to EBITDAX at most 3.50x, relaxed by amendment to between 4.0x and 4.5x for 2025 and 2026 test dates; margin 4.00% to 5.50% over term SOFR stepping up with time; commitment fees of 30% of margin on available and 20% on unavailable commitments); Kosmos 2011 (FLCR 1.30x, LLCR 1.10x, LIBOR plus 3.25% to 4.75%; technical and modeling banks Société Générale and Crédit Agricole CIB); Talos (borrowing base USD 700 million; a margin that rises with utilization; under the facility in place through 2025, mortgages over at least 85% of oil and gas assets, a debt to EBITDAX covenant and a current ratio of at least 1.0x). Parameters with no verified figure (the facility discount rate, the tail requirement, advance rates) are taught as mechanisms and printed only as indicative under D-011, labeled with market and period and signed off by the domain reviewer; Example 75.2's ratios are labeled Illustrative facility terms.
- 75.6.2 Midstream and pipeline financing terms (`ssec:75.6.2`). Verified anchors: TAP's EUR 3,765 million and 16.5-year tenor (`tap-pipeline`); Chad–Cameroon's IFC A/B and ECA structure; LNG-export covenant packages as the nearest verified midstream-style covenants (`t-market-norms`: 1.10x historical DSCR covenants, 1.20x distribution tests, six-month DSRAs, seven-year bank mini-perms), with a one-sentence forward reference to Chapter 76.
- Exhibit 75.3: upstream and midstream financing structures compared (US RBL, international RBL, partner-level development finance at Jubilee, NOC prepayment, pipeline project finance at TAP), with source and date per row.

**75.7 Modeling upstream and midstream** (`sec:75.7`)
- 75.7.1 Production, prices, and the fiscal regime (`ssec:75.7.1`). Profiles by reserves category, price deck, production sharing contract waterfall; cross-reference sec:45.7.
- 75.7.2 The borrowing-base model (`ssec:75.7.2`). Layout and cell logic of Example 75.2 in Excel (excel block: production row, price row, revenue, royalty, opex, capex, tax, cash flow, discount factor at mid-year, NPV loan life, NPV field life, borrowing base).
- 75.7.3 Pipeline throughput and tariff models (`ssec:75.7.3`). Reserved capacity, actual throughput, tariff reset, shipper default scenario.

## 75.5 Opening

Real deal moment from `upstream-field-pf`: August 23, 2010, three months before first oil at Jubilee off Ghana. Kosmos Energy, a private equity-backed company with no production, raises its commercial debt facilities to USD 1.25 billion (USD 950 million senior, USD 200 million junior, USD 100 million from IFC) to fund its share of a deepwater field that has never produced a barrel. Two paragraphs: the lenders are not relying on a plant or a contract but on a forecast of oil still in the reservoir, valued on their own model and re-tested every June and December; by the end of 2010 about USD 1.05 billion is drawn and the borrowing base, not the commitment, limits what Kosmos can draw. Close with the chapter's question: how can a loan secured on oil still in the ground be sized, and why can it shrink, or grow, without a single barrel changing? (No Phase 1 total cost, no single field-level loan, no IFC total; see `upstream-field-pf` Do not state.) Example 75.3 then shows the shrinking side on Illustrative numbers.

## 75.6 Worked examples

**Example 75.1. Who takes what under a production sharing contract in Equatorial Guinea** (`ex:75.1`, Illustrative; Section 75.2.2). Setting: Rio Muni Offshore Ltd (fictional), contractor on an offshore block in Equatorial Guinea in 2025 under illustrative terms (not the country's model contract). The field produces 42,000 bbl/d for a year at USD 71.50/bbl: gross revenue USD 1,096.1 million. Royalty 8%: USD 87.7 million. Cost recovery limited to 60% of revenue after royalty; recoverable costs incurred USD 612.0 million; cost oil USD 605.0 million (USD 7.0 million carried forward). Profit oil USD 403.4 million, split 55% state (USD 221.8 million) and 45% contractor (USD 181.5 million). Contractor income tax 30% on its profit oil: USD 54.5 million. Contractor cash entitlement USD 732.1 million (cost oil plus profit oil less tax); state take USD 364.0 million (royalty, profit oil, tax), 74.1% of revenue after cost recovery. At USD 55.00/bbl (Exercise 75.8): gross USD 843.1 million; cost oil USD 465.4 million (USD 146.6 million carried forward); profit oil USD 310.3 million; contractor USD 563.2 million; state USD 280.0 million. Lesson: the cost-recovery limit makes the contractor's cash, and so its borrowing base, more sensitive to price than gross revenue suggests; lenders model the production sharing contract, not the field.

**Example 75.2. Computing a borrowing base for a West African producer** (`ex:75.2`, Illustrative; Section 75.3.2). Setting: Cape Coast Oil Ltd (fictional), an independent with three producing fields offshore Ghana under an illustrative royalty-and-tax regime (not Ghana's actual fiscal terms), negotiating an international-style RBL with a fictional technical bank in October 2024. The producer's borrowing-base assets: production (thousand bbl/d) by year 1 to 9: 24.6, 27.9, 26.2, 22.8, 19.1, 15.7, 12.4, 9.6, 7.1 (total 60.37 million bbl). Bank base price deck (USD/bbl): 68.0, 66.0, 65.0, 65.0, 65.0, 66.3, 67.6, 69.0, 70.4. Royalty 10% of revenue; opex USD 14.20/bbl; capex (USD million) 212.0, 96.0, 41.0, 18.0, 12.0, 8.0, 6.0, 4.0, 38.5 (the last including partial abandonment); tax 35% on revenue less royalty, opex and capex (capex expensed in the year incurred, a simplification stated in the text). Annual post-tax cash flow (USD million): 136.5, 236.8, 248.7, 227.9, 192.9, 164.2, 133.3, 106.5, 57.8. NPV at 10% with mid-year discounting. Tests: loan-life cover 1.30x on NPV to final maturity; field-life cover 1.50x on NPV of all remaining reserves; tail test: reserves remaining at final maturity at least 25% of the total (all three are Illustrative facility terms, not market norms). With a six-year final maturity: NPV loan life USD 917.5 million (borrowing base 705.8 by loan life); NPV field life USD 1,067.1 million (711.4 by field life); but the tail is 17.6% of reserves, so the tail test fails. With a five-year final maturity: NPV loan life USD 820.3 million, borrowing base by loan life USD 631.0 million, field life still 711.4, tail 27.1% (passes); borrowing base USD 631.0 million. Lesson: the tail test, not the cover ratio, set the tenor, and the tenor then set the borrowing base. Equation 75.1: $\mathrm{BB} = \min\left(\frac{\mathrm{NPV}_{\mathrm{LL}}}{c_{\mathrm{LL}}}, \frac{\mathrm{NPV}_{\mathrm{FL}}}{c_{\mathrm{FL}}}\right)$ subject to the tail condition, with Excel `=MIN(NPV_LL/LLCR_min, NPV_FL/FLCR_min)` (writer converts to cell references).

**Example 75.3. Cape Coast Oil's spring 2025 redetermination after the price deck falls** (`ex:75.3`, Illustrative; Section 75.3.3). The technical bank cuts the deck by 18% across all years for the April 2025 redetermination; volumes, costs and terms unchanged. With a five-year maturity: NPV loan life USD 574.2 million; field life USD 758.9 million; borrowing base USD 441.7 million (by loan life; the field-life test gives USD 506.0 million). Drawn USD 607.4 million: deficiency USD 165.7 million. The writer walks through the cure options (prepay within a cure period, add assets to the borrowing base, pledge cash, amortize the deficiency in instalments) as drafted in Clause 75.1, and shows that at a 1.40x loan-life ratio (Exercise 75.6) the pre-cut base would have been USD 585.9 million. With a six-year maturity and the same cut the base would have been USD 498.2 million, but that tenor fails the tail test, which the writer notes.

**Example 75.4. A cost-of-service tariff on a US Gulf Coast pipeline** (`ex:75.4`, Illustrative; Section 75.4.2). Setting: Sabinal Gas Transmission LLC (fictional), an interstate gas pipeline in Texas and Louisiana filing a cost-of-service rate case in 2025; the numbers are the example's. Rate base USD 1,163.8 million, allowed return 9.25%, straight-line depreciation over 30 years and opex USD 38.7 million: revenue requirement USD 107.65 million (return) + 38.79 (depreciation) + 38.70 (opex) = USD 185.14 million. Reserved capacity 640,000 MMBtu a day: tariff USD 0.7926/MMBtu if fully reserved; USD 0.9110/MMBtu if only 87% is under ship-or-pay contracts and the regulator lets the tariff recover the full requirement from contracted shippers. At an allowed return of 8.25% (Exercise 75.9): requirement USD 173.51 million; tariff USD 0.7428/MMBtu. Lesson: cost of service protects the return on the asset, not the volume; the lender's question is whether the regulator will spread uncontracted capacity costs over the shippers who remain. Equation 75.2: $R = \mathrm{RB} \times r_a + \mathrm{Dep} + \mathrm{Opex}$.

## 75.7 Walkthrough

**Walkthrough: a borrowing-base redetermination from reserves report to new commitment** (`sec:75.8`). The artifact is the sequence of documents in Cape Coast Oil's April 2025 redetermination (Exhibit 75.4, an Illustrative timetable and document list, with a column showing the verified US equivalents from the Talos facility in `t-rbl`, such as the December 31 independent reserve report, the April 30 effective date, the 100% and 66⅔% voting thresholds and the 15-day response period: borrower's reserves report or update; the technical bank's review; the bank price deck; the borrowing-base model; the technical bank's proposal; the lenders' vote; the redetermination notice; deficiency notice if any). Steps:
1. Read the reserves update: changes by field and category, with reasons (production, revisions, new wells).
2. Read the new bank price deck and compare with the previous one.
3. Rerun the borrowing-base model (Example 75.2) with both changes separately, so the borrower and lenders see which moved the base.
4. Apply the cover-ratio and tail tests.
5. Apply any facility caps (commitments, amortization schedule).
6. Compare the new base with drawn amounts and LCs.
7. Prepare the notice and, if needed, the deficiency cure plan under Clause 75.1.
8. Note the voting rule for increases and decreases (unanimity for increases and two-thirds for decreases in the Talos facility; cross-reference sec:51.7 for amendments and voting).

## 75.8 Frameworks

**Framework 75.1: Borrowing base walk** (`fw:borrowing-base-walk`; original). Purpose: explain any change in a borrowing base by its causes. Steps: (1) start from the previous base; (2) roll forward one period (production taken, debt amortized); (3) apply reserves revisions; (4) apply the price-deck change; (5) apply cost and fiscal changes; (6) apply cover-ratio, tail and cap tests; (7) end at the new base and attribute the difference to each step. When to use: every redetermination, and when negotiating a deficiency cure. Demonstrated on Examples 75.2 and 75.3.

**Framework 75.2: Throughput credit test** (`fw:throughput-credit-test`; original). Purpose: test whether a midstream asset's revenue will survive its debt. Steps: (1) list contracted shippers and their reserved capacity; (2) for each, identify the reserves behind its volumes and their life against debt tenor; (3) assess each shipper's credit and its alternatives (other routes, other markets); (4) compare contract tenors with debt tenor; (5) identify tariff reset or regulatory review dates and the regulator's treatment of uncontracted capacity; (6) for cross-border lines, add transit-state and sanctions risk; (7) state the residual volume risk to debt. When to use: any pipeline, processing plant or terminal financing. Demonstrated on Example 75.4, Chad–Cameroon and the Case P installment.

## 75.10 Running-case installment

**Case P: Sombé West's lenders and the GCK pipeline** (`sec:75.9`). Case Bible Part 6, Chapter 75 row as amended by Annex P 2.2: story dates 2017 and 2023; characters Félix Adandé (SNHK Commercial Director) and Tomasz Wierzbicki (approved by Annex P 2.1); inputs: reserves 1,140 bcf, Annex P 1.8 and 4.13; figures P-F59 and P-F46.

The first sentence of the installment states the two dates. Two short beats.

Beat 1 (`casescene`, "SNHK headquarters, Dabakro, November 2017", the month the GSA and GTA were signed). Félix and Tomasz after signing: Félix explains why he fought for the 80% take-or-pay and the 22-year term, because Halbeck Energy's lenders (operator, 65%) sized the reserve-based loan they signed in October 2017 on Sombé West's sales; he fears Halbeck walking away (character sheet). Tomasz asks what happens to Bélanou if Halbeck's banks cut their lending. No numbers beyond inputs (2P reserves of 1,140 bcf certified in 2017; production from 2019; 72 m water depth; Halbeck 65%, SNHK 35%).

Narration after beat 1, Halbeck's RBL (Illustrative, Annex P 4.13): commitment USD 600 million, seven-year tenor, margin 4.25%, borrowing base equal to the NPV at 10% of the P50 (2P) case on the bank price deck divided by 1.30, also tested on a P90 case at 1.00x, semiannual redeterminations; gas sold to SNHK at USD 3.60/MMBtu in 2018 escalating 2.0% a year; condensate on a bank deck of Brent USD 60/bbl in 2017. From Case Bible ledger: P-F59 (shown with P-F46 in Exhibit 75.5, `\casep`), the NPV of Halbeck's 65% share was USD 364.9 million at signing, giving a borrowing base of USD 280.7 million, well below the USD 600 million commitment; P-F59 also gives the gas price (USD 4.62/MMBtu in 2018 terms) at which the base would have reached USD 420 million. The reading applies Framework 75.1: a contracted gas price replaces a price deck for the gas, so volumes, reserves revisions and the condensate deck drive the base. Then Framework 75.2 applied to GCK's 112 km pipeline (68 km offshore, 44 km onshore), reserved capacity 96,500 MMBtu a day, 100% ship-or-pay, reservation charge USD 0.62/MMBtu and commodity charge USD 0.19/MMBtu (2018 prices, escalating 1.5% a year), all passed through to SEKA (Case Bible 1.4): the shipper is the project company, the reserves behind it are Sombé West's (2P coverage is P-F61, shown in Chapter 48 and only cross-referenced here), and the ultimate payer is SEKA. From Case Bible ledger: P-F46, GCK's 2022 revenue from Bélanou's GTA was USD 23.2 million reservation plus USD 5.4 million commodity, USD 28.5 million in total, all passed to SEKA. GCK's own financing (Annex P 1.8): capex USD 410 million, 40% SNHK equity and 60% a USD 246 million 12-year loan from regional banks with UBK, guaranteed by SNHK and secured on the assignment of GCK's ship-or-pay GTAs with Bélanou Power and SEKA, so the pipeline lenders' credit is the throughput credit test plus SNHK's guarantee.

Beat 2 (narration, with an optional short scene, "SNHK headquarters, Dabakro, July 2023"). After the June 29, 2023 tripartite netting agreement (Case Bible 1.9: up to USD 9.0 million a month of SEKA energy-charge arrears set off against the project company's gas payables to SNHK), SNHK receives less cash from Bélanou; Félix worries what that does to SNHK's payments onward to Halbeck and to Halbeck's lenders' view of its receivables at the next redetermination. Chapter 59 told the netting agreement; this beat shows only the upstream consequence. From Case Bible ledger: P-F59, at the 2023 redetermination the NPV was USD 471.4 million and the borrowing base USD 362.7 million, higher than at signing; the writer explains the rise only from Annex P 4.13's inputs (the condensate deck rose from Brent USD 60/bbl to USD 70/bbl and the 2017 to 2019 development spend no longer sits in the NPV); the narration makes the point that the base rose while the cash behind SNHK's payments weakened, the gap between a reserves-based number and counterparty credit that the walkthrough's step 6 warns about.

Ledger figures displayed: P-F59 and P-F46. Inputs cited: Case Bible 1.1 (Sombé West), 1.4 (GSA, GTA), 1.9 (netting); Annex P 1.8 and 4.13.

## 75.11 Practitioner's notebook (outline)

Checklist: reserves report date, author and categories used in the base; production history against forecast; bank price deck and its date; fiscal terms modeled at the level of the production sharing contract; hedging against the base; decommissioning obligations and security; cover-ratio and tail tests; redetermination dates and voting; for midstream: shippers, reserved capacity, contract tenors, reserves behind each shipper, tariff regime and reset dates, transit agreements, sanctions screening of counterparties.

Red flags: a borrowing base dominated by one undeveloped field; reserves report older than the last price cycle; fiscal terms under renegotiation; tail test close to failing; decommissioning security due soon; pipeline contracts shorter than debt; a single shipper whose reserves end before maturity; a cross-border line with no transit agreement in one state.

Rules of thumb and their limits: RBL capacity falls every period as reserves are produced, so amortizing commitments are normal; a higher price deck raises the base less than proportionally when cost recovery under a production sharing contract and tax bite (Example 75.1); cost-of-service tariffs protect return, not volume.

Common mistakes: computing the base on gross field cash flow instead of the borrower's entitlement under the production sharing contract; ignoring abandonment cost at the end of the profile; using a single-year price instead of the deck; treating ship-or-pay as covering throughput after the shipper's reserves run out.

Questions experts ask: What moved the base: price, reserves or time? How much of the base is undeveloped reserves? What does the state take at USD 55 oil? Who ships on this pipeline in the last five years of the loan, and from which field?

## 75.12 Judgment drill

The situation. You are the technical bank and agent on a USD 900 million RBL for an independent producer with four producing fields offshore West Africa (Illustrative; fictional borrower), in May 2025. After a price fall, the new deck cuts the borrowing base from USD 812 million to USD 566 million; USD 790 million is drawn. The facility allows a six-month cure. The producer asks the lenders to defer the cure for 12 months and to count an undeveloped discovery in the base, citing a farm-out it expects to sign. Lenders vote next week.

Reasoning: apply Framework 75.1 to show what moved the base (price, not reserves); the request has two parts: time and collateral quality; undeveloped reserves without a sanctioned development plan carry lower confidence and often are excluded or heavily risked; an expert would offer instalment cure over the six-month period with a cash sweep and hedging of a large part of next year's production at current prices, consider including the discovery only at a heavy risking factor and only after the development is sanctioned and funded, tighten information covenants, and keep the right to accelerate on any further deficiency.

What would change the answer: a signed farm-out with a creditworthy partner funding development (discovery can enter at a risked value); forward prices recovering above the deck (less pressure, same mechanics); hedges already in place covering the cash flow (lower risk of a second deficiency); a producer with other unencumbered assets to add.

## 75.13 Exercise set (13 exercises)

Tier 1 (4):
- 75.1 Explain why upstream exploration is equity-financed and producing fields support RBL debt.
- 75.2 Explain how a tail test can bind before a cover ratio does.
- 75.3 Distinguish cost oil and profit oil, and explain why the cost-recovery limit matters to lenders.
- 75.4 Explain why cost-of-service regulation protects return but not throughput.

Tier 2 (6):
- 75.5 Recompute Example 75.2 with a six-year maturity and no tail test, and state the borrowing base (answer: USD 705.8 million).
- 75.6 Recompute the five-year base at a 1.40x loan-life ratio (answer: USD 585.9 million).
- 75.7 Compute the deficiency in Example 75.3 if USD 523.8 million were drawn (answer: USD 82.1 million).
- 75.8 Recompute Example 75.1 at USD 55.00/bbl (answer: contractor USD 563.2 million; state USD 280.0 million; cost carried forward USD 146.6 million).
- 75.9 Recompute Example 75.4 at an allowed return of 8.25% (answer: requirement USD 173.51 million; tariff USD 0.7428/MMBtu fully reserved and USD 0.8537/MMBtu at 87% contracted).
- 75.10 Draft Clause 75.1 variants a and b (redetermination and deficiency cure) and annotate.

Tier 3 (3):
- 75.11 Model task: build Example 75.2 in Excel with a price-deck selector (base deck and an 18% cut) and a tenor selector (five or six years), then a borrowing-base walk (Framework 75.1) from the October 2024 base to the April 2025 base of Example 75.3 (answer: base USD 631.0 million at five years; the price-deck step alone takes it to USD 441.7 million, a fall of USD 189.3 million; the six-year tenor would give USD 705.8 million before the cut and USD 498.2 million after, but fails the tail test at 17.6% of reserves remaining; the walk attributes the whole change to price because volumes, costs and terms are unchanged).
- 75.12 Case problem: apply Framework 75.2 to a two-state export pipeline (Illustrative; a fictional 1,046 km gas export line from a producing state through one transit state, 2026, capex USD 3,184.6 million). Inputs: ship-or-pay revenue USD 386.9 million a year at full contract; opex USD 61.4 million a year; three shippers: A (45% of capacity, reserves behind its volumes last 21 years, contract 20 years, rated BBB), B (30%, reserves 14 years, contract 18 years, BB−), C (25%, reserves 25 years, contract 15 years, A−); debt at 6.85%; target DSCR 1.35x on revenue backed by both reserves and contract. Recommend debt tenor and structure (answer: backed capacity is 100% in years 1 to 14, 70% in year 15 (B's reserves run out; C's contract ends after year 15) and 45% in years 16 to 20; a 14-year level annuity at 1.35x supports USD 2,127.7 million, 66.8% of capex; an 18-year level profile on the same debt service would fall to 0.87x in year 15 and 0.47x in years 16 to 18; sculpting to backed revenue over 18 years supports USD 2,266.5 million; the expert offers 14 years level, or 18 years sculpted with a sweep and a requirement to recontract C, and treats B as the weakest credit).
- 75.13 Case P: using only Case Bible inputs (1.1, 1.4, 1.9; Annex P 1.8 and 4.13) and ledger figures P-F46 and P-F59, write a half-page note explaining how Sombé West's sales contracts and SEKA's 2022 to 2024 arrears connect Bélanou, GCK, SNHK and Halbeck's lenders; no running-case figures computed.

## 75.15 Glossary entries owned

| Term | Abbreviation | Definition | Home section |
|---|---|---|---|
| borrowing base | – | The maximum amount available under a reserve-based loan, recalculated periodically from the value of the borrower's reserves. | 75.3.2 |
| borrowing-base assets | – | The fields and interests whose reserves and cash flows are included in the borrowing-base calculation. | 75.3.2 |
| bank price deck | – | The commodity price forecast set by the lenders and used to value reserves in a borrowing-base calculation. | 75.3.2 |
| redetermination | – | The periodic recalculation of a borrowing base, usually semiannual. | 75.3.3 |
| borrowing-base deficiency | – | The amount by which loans and other utilizations exceed a newly redetermined borrowing base, which the borrower must cure. | 75.3.3 |
| field life cover ratio | FLCR | In reserve-based lending, the NPV of a borrowing base's cash flows over the remaining field life divided by the debt. | 75.3.2 |
| reserve-based lending | RBL | Revolving or amortizing debt to oil and gas producers whose available amount is a borrowing base set by the value of reserves under a bank price deck and redetermined periodically. | 75.3 |
| production sharing contract | – | A contract under which the state owns the resource and a contractor recovers costs and shares profit oil with the state (always spelled out). | 75.2.2 |
| cost oil | – | The share of production a contractor under a production sharing contract receives to recover its costs, usually subject to a cap. | 75.2.2 |
| profit oil | – | Production remaining after cost oil, split between the state and the contractor. | 75.2.2 |
| cost-of-service tariff | – | A regulated tariff set to recover an asset's operating costs, depreciation and an allowed return on its rate base. | 75.4.2 |
| host government agreement | HGA | An agreement between a cross-border pipeline's owners and each host or transit state setting the project's legal and fiscal terms. | 75.5.1 |

Note: the loan life cover ratio used in RBL (`c_LL` in Equation 75.1) is computed on NPV to final maturity like the project-finance LLCR owned by Chapter 35 (ssec:35.3.1); the writer cross-references it and explains only the RBL difference (a revolving balance and a price deck). Removed: tail test, a synonym of the reserve tail homed at ssec:45.6.3 (R-104); the writer uses "tail test" in prose and cross-references the home. Added: reserve-based lending, whose glossary home is sec:75.3 (R-101).

## 75.16 Neighbors

Chapter 74 (Nuclear power) teaches the asset class where construction risk is too large for private balance sheets alone, through five financing models (HPC's CfD, SZC's RAB, Vogtle's regulated utilities and federal guarantees, Barakah's sovereign finance, and state-sponsored SMRs). Chapter 76 (LNG liquefaction, regasification, and FPSOs) continues the gas chain to its most capital-intensive links: liquefaction trains financed on long-term SPAs and fixed fees, ECA-led mega-financings with completion guarantees, regasification terminals and FSRUs, and FPSOs financed on charter contracts with oil companies.
