# Chapter 76. LNG liquefaction, regasification, and FPSOs

## 76.0 Rulings applied

- R-026: liquefaction train (ssec:12.2.1), FSRU (ssec:12.2.2) and FPSO (ssec:12.1.4) are homed in Chapter 12; Chapter 76 uses them without bold and drops them from its glossary; Chapter 76 owns FPSO financing (sec:76.8; sequencing defect 31 asks the canon to correct its note from sec:76.9 to sec:76.8).
- R-050: financial completion is defined in Chapter 26 (ssec:26.5.2); Chapter 61 owns lenders' completion tests in practice (sec:61.11) and the release of sponsor support (sec:61.12); Chapter 76 applies the Ichthys test and drops "financial completion" from its glossary.
- R-054: LNG tolling is owned by Chapter 21 (ssec:21.2.3); Chapter 76 applies it to project structures.
- R-067 (CR-u15-3): Chapter 76 is the glossary home of terminal use agreement (ssec:76.7.1).
- R-101, R-105, R-113: Part XVI ownership; Case Bible Part 6 row with Annex P 2.1 (Devesh Raval asks the investment committee's question) and Annex P 1.7.1 (the GSA-PPA term gap behind MEH's FSRU study); architecture title with serial comma.

## 76.1 Header

- Chapter: 76
- Title: LNG liquefaction, regasification, and FPSOs
- File: `chapters/76-lng-and-fpsos.tex`
- Label: `ch:76`
- Target length: 11,000 to 13,000 words (raised in round 1: about 6,500 on liquefaction, 2,000 on regasification and FSRUs, 3,000 on FPSOs and floating LNG; the target is a floor for the core teaching of each sector, not a cap)
- Dominant expert lens: the ECA and commercial banker who has arranged multi-billion LNG financings with completion guarantees, paired with the finance lawyer (SPA portfolio, completion tests, intercreditor arrangements among ECAs). The government lens enters through host-state participation and security (Mozambique); the contractor lens through LSTK EPC for trains and FPSO lease-and-operate contracts.

## 76.2 Purpose and capability statement

Purpose. LNG liquefaction plants are the largest single assets project finance funds, at USD 10 billion to over USD 20 billion a project, and the structures that finance them are the clearest demonstration of the discipline's logic at scale: long-term sales contracts with creditworthy buyers, LSTK construction by a handful of specialist contractors, sponsor completion guarantees until a financial completion test is passed, and lender groups led by export credit agencies. The chapter teaches the economics of the LNG chain, the three project structures (integrated, merchant or tolling, and US fixed-fee), how lenders test an SPA portfolio, what a completion guarantee and a financial completion test do, and how the same logic finances regasification terminals, FSRUs and FPSOs, where the revenue is a charter rather than a sale.

What you will be able to do:
- Explain how an LNG project's structure (integrated, tolling, fixed-fee) decides what lenders rely on, and test an SPA portfolio's coverage of debt service (Capability 3, Capability 5, Capability 13).
- Read the completion guarantee and financial completion test of an LNG financing and explain when the credit changes character (Capability 6, Capability 10).
- Size and structure the financing of a charter-backed floating asset (FPSO or FSRU), including early termination protection (Capability 5).
- Judge country, security and sanctions risk in an LNG project and the role of ECAs and sponsors in carrying it (Capability 8, Capability 15).

## 76.3 Concepts owned and concepts assumed

### Owned

1. LNG industry economics: liquefaction cost per metric ton of capacity, trains and scale, feed gas, shipping, regasification; how prices are set (oil-indexed, hub-indexed with a fee, spot); the contracted volume threshold for FID.
2. LNG project structures: integrated upstream-to-LNG (PNG LNG, Ichthys, Mozambique LNG), incorporated joint venture and marketing company, merchant or tolling model, US fixed-fee model on Henry Hub (Sabine Pass; SPA mechanics owned by Chapter 21).
3. The SPA portfolio as collateral: contracted share of capacity, buyer credit, tenor match, fixed-fee coverage of opex and debt service, cancellation rights and their effect on lenders (put-option logic taught in Chapter 21, applied here); Framework 76.1 (LNG chain credit map, original).
4. Completion support in LNG as applied from Chapters 26 and 61: several (not joint) sponsor completion guarantees, government backstops of a sponsor's guarantee (JOGMEC at Ichthys), debt service undertakings capped pro rata until lender-validated performance tests (Coral Sul), financial completion tests (Ichthys's 90-day test; the term is defined in ssec:26.5.2), release and the change in credit character; refinancing after completion (Ichthys 2020 and 2022; Barakah-type pattern in Chapter 74).
5. LNG construction risk: LSTK EPC for trains, interface and subcontract risk inside a lump EPC (Ichthys's power-plant subcontract), cost-overrun anatomy (FX, land access, weather: PNG LNG), staging trains to match contracts (Sabine Pass).
6. Country, security and sanctions risk in LNG: frontier onshore sites (Mozambique LNG's 2021 force majeure, restart and re-documentation), sanctions on LNG projects (Arctic LNG 2), ECA withdrawal and replacement with equity.
7. ECA-led mega-financings and the sponsor as co-lender (PNG LNG's ExxonMobil co-loans; Ichthys's USD 4.0 billion senior sponsor loans); lender structure taught in Chapter 29, applied to LNG.
8. Regasification and FSRUs: onshore terminals with terminal use agreements, FSRU charters (fixed daily hire for 5 to 20 years with extension, break and purchase options), their financing by sale-and-leaseback and limited-recourse loans (Excelerate, Höegh, from `fsru-charters`), cost and time trade-offs, import-terminal credit (the buyer's credit as the revenue), redeployment and dispute risk.
9. FPSOs: lease-and-operate contracts with oil companies, day rates with capital and operating elements, firm periods and extension options, early termination fees, charter-backed project finance with sponsor guarantees until completion (SBM Offshore in Brazil and Guyana, from `fpso-financing`); Framework 76.2 (charter-backed credit test, original).
10. Floating LNG: the first project financing of a liquefaction floater (Coral Sul, Mozambique, 2017, from `coral-sul-flng`) and how it combines a single long-term buyer, ECA-heavy debt, sponsor debt service undertakings and cash-flow-only security that excludes the reserves.
11. Financing terms for LNG and floating assets: verified US filings (Port Arthur, Rio Grande, CP2), PNG LNG, Ichthys, Mozambique LNG, Sabine Pass, Coral Sul; verified FPSO terms (tenor following the charter, pricing data points) and FSRU covenants (Moheshkhali); indicative ranges under D-011 only where no anchor exists.
12. Modeling specifics: liquefaction revenue by SPA (fixed fee plus variable), contracted-coverage tests, completion test timing, charter revenue with uptime, off-hire and early termination; cross-reference sec:45.7 for commodity-linked revenue.
13. Sector clause: Clause 76.1 (early termination fee in an FPSO lease-and-operate contract).

## 76.4 Locked table of contents

Opening (no heading). See 76.5.

`\section*{What you will be able to do}`.

**76.1 The economics of LNG** (`sec:76.1`)
- 76.1.1 The chain and where the money goes (`ssec:76.1.1`). Field, pipeline, liquefaction, shipping, regasification; Exhibit 76.1 (TikZ chain with contracts at each link; at most 10 nodes).
- 76.1.2 How LNG is priced (`ssec:76.1.2`). Oil-indexed, hub-indexed plus fee (Sabine Pass: about 115% of Henry Hub plus a fixed fee of USD 2.25 to 3.00/MMBtu; `sabine-pass`), spot; who carries the margin in each.
- 76.1.3 Contracting before FID (`ssec:76.1.3`). Sabine Pass reached about 16 mtpa, 89% of nominal Trains 1 to 4 capacity, before FID (`sabine-pass`); the threshold is computed in Example 76.2 (ssec:76.3.1), after the fixed-fee arithmetic of Example 76.1.

**76.2 Three ways to structure an LNG project** (`sec:76.2`)
- 76.2.1 Integrated projects (`ssec:76.2.1`). Upstream, pipeline and plant under one joint venture with a borrower and marketing company (PNG LNG Global Company LDC; Ichthys LNG Pty Ltd) (`png-lng`, `ichthys`).
- 76.2.2 Tolling and merchant liquefaction (`ssec:76.2.2`). The plant sells capacity, the buyer brings gas; or the plant buys gas and sells LNG at its own risk.
- 76.2.3 The US fixed-fee model (`ssec:76.2.3`). Sabine Pass's design and why it made liquefaction bankable for a developer without upstream; 2020 as the stress test (Cheniere recognized USD 932 million of revenue from cancelled cargoes in the first nine months of 2020, company-wide; `sabine-pass`).
- Exhibit 76.2: the three structures compared (who owns gas, who bears price, what lenders rely on, typical lender group).

**76.3 The SPA portfolio as collateral** (`sec:76.3`)
- 76.3.1 Fixed fees, buyers, and tenor (`ssec:76.3.1`). Example 76.1; Example 76.2 (the contracted volume needed before FID); buyer credit and parent guarantees; tenor match.
- 76.3.2 Cancellation rights and lenders (`ssec:76.3.2`). Applies Chapter 21's put-option logic: the fixed fee survives cancellation; the variable component is a pass-through.
- 76.3.3 The LNG chain credit map (`ssec:76.3.3`). Framework 76.1.

**76.4 Building an LNG plant** (`sec:76.4`)
- 76.4.1 LSTK contracts and the few contractors who sign them (`ssec:76.4.1`). Sabine Pass's LSTK with Bechtel (`sabine-pass`); why lenders want proven process and contractor. Verified security terms from `t-construction-norms`: Bechtel's Rio Grande LNG contracts require a standby letter of credit of 10% of the contract price, stepping down to 5% at substantial completion, plus a parent guarantee.
- 76.4.2 Cost growth and its anatomy (`ssec:76.4.2`). PNG LNG from about USD 15 billion to about USD 19 billion (currency appreciation, landowner disputes and stoppages, weather and logistics; ExxonMobil's attribution; `png-lng`); Ichthys from USD 34 billion at FID to approximately USD 45 billion, with the combined-cycle power plant subcontract terminated on January 25, 2017 (`ichthys`; no apportionment of the overrun).
- 76.4.3 Completion guarantees and financial completion (`ssec:76.4.3`). PNG LNG sponsor completion guarantees released at financial completion on February 6, 2015; Ichthys several guarantees, JOGMEC backstop of up to USD 2 billion of INPEX's guarantee, 90-day test passed December 7, 2019, financial completion announced December 20, 2019 (`png-lng`, `ichthys`). Cross-reference Chapters 26 and 61 for the instruments and tests.

**76.5 Country, security, and sanctions risk** (`sec:76.5`)
- 76.5.1 Force majeure, restart, and re-documentation at Mozambique LNG (`ssec:76.5.1`). FID June 2019; USD 14.9 billion of senior debt signed July 17, 2020 from eight ECAs, 19 commercial bank facilities and the AfDB (TotalEnergies later cited USD 15.4 billion; both cited with sources); force majeure April 26, 2021 after the Palma attack; lifted November 7, 2025; budget from about USD 15.5 billion to about USD 20.5 billion; UKEF's participation ended December 1, 2025 and Atradius did not reconfirm, about 10% of external financing replaced by equity; full restart January 29, 2026; first LNG targeted 2029 (`mozambique-lng`). Political angle in Chapter 60; here the LNG lesson: onshore frontier sites carry security risk that offshore or brownfield projects avoid, and a multi-year suspension forces re-documentation of every lender's approval. One sentence contrasts the offshore Coral Sul FLNG in the same country, which shipped its first cargo in November 2022 while the onshore project was under force majeure (`coral-sul-flng`; detail in ssec:76.8.5).
- 76.5.2 Arctic LNG 2 under sanctions (`ssec:76.5.2`). One paragraph from `sanctions-2022` (US designation November 2, 2023; EU 2024 prohibition on completing Russian LNG projects; TotalEnergies' 10% transferred to a Novatek subsidiary on August 27, 2026 with a sanctions-contingent right to about USD 1.3 billion of shareholder loans); detail in Chapter 60.

**76.6 Financing LNG** (`sec:76.6`)
- 76.6.1 ECA-led mega-financings (`ssec:76.6.1`). PNG LNG (USD 14 billion signed December 15, 2009; six ECAs about USD 8.3 billion; uncovered banks USD 1.95 billion; ExxonMobil co-loans USD 3.75 billion; EXIM 17.5 years, China Exim 17 years, commercial 15 years; `png-lng`); Ichthys (USD 20 billion on December 18, 2012: ECA direct USD 5.8 billion; ECA-covered USD 5.4 billion; uncovered USD 4.8 billion from 24 banks; senior sponsor loans USD 4.0 billion; about 16 years from first drawdown; `ichthys`). Lender structure owned by Chapter 29; this section reads them as LNG credits.
- 76.6.2 US Gulf Coast bank mini-perms (`ssec:76.6.2`). Port Arthur (March 20, 2023: term loan approximately USD 6.8 billion against approximately USD 13.0 billion capex; SOFR plus 2.00% before completion and 2.25% after; commitment fee 30% of margin; seven-year maturity against a 20-year sculpted profile; at least 60% hedged; six-month DSRA; 1.20x historical and projected distribution test; 1.10x minimum historical DSCR; 1.40x projected DSCR for additional debt), Rio Grande (July 12, 2023: bank loans up to USD 10.3 billion at SOFR plus 2.25%; USD 700 million 6.67% notes due 2033; seven-year bank maturity; 1.10x covenant) and CP2 (July 28, 2025: USD 11.25 billion construction/term loan at SOFR plus 2.25% to 2.75% and a separate USD 2.809 billion holdco equity bridge) (`t-market-norms`). Why lenders accept 50% to 65% gearing in LNG against 80% and more in contracted renewables (the reader computes Port Arthur's ratio in Exercise 76.6).
- 76.6.3 Refinancing after completion (`ssec:76.6.3`). Sabine Pass's move from bank loans to long-dated bonds (`sabine-pass`); Ichthys's June 2020 refinancing of about USD 8.3 billion and 2022 bank and private placement refinancing (`ichthys`).
- Exhibit 76.3: LNG financings compared (verified figures only).

**76.7 Regasification terminals and FSRUs** (`sec:76.7`)
- 76.7.1 Onshore terminals and terminal use agreements (`ssec:76.7.1`). Sabine Pass began as an import terminal with 20-year TUAs with Total and Chevron, each paying about USD 125 million a year whether or not it used the terminal (`sabine-pass`).
- 76.7.2 FSRU charters and their financing (`ssec:76.7.2`). Charter structure, speed and flexibility; Example 76.3. Landmark cases from `fsru-charters`: Excelerate Energy (11 floating regasification terminals at December 31, 2025; about USD 3,315.2 million of minimum contracted terminal-service cash flows with a 5.8-year weighted average remaining term; fixed daily rates set on the investment's return requirement). Its financing forms: the 2016 Experience sale-leaseback of USD 247.5 million, accounted for as a financing because Excelerate must repurchase, now at three-month SOFR plus 3.4% to December 2033, with a three-month debt service reserve and a vessel value of at least 110% of debt; and the June 2017 limited-recourse loans for Moheshkhali LNG in Bangladesh (up to USD 32.8 million and USD 92.8 million, maturing October 2029) requiring a six-month DSRA, a monthly-funded off-hire reserve for drydocks, stand-alone insurance and a quarterly DSCR of at least 1.10x. Charter design from Höegh LNG Partners: the 20-year Lampung charter in Indonesia with extension and purchase options, against which PGN LNG served notice of arbitration on August 2, 2021 seeking annulment or termination; and Höegh Grace in Colombia, a 20-year charter with a 10-year non-cancellable period and mutual penalty-free breaks at years 10 and 15, so a 20-year contract is a 10-year credit. Lesson: lenders to a mobile asset look beyond tenor to termination payments, purchase-option pricing and redeployability. Not stated: day rates for named FSRUs, the Lampung outcome, Golar's financings, or typical FSRU capex and day-rate ranges (Do not state).
- 76.7.3 Import-terminal credit (`ssec:76.7.3`). The importing buyer's credit as the revenue; link to payment security (Chapter 59) for emerging-market importers.

**76.8 FPSOs** (`sec:76.8`)
- 76.8.1 What an FPSO is and who owns it (`ssec:76.8.1`). Owner-operated versus oil-company-owned; lease-and-operate contracts; firm period and options; the FPSO as an asset is described in ssec:12.1.4.
- 76.8.2 Day rates and the charter as collateral (`ssec:76.8.2`). Capital and operating elements; uptime; Example 76.4. Landmark financings from `fpso-financing` (SBM Offshore), two charter archetypes. Brazil: FPSO Sepetiba (Mero; 22.5-year lease and operate contract with Petrobras signed December 11, 2019; a USD 600 million six-month SPC bridge with sponsor guarantees in July 2020; a USD 1.6 billion project loan on September 16, 2021 from 13 banks with NEXI and SACE cover, a 4.3% weighted average cost of debt and 14 to 15 years after completion) and FPSO Alexandre de Gusmão (USD 1.615 billion announced June 20, 2023, 12 banks and three ECAs, about 6.6% weighted average cost, 14 years after completion). Guyana: FPSO Liza Destiny (USD 720 million from 12 banks on December 19, 2017, 10 years after completion at LIBOR plus 1.65%, matching a 10-year lease), Liza Unity (USD 1.14 billion on October 16, 2019, a two-year post-completion tenor "in line with the duration of the charter" at LIBOR plus 1.50%, repaid in full from ExxonMobil Guyana's purchase of about USD 1.26 billion on November 9, 2023) and ONE GUYANA (USD 1.75 billion on July 21, 2022 at SOFR plus 2.2%, repaid from a purchase of about USD 2.32 billion completed February 4, 2026). Lesson: the loan is guaranteed by the sponsors until completion and acceptance and then becomes non-recourse; in a long charter the lender relies on charterer credit and day-rate cover, in a short lease-and-purchase it relies on the purchase price. Not stated: day rates, DSCR covenants, the three ECAs on Alexandre de Gusmão, MODEC's financings, or that Guyana purchase prices equal construction cost (Do not state).
- 76.8.3 Early termination and the lenders (`ssec:76.8.3`). Termination for convenience, fees that track outstanding debt; Clause 76.1 variants a (sponsor-friendly), b (lender-friendly) and c (charterer-friendly), set in a 2026 lease-and-operate contract offshore Brazil with fictional parties, with an indicative landing paragraph (D-011).
- 76.8.4 The charter-backed credit test (`ssec:76.8.4`). Framework 76.2; residual value and redeployment; capital recycling after start-up (SBM's 13.5% sale of the Sepetiba SPCs to China Merchants Financial Leasing on October 24, 2024, and its first sale-and-leaseback, USD 400 million non-recourse for 8 years on FPSO Cidade de Paraty in April 2025); contractor-finance risk seen from the field partners (Jubilee's partners bought their FPSO when MODEC's financing failed, cross-reference ssec:75.3.5).
- 76.8.5 Floating LNG at Coral Sul (`ssec:76.8.5`, appended in round 1). From `coral-sul-flng`: Coral Sul FLNG (about 3.4 mtpa, six subsea wells, Area 4 offshore Mozambique, operated by Eni through Mozambique Rovuma Venture), all LNG sold to BP under a 20-year contract signed October 2016 with a 10-year option; FID June 1, 2017; financial close December 2017 (not 2020), described by Eni as the first project financing for a liquefaction floater, from 15 banks guaranteed by five ECAs and covering 60% of the FLNG cost; Eni's statements give EUR 4,164 million of ECA and bank financing against an EPCIC contract value of EUR 4,673 million; a debt service undertaking during construction, several and capped pro rata (Eni's 25% share up to EUR 1,425 million), to fall away once lenders validated the performance tests, after which the debt is non-recourse, repaid pay-when-paid from LNG sales and secured only on sale cash flows, excluding the gas reserves; the partners funded the state company ENH's share; first cargo November 2022; Coral North FLNG (3.6 mtpa) took FID in October 2025. Lesson: a new asset class was financed by keeping completion risk with the sponsors until lender-tested performance and by securing the lenders only on the vessel's own cash flows. Not stated: a 2020 date, the "USD 4.7 billion" figure, the ECA names, tenor, margin, or the date the undertaking was released (Do not state).

**76.9 Modeling LNG and floating assets** (`sec:76.9`)
- 76.9.1 SPA revenue and coverage tests (`ssec:76.9.1`). Fixed and variable components by buyer; contracted coverage; cancellation scenario.
- 76.9.2 Completion timing and the guarantee release (`ssec:76.9.2`). Modeling the switch from sponsor-guaranteed to project-risk credit.
- 76.9.3 Charter revenue (`ssec:76.9.3`). Day-rate elements, uptime, off-hire reserve for drydocks (Moheshkhali), escalation, early termination fee schedule versus debt balance.

## 76.5 Opening

Real deal moment from `sabine-pass`: in 2020, as COVID-19 collapsed global gas prices, buyers cancelled US LNG cargoes in large numbers and kept paying the fixed fees; Cheniere recognized USD 932 million of revenue from cancelled cargoes in the first nine months of 2020 (company-wide, including Corpus Christi; the writer says so). Two paragraphs contrasting that with Mozambique LNG's position a year later (force majeure in April 2021 after the Palma attack, `mozambique-lng`). Close with the question: why did one project's revenue survive a world with no demand for its product, while another's construction stopped for four years, and what did each set of lenders rely on?

## 76.6 Worked examples

**Example 76.1. Fixed-fee coverage of a Louisiana liquefaction project** (`ex:76.1`, Illustrative; Section 76.3.1). Setting: Calcasieu Bend LNG LLC (fictional), a 12.0 mtpa US Gulf Coast project seeking a bank mini-perm in 2023 with fictional Asian and European buyers. The project has 10.4 mtpa under 20-year SPAs with fixed fees of USD 2.65/MMBtu; conversion 52.0 MMBtu per metric ton (an Illustrative round conversion the writer states). Fixed-fee revenue: 10.4 million t × 52.0 × 2.65 = USD 1,433.1 million a year. Fixed opex USD 318.4 million; annual debt service USD 812.6 million. CFADS from fixed fees alone USD 1,114.7 million; DSCR 1.37x with no margin from variable sales. Lesson: in the fixed-fee model, debt is sized on fixed fees and buyer credit; the variable component is a pass-through. (The writer uses an illustrative fee in the range Sabine Pass's SPAs set and states that the example is not Sabine Pass.)

**Example 76.2. How much Calcasieu Bend must contract before FID** (`ex:76.2`, Illustrative; Section 76.3.1). Same project: contracted volume needed so that fixed fees cover fixed opex plus debt service at a target ratio: at 1.20x, (812.6 × 1.20 + 318.4) / (52.0 × 2.65) = 9.39 mtpa (78.2% of capacity); at 1.40x, 10.57 mtpa (88.1%). At a fee of USD 2.25/MMBtu and 1.20x (Exercise 76.7): 11.06 mtpa (92.2%). Lesson: the fee and the debt quantum together set the contracting threshold; Sabine Pass's 89% before FID sits in this range.

**Example 76.3. FSRU or onshore regasification for a Vietnamese importer** (`ex:76.3`, Illustrative; Section 76.7.2). Setting: PetroDelta Gas JSC (fictional), a state-owned importer in southern Vietnam comparing options in 2026; the charter rate and capex are the example's, not market ranges (`fsru-charters` verifies none). The importer needs 2.6 mtpa (135.2 million MMBtu a year). FSRU: charter USD 128,500/day (USD 46.90 million a year), opex USD 9.6 million, jetty and pipeline USD 212.0 million annualized over 20 years at 9% (USD 23.22 million): USD 79.73 million a year, USD 0.590/MMBtu. Onshore terminal: capex USD 748.0 million annualized over 25 years at 9% (USD 76.15 million) plus opex USD 21.4 million: USD 97.55 million a year, USD 0.722/MMBtu. At 4.0 mtpa (Exercise 76.9): FSRU USD 0.383/MMBtu, onshore USD 0.469/MMBtu. The FSRU stays cheaper until its charter rate exceeds about USD 177,300 a day (Exercise 76.10). Lesson: FSRUs win on speed and flexibility at these inputs; the credit question for either is the importer's ability to pay the charter or tariff (Chapter 59).

**Example 76.4. Financing a Brazilian FPSO on its charter** (`ex:76.4`, Illustrative; Section 76.8.2). Setting: Atlântica FPSO BV (fictional), owner of a unit chartered in 2023 to Southern Cross Energy (fictional, rated A−) for a pre-salt field offshore Brazil; the day rate is the example's (no real FPSO day rate is verified). The FPSO costs USD 1,386.5 million; the charter has a 15-year firm period; day rate USD 468,900 capital element plus USD 131,600 operating element (the operating element covers opex and is passed through); uptime 97%. Capital-element revenue USD 166.01 million a year. Debt amortizing over 12 years at 6.75%, sized at 1.40x on the capital element (Illustrative ratio): annual debt service USD 118.58 million; debt USD 954.5 million (68.8% of cost). Equity USD 432.0 million receives USD 47.43 million a year for 12 years and USD 166.01 million a year for years 13 to 15: pre-tax equity IRR 10.7% before any residual value or extension. Outstanding debt at the end of year 5: USD 644.7 million, the minimum an early termination fee at that date must cover. Lesson: the charter's firm period and its termination fee schedule are the collateral; debt amortizes inside the firm period, and redeployment value is equity's upside.

## 76.7 Walkthrough

**Walkthrough: reading an LNG financing's completion test** (`sec:76.10`). The artifact is Exhibit 76.4, an Illustrative completion test schedule from a 2024 common terms agreement for Kikori Delta LNG (fictional), a two-train integrated project in Papua New Guinea (physical completion of both trains certified by the IE; a continuous operating test of 90 days at a minimum average production rate; a minimum number of cargoes delivered under SPAs; historical and projected DSCR thresholds; all reserve accounts funded; no default; legal and permit conditions). Steps:
1. Read the physical completion conditions and the IE's role (cross-reference Chapter 61).
2. Read the operating test: duration, rate, measurement (Ichthys used a 90-day test measured on cumulative production; `ichthys`).
3. Read the financial conditions: DSCR tests, reserves funded.
4. Identify what is released at financial completion: the sponsors' several completion guarantees and any government backstop (JOGMEC at Ichthys).
5. Identify the long-stop date and what happens if completion is not reached (guarantee called, debt accelerated, or extension by vote).
6. State how the credit changes after completion and why that enables refinancing (Ichthys 2020 and 2022).

## 76.8 Frameworks

**Framework 76.1: LNG chain credit map** (`fw:lng-chain-credit`; original). Purpose: map who owns, contracts and guarantees each link of an LNG chain, and what lenders rely on. Steps: (1) draw the chain (fields, pipeline, liquefaction trains, marketing company, shipping, buyers, regasification); (2) for each link, record owner, contract, counterparty credit and tenor; (3) mark which links are inside the borrower and which are third-party; (4) identify the completion support and its release test; (5) identify the revenue that covers debt service (fixed fee, take-or-pay, marketing margin) and the buyers behind it; (6) list country, security and sanctions exposures by link; (7) state the credit before and after completion. When to use: any LNG financing review. Demonstrated on Exhibit 76.2, PNG LNG, Ichthys and Sabine Pass.

**Framework 76.2: Charter-backed credit test** (`fw:charter-credit-test`; original). Purpose: test whether a charter can carry debt on a floating asset. Steps: (1) charterer credit and parent support; (2) firm period against debt tenor; (3) day-rate split between capital and operating elements and their indexation; (4) uptime and performance deductions; (5) termination for convenience and the fee schedule against outstanding debt; (6) termination for default by the owner and the lenders' step-in or cure rights; (7) residual and redeployment value; (8) political and field risk of the host state. When to use: FPSO, FSRU and other charter-backed assets. Demonstrated on Example 76.4 and Clause 76.1.

|---|---|---|
| `sabine-pass` | Opening; Sections 76.1.2, 76.1.3, 76.2.3, 76.4.1, 76.6.3, 76.7.1 | Fixed-fee SPAs with investment-grade buyers make liquefaction bankable without upstream; contract before FID; stage trains to signed SPAs; refinance construction loans into bonds. |
| `png-lng` | Sections 76.2.1, 76.4.2, 76.4.3, 76.6.1 | Integrated frontier LNG: jointly owned borrower and marketing company, ECA-led club negotiating first in a closed market, completion guarantees until financial completion, cost overrun from FX, land and logistics. |
| `ichthys` | Sections 76.2.1, 76.4.2, 76.4.3, 76.6.1, 76.6.3, walkthrough | Interface risk inside a lump EPC can drive a multi-billion overrun; several completion guarantees protect lenders; the 90-day completion test changes the credit and enables refinancing. |
| `mozambique-lng` | Opening; Section 76.5.1 | Onshore frontier sites carry security risk; force majeure suspends obligations but not costs; restart requires re-approval by every lender and replacement of withdrawn ECAs with equity. |
| `sanctions-2022` | Section 76.5.2 | Sanctions can strand an LNG project under construction and reduce foreign partners' exit to contingent value. |
| `t-market-norms` | Section 76.6.2 | Full US LNG term-sheet skeleton from SEC filings. |
| `fpso-financing` | Sections 76.8.2 to 76.8.4; Framework 76.2 | Tenor follows the charter (14 to 15 years after completion on 22.5-year Petrobras charters; two years on Guyana leases repaid from the purchase price); sponsor guarantees until completion, non-recourse after; capital recycling by stake sales and sale-and-leaseback. |
| `fsru-charters` | Section 76.7.2; Example 76.3; Case P narration | A 20-year charter can be a 10-year credit (break options); limited-recourse FSRU loans carry a DSRA, an off-hire reserve and a 1.10x DSCR; charterer disputes and redeployment are the residual risks. |
| `coral-sul-flng` | Sections 76.5.1, 76.8.5 | The first FLNG project financing: a single long-term buyer, ECA-heavy debt, capped several sponsor undertakings until lender-tested performance, and security on sale cash flows that excludes the reserves. |
| `t-construction-norms` | Section 76.4.1 (one sentence) | US LNG EPC security: Bechtel's Rio Grande contracts require a standby LC of 10% of the contract price, stepping down to 5% at substantial completion, plus a parent guarantee. |
| `upstream-field-pf` | Section 76.8.4 | When a contractor's vessel financing fails, the field partners may have to buy the FPSO (Jubilee). |

All fact sheets this chapter needs are delivered; the earlier requests are met by `fpso-financing`, `fsru-charters` and `coral-sul-flng`.

## 76.10 Running-case installment

**Case P: an FSRU for Bélanou Phase 2** (`sec:76.11`). Case Bible Part 6, Chapter 76 row with Annex P 2.1: Case P (small), 2026; Tomasz Wierzbicki (Head of Portfolio Management at Kilnworth since 2024); the investment committee's question is asked by Devesh Raval (Kilnworth CEO; verbal habit "Show me the downside first."); no figures.

Short installment (500 to 700 words). First sentence: in 2026, eight years after financial close and the year Kilnworth sold 24% of Bélanou to Coldharbour (completed September 30, 2026). Scene (`casescene`, "Kilnworth Power International, London, 2026"; month chosen by the writer and stated once). MEH has commissioned a study of an FSRU to import LNG for a possible Bélanou Phase 2. Two reasons from the Case Bible: Sombé West's 2P reserves of 1,140 bcf were certified for Bélanou's 22-year GSA plus SEKA's existing contract (Case Bible 1.1; coverage figure P-F61 is Chapter 48's and is only cross-referenced), leaving no headroom for a second plant; and the GSA ends on November 30, 2043, three years before the PPA, a gap that Annex P 1.7.1 names as the reason for the 2026 study. One sentence notes that the proposed Azotes de Bélanou fertilizer plant (Annex P 4.14) would also need a new discovery or LNG imports. Tomasz reads the study and converts the FSRU charter into tariff terms ("What does that cost us per kilowatt-month?", once). Devesh asks who would sign the FSRU charter: SEKA, SNHK or the project company, and what the downside is if the charterer stops paying.

Narration outside the box applies Framework 76.2 qualitatively: the charter's credit would rest on a Kessaran state entity whose payment record in 2022 to 2024 the reader knows (pointer to Chapter 59); an LNG import adds a USD-priced commodity with no pass-through to the cauri-earning buyer unless the PPA fuel charge covers it, which it does for Bélanou's current GSA gas; and the FSRU cases of ssec:76.7.2 (Moheshkhali's off-hire reserve and DSCR covenant, Höegh Grace's break options, the Lampung dispute) show what lenders to such a charter would require. No figures, no FSRU rate for Kessara (Example 76.3 is not Kessara).

## 76.11 Practitioner's notebook (outline)

Checklist: SPA portfolio (volumes, fixed and variable components, buyer credit and guarantees, tenor, cancellation rights); feed gas supply and reserves behind it (Framework 75.2); LSTK EPC terms and contractor track record; completion guarantees, backstops and the completion test; ECA and DFI participation and their conditions; hedging minimums; reserve accounts; security and community plans for onshore frontier sites; sanctions screening of partners, contractors, shipping and buyers; for FPSOs and FSRUs: charterer credit, firm period, day-rate split, termination fee schedule against debt, residual value.

Red flags: FID with contracted volumes below the coverage threshold; a buyer whose SPA is guaranteed by a parent with weaker credit than assumed; an EPC price with large provisional sums; subcontract packages with weak contractors inside a lump EPC (Ichthys's power plant); completion guarantees that are several but whose weakest sponsor is large; an onshore site in an area with active insurgency; an ECA whose government faces domestic pressure to exit; an FPSO termination fee schedule below the debt balance.

Rules of thumb and their limits: LNG gearing of roughly 50% to 65% in verified US filings (Port Arthur about 52% of capex; Rio Grande about two-thirds) against 80% and more in contracted renewables, because of construction scale and completion risk, not revenue weakness; fixed fees carry the debt; financial completion is the moment the credit changes.

Common mistakes: counting variable sales margin in sizing; confusing a cancellation with a default; treating force majeure as cost relief; treating headline "largest financing ever" claims as facts rather than the sponsors' description at the time.

Questions experts ask: What share of capacity is under fixed-fee or take-or-pay contracts, and with whom? Whose guarantee covers us until completion, and what releases it? What happens to the debt if the site is shut for two years? Which ECA might withdraw, and who replaces it? If the charterer terminates in year five, does the fee repay us?

## 76.12 Judgment drill

The situation. You are a lender to Atlântica FPSO BV, the owner in Example 76.4, in March 2026. The charterer, an international oil company rated A−, offers a 15-year firm charter with termination for convenience after year 3. It proposes a flat termination fee equal to 24 months of the capital element of the day rate, USD 332.0 million, which on your model (Example 76.4's debt of USD 954.5 million amortizing over 12 years) falls below outstanding debt from year 4 to year 8. The sponsor wants debt sized at 1.40x on the capital element over 12 years. The term sheet is due in five days.

Reasoning: apply Framework 76.2; the termination fee is the lenders' protection against the charterer walking away; a fee below debt in years 4 to 8 leaves lenders exposed to redeployment value; an expert would require the fee to be at least outstanding debt plus breakage at each date (Clause 76.1 lender-friendly variant), or a sponsor guarantee of the shortfall, or faster amortization so debt stays below the fee; the charterer's A- rating supports the fee's credit but not its amount; check field-life risk (reserves behind the charter) and host-state risk.

What would change the answer: no termination for convenience before year 8 (fee issue disappears in the exposed window); an FPSO design easily redeployable to other fields (residual value supports the gap); a charterer parent guarantee of the fee; a shorter firm period with a strong extension incentive (worse, unless amortization shortens).

## 76.13 Exercise set (13 exercises)

Tier 1 (4):
- 76.1 Compare what lenders rely on in an integrated LNG project and in a US fixed-fee project.
- 76.2 Explain why a buyer's cargo cancellation does not reduce the fixed-fee revenue lenders size on.
- 76.3 Explain what changes in an LNG credit at financial completion.
- 76.4 Explain why FPSO debt amortizes inside the charter's firm period.

Tier 2 (6):
- 76.5 Recompute Example 76.1's DSCR if fixed opex rises to USD 361.0 million (answer: (1,433.1 − 361.0) / 812.6 = 1.32x).
- 76.6 Compute Port Arthur's term loan as a share of estimated capex from the verified figures (answer: approximately 52%, from approximately USD 6.8 billion against approximately USD 13.0 billion).
- 76.7 Recompute Example 76.2 at a fee of USD 2.25/MMBtu and 1.20x (answer: 11.06 mtpa, 92.2%).
- 76.8 Draft Clause 76.1 (FPSO early termination fee) in sponsor-, lender- and charterer-friendly variants; annotate.
- 76.9 Recompute Example 76.3 at 4.0 mtpa (answer: FSRU USD 0.383/MMBtu; onshore USD 0.469/MMBtu).
- 76.10 Find the FSRU charter rate at which onshore regasification becomes cheaper in Example 76.3 (answer: about USD 177,300 a day: (97.55 − 9.6 − 23.22) / 365).

Tier 3 (3):
- 76.11 Apply Framework 76.1 to PNG LNG using only `png-lng` facts and state the credit before and after February 6, 2015.
- 76.12 Model task: build Example 76.4 in Excel with two termination-fee schedule rows and a check row comparing each fee with outstanding debt at each year end, for termination allowed after year 3: (a) the drill's flat fee of 24 months of capital element (USD 332.0 million); (b) a fee of 85% of the remaining firm-period capital-element revenue discounted at 10% (answer: debt outstanding at the end of years 4 to 9 is USD 715.0, 644.7, 569.6, 489.5, 403.9 and 312.6 million; fee (a) falls short in years 4 to 8 by USD 383.0, 312.7, 237.6, 157.5 and 71.9 million and covers debt from year 9; fee (b) is USD 916.5 million in year 4 falling to USD 687.0 million in year 8 and covers debt in every year, so it is the lender-friendly design).
- 76.13 Case P: write the one-page note Tomasz would send Kilnworth's investment committee on who should sign an FSRU charter for Bélanou Phase 2, applying Framework 76.2 and Chapter 59's payment-security tools; no invented figures.

## 76.15 Glossary entries owned

| Term | Abbreviation | Definition | Home section |
|---|---|---|---|
| integrated LNG project | – | An LNG project in which the same owners develop the gas fields, pipeline and liquefaction plant and sell LNG through a joint marketing company. | 76.2.1 |
| marketing company (LNG) | – | The entity, often jointly owned by an LNG project's participants, that signs sale and purchase agreements and sells the project's LNG. | 76.2.1 |
| terminal use agreement | TUA | A contract reserving capacity at an LNG terminal for a fee payable whether or not the capacity is used. | 76.7.1 |
| lease-and-operate contract | – | A charter under which an FPSO owner leases and operates the unit for an oil company for a day rate. | 76.8.1 |
| day rate (capital and operating elements) | – | The daily charter payment for a floating asset, split into an element that repays capital and an element that covers operating costs. | 76.8.2 |
| early termination fee | – | The amount a charterer pays if it ends a charter before the firm period expires, usually scheduled to cover outstanding debt and an equity return. | 76.8.3 |

Removed (homed elsewhere; used without bold and cross-referenced): liquefaction train (ssec:12.2.1), floating storage and regasification unit (ssec:12.2.2) and floating production, storage and offloading unit (ssec:12.1.4) (R-026, R-121); financial completion (ssec:26.5.2; R-050). Terminal use agreement stays here (R-067).

## 76.16 Neighbors

Chapter 75 (Upstream and midstream oil and gas, including reserve-based lending) teaches reserve-based lending (borrowing base, price decks, redeterminations, deficiency cures), production sharing contracts, and pipelines financed on ship-or-pay and cost-of-service tariffs, with Chad–Cameroon and Nord Stream 2 as the midstream cases. Chapter 77 (Refining, petrochemicals, and manufacturing finance) moves to industrial plants whose products rarely sell under long-term fixed-fee contracts, so lenders rely on margins, sponsor support and ECA backing (Sadara) or on offtake from strategic buyers, and to gigafactories financed on scale-up risk (Northvolt).

- Example 72.4: year-3 steam capacity corrected from 104.54 to 104.52 MW (the well schedule and cost are unchanged).
- Example 73.1 (was 73.2): the augmentation rule was ambiguous; restated so the printed results follow from it (reach 400 MWh at the end of the third year after installation, or at the toll's end).
- Chapter 76 judgment drill and Exercise 76.12: the proposed fee (85% of discounted remaining capital-element revenue) never fell below Example 76.4's debt, contradicting the drill; the drill now uses a flat fee of 24 months of capital element (short in years 4 to 8, verified), and the exercise compares both designs.
- Unit rule 3: REIPPPP gearing (bid windows 1 to 4) and Baltic Power's 25-year CfD are now verified by `t-market-norms-2` and may be printed only in the attributed form; the other three banned figures stay banned.
- LDES Window 1: the brief followed `t-frontier-data` (73 eligible, 8 to 22 hours); `t-cap-and-floor` gives 77 eligible and 73 assessed, durations 8 to 32 hours, consultation closing August 14, 2026; the brief now follows `t-cap-and-floor`.
- Reserved abbreviations: "PSC" removed for production sharing contract and for Georgia's Public Service Commission; FSA removed; PFE spelled out in prose.
- Exhibit placements added for Exhibits 72.1, 72.2 and 73.1 to 73.4; Exhibit 72.6 (Case P drought) and Exhibit 75.5 (Case P RBL and GTA) added for the new ledger displays.

## Not fixed, with reason

- Registry, glossary canon and architecture edits are outside this brief (instructions); they are listed below.
- Kenya's steam-sales model: no fact sheet; listed as an optional open request and taught as a category.

- D-011 indicative ranges remain for Gulf contracted thermal margins and DSCRs, European CfD offshore wind tenor and DSCR, hydro, transmission, FPSO and FSRU terms where no sheet verifies them; each chapter's status note must list them for sign-off.
